# P001: Foundations of Agnostic Statistics

[All works](README.md) · [Machine-readable detail](../../catalog/works/P001.json)

P. M. Aronow and Benjamin T. Miller (2019). Foundations of Agnostic Statistics. Cambridge University Press.

DOI: [10.1017/9781316831762](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781316831762)

## Overview

Foundations of Agnostic Statistics develops probability, statistical estimation, regression, missing-data methods and causal inference. The Lean development covers selected probability and asymptotic results, conditional-expectation identities, likelihood calculations, and population identification and bounding arguments. It also formalizes counterexamples and corrected statements concerning differentiability, regression interpretation, likelihood targets, mixture maximization, overlap and other selected passages; these records do not constitute verification of the entire book.

Areas: Probability, Asymptotic statistics, Causal identification.

## Key results

For a square-integrable outcome and supplied conditional-mean and conditional-variance versions, total variance decomposes into expected conditional variance plus variance of the conditional mean, and the conditional mean minimizes mean squared prediction error with an exact excess-risk identity.

[`AronowAudit.P001.total_variance_and_cef_best_prediction`](../../AronowAudit/P001/P001_Foundations.lean#L3673) · [P001-T107](#p001-t107)

The sample mean of an i.i.d. real sequence with finite second moment converges in probability to its population mean, including when the variance is zero.

[`AronowAudit.P001.weak_law_finite_variance`](../../AronowAudit/P001/P001_Foundations.lean#L4049) · [P001-T125](#p001-t125)

For an i.i.d. real sequence with finite second moment and positive variance, the centered sample mean multiplied by the square root of sample size converges in distribution to a centered Gaussian with the population variance.

[`AronowAudit.P001.central_limit_finite_variance`](../../AronowAudit/P001/P001_Foundations.lean#L4073) · [P001-T126](#p001-t126)

For i.i.d. observations with a density continuous on the whole real line, the rectangular-kernel density estimate converges in probability at each real point when deterministic positive bandwidths satisfy $`h_n\to0`$ and $`nh_n\to\infty`$.

[`AronowAudit.P001.rectangular_kernel_consistency`](../../AronowAudit/P001/P001_Foundations.lean#L4312) · [P001-T127](#p001-t127)

With both potential outcomes in $`[a,b]`$ almost surely and both treatment arms having positive probability, the observable-law bounds contain the average treatment effect, have width $`b-a`$, and attain every value in that interval under alternative potential outcomes preserving the joint law of observed outcome and treatment.

[`AronowAudit.P001.Wave1.ate_sharp_paper_bounds_same_observable_law`](../../AronowAudit/P001/Wave1/AteSharpBounds.lean#L17) · [P001-T114](#p001-t114)

Under conditional independence of treatment and potential outcomes given a finite real covariate vector, uniform overlap and the stated integrability conditions, IPW identifies the average treatment effect and the augmented identity remains valid if either the propensity is correct or both arm regressions are correct with a candidate propensity uniformly separated from zero and one.

[`AronowAudit.P001.Wave1.causal_ipw_and_dr_paper_contract`](../../AronowAudit/P001/Wave1/CausalIpwDr.lean#L23) · [P001-T117](#p001-t117)

## Corrections and counterexamples

The printed claim that finite variance suffices for Hadamard differentiability of the mean in Kolmogorov distance is false: Lean refutes that universal implication even with the mean functional restricted to distributions having finite second moment.

[`AronowAudit.P001.Wave1.printed_finite_variance_mean_hadamard_refuted`](../../AronowAudit/P001/Wave1/MeanHadamard.lean#L15) · [P001-T118](#p001-t118)

The printed supportwise differentiability conditions do not make the derivative of the conditional mean at the covariate mean distributionally well defined: Lean refutes the universal scalar contract using conditional-mean versions that agree on the positive-density support but have different derivatives at the mean.

[`AronowAudit.P001.Wave1.pda_at_mean_printed_contract_refuted`](../../AronowAudit/P001/Wave1/Gap/RepairT3.lean#L44) · [P001-T3](#p001-t3)

Against the printed fixed-covariate KL target in Theorem 5.2.8, a binary joint law has conditional KL minimizers $`1/4`$ and $`3/4`$, while its unique pooled Bernoulli MLE on $`[1/10,9/10]`$ converges to $`1/2`$, the minimizer of the covariate-averaged KL divergence.

[`AronowAudit.P001.conditional_kl_mle_wrong_target`](../../AronowAudit/P001/P001_Foundations.lean#L7557) · [P001-T7](#p001-t7)

The passage treating the unrestricted normal/log-normal/exponential mixture likelihood as having a maximizer is false in general: Lean negates the universal assertion that every nonempty strictly positive sample has an admissible global likelihood maximizer.

[`AronowAudit.P001.Wave1.printed_unrestricted_mixture_maximum_refuted`](../../AronowAudit/P001/Wave1/MixtureMaximum.lean#L13) · [P001-T103](#p001-t103)

The printed interpretation of the linear placebo regression test as testing conditional independence fails at the population-coefficient level: a twelve-atom law satisfies the placebo null, positivity and potential-outcome ignorability, yet its unique augmented best linear predictor has placebo coefficient $`4/17`$ and lower risk than the restricted predictor.

[`AronowAudit.P001.placebo_full_finite_counterexample`](../../AronowAudit/P001/P001_Foundations.lean#L6771) · [P001-T17](#p001-t17)

The printed implication from failure of uniform positivity to incomplete overlap is false: a normalized countable joint law has positive mass at every covariate value in both arms and propensities strictly between zero and one, but no common positive bound separating all propensities from zero and one.

[`AronowAudit.P001.complete_overlap_without_uniform_positivity`](../../AronowAudit/P001/P001_Foundations.lean#L1334) · [P001-T19](#p001-t19)

## Caveats

- Conditional expectations are measurable versions specified by integral identities. Several identification results use almost-sure assumptions; individual supporting statements can impose stronger pointwise assumptions, as noted in P001-T22 and P001-T88.
- The IPW and double-robustness result is a population identity for finite-dimensional real covariates, with integrable potential outcomes and nuisance regressions; it does not establish consistency of an estimator with fitted nuisance functions.
- The conditional-mean prediction result explicitly requires square integrability of the outcome, conditional mean and competing predictor, and does not assert pointwise uniqueness of a minimizing function.
- The kernel result assumes global continuity of the density and proves pointwise consistency at each point; it does not assert uniform consistency or allow arbitrary data-dependent bandwidths.
- The sharp ATE bounds require known common bounds on both potential outcomes and positive probability of both arms, and preserve the observable joint law rather than identifying the unobserved potential outcomes.
- The selected Hadamard refutation concerns the finite-variance implication, and the selected mixture refutation concerns maximizer existence; neither declaration resolves every clause bundled into its target.
- The selected placebo declaration is a finite population-regression counterexample; sample F/Wald limits and rejection probabilities belong to separate associations.

## Targets

Each result below credits one target–declaration pair. A proof applies under the assumptions in its Lean statement.

### P001-T1

A counterexample to the sentence of Section 3.3.1 on the population mean, read with footnote 31, as printed

**Kind:** `counterexample`

**Location:** p. 120 (PDF p. 138), paragraph after Theorem 3.3.6, and footnote 31; the mean functional $`T_{\mathrm{E}}`$, p. 118 (PDF p. 136)

For a statement giving a counterexample, “Proved” means that the counterexample is proved. The Lean statements formalize the counterexample of P001-C03-E001. Let $`F`$ put mass $`q_k=2^{-(k+1)}`$ on each integer $`k\ge0`$. For $`n\ge2`$, let $`G_n=(1-n^{-3})F+n^{-3}H_n`$, where $`H_n`$ is the distribution of $`X+n`$ for $`X\sim F`$. The main statement asserts that $`F`$ and every $`G_n`$ are probability distributions with finite second moments, that $`\mathrm{E}_F[X]=1`$ and $`\mathrm{E}_F[X^2]=3`$, and that $`G_n`$ puts positive mass on every integer $`k\ge0`$. It asserts that, for every $`n\ge2`$, $`\mathrm{E}_{G_n}[X]=1+n^{-2},\qquad \mathrm{E}_{G_n}[X^2]=3+n^{-1}+2n^{-2},\qquad \frac{T_{\mathrm{E}}(G_n)-T_{\mathrm{E}}(F)}{n^{-2}}=1 ,`$ and that $`\mathrm{Var}_{G_n}[X]\to2`$ and $`n^{2}\sup_x|G_n(x)-F(x)|\to0`$ as $`n\to\infty`$. Three further statements state the partial sums of the masses and of the first two moments of $`F`$ for every $`K\ge0`$, for example $`\sum_{k=0}^{K}k^2q_k=3-(K^2+4K+6)2^{-(K+1)}`$. In the Lean statements a distribution is a sequence of masses on the nonnegative integers, and its mean, second moment and CDF are sums of that sequence. The main statement does not define Hadamard differentiability, and it does not state the conclusion that $`T_{\mathrm{E}}`$ is not Hadamard differentiable at $`F`$ with respect to the Kolmogorov distance. It asserts the facts about $`G_n`$ and $`t_n=n^{-2}`$ on which that conclusion rests: the difference quotient equals $`1`$ for every $`n`$, while $`(G_n-F)/t_n`$ tends to zero uniformly.

### P001-T2

The example of the usual plug-in regularity conditions in Section 3.3.1, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** p. 120 (PDF p. 138), paragraph after Theorem 3.3.6; Theorem 3.2.24, p. 109 (PDF p. 127); Example 3.3.4, p. 119 (PDF p. 137)

Two Lean statements cover the positive part of the correction of P001-C03-E001: the plug-in estimator of the mean is the sample mean, and it is asymptotically normal by the Central Limit Theorem. The first asserts that $`T_{\mathrm{E}}(\hat F)=\overline{X}`$ for every $`n\ge1`$ and every $`n`$ real observations, with no assumption on how they were drawn. The second takes an infinite sequence $`X_1,X_2,\ldots`$ of i.i.d. random variables with finite second moment, $`\mathrm{E}[X]=\mu`$ and $`\mathrm{V}[X]=\sigma^2\gt 0`$. It asserts the same identity and $`\sqrt{n}\,(\overline{X}-\mu)\xrightarrow{d}N(0,\sigma^2),`$ which is the conclusion of Theorem 3.2.24. The empirical CDF $`\hat F`$ is represented by the distribution that puts mass $`1/n`$ on each of the first $`n`$ observations, and $`T_{\mathrm{E}}(\hat F)`$ is the mean of this distribution. Convergence in distribution is convergence of the CDFs at every continuity point of the limit, as in Definition 3.2.22. The negative part of the correction, that finite variance does not imply Hadamard differentiability of $`T_{\mathrm{E}}`$ with respect to the Kolmogorov distance, is not restated here. It is the subject of the counterexample of the same finding.

### P001-T3

A counterexample to Definition 4.3.7 (Partial Derivative at the Average) and to the remark that the PDA “has a well-defined meaning”, as printed

**Kind:** `counterexample`

**Location:** Definition 4.3.7, p. 167 (PDF p. 185); the remark, p. 168 (PDF p. 186); footnote 13, p. 166 (PDF p. 184); Definition 2.2.15, p. 69 (PDF p. 87)

The Lean statements formalize the first counterexample of P001-C04-E002. Let $`X`$ have PDF $`f_X(x)=1/2`$ for $`x\in[-2,-1]\cup[1,2]`$ and $`f_X(x)=0`$ otherwise, and let $`Y\sim U(-1,1)`$ be independent of $`X`$. Define $`m_1(x)=x(1-x^2)^2`$ for $`|x|\lt 1`$ and $`m_1(x)=0`$ for $`|x|\ge1`$. The main statement asserts that $`(X,Y)`$ has the joint PDF $`f_X(x)\,\mathbf{1}\{|y|\le1\}/2`$, that $`X`$ and $`Y`$ are independent with the stated marginal distributions, and that $`\mathrm{E}[X]=0`$ while $`f_X(0)=0`$. It asserts that the zero function is a CEF of $`Y`$ given $`X`$, and that $`m_1`$ is continuously differentiable and vanishes wherever $`f_X\gt 0`$. Its last assertion is that, for every real $`a`$, the function $`am_1`$ is a CEF of $`Y`$ given $`X`$ and has derivative $`a`$ at $`\mathrm{E}[X]`$. A second statement asserts, for the same distribution, that $`0`$ and $`m_1`$ are both CEFs of $`Y`$ given $`X`$, with derivatives $`0`$ and $`1`$ at $`\mathrm{E}[X]`$. The third statement refutes the general scalar assertion that any two CEF versions differentiable wherever the covariate PDF is positive must have a common derivative at the covariate mean. It assumes an integrable covariate and integrable response, and does not require the mean to lie where the PDF is positive. In the Lean statements a function $`m`$ is a CEF of $`Y`$ given $`X`$ if $`m`$ is measurable, $`Y`$ and $`m(X)`$ are integrable and $`\mathrm{E}[Y\mathbf{1}\{X\in A\}]=\mathrm{E}[m(X)\mathbf{1}\{X\in A\}]`$ for every measurable set $`A`$. This identity determines $`m`$ only up to null sets. Definition 2.2.15 gives the CEF at each point of $`\mathrm{Supp}[X]`$.

### P001-T4

Definition 4.3.7 (Partial Derivative at the Average), as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** Definition 4.3.7, p. 167 (PDF p. 185); first paragraph of p. 168 (PDF p. 186)

The Lean statements do not restate the corrected definition of P001-C04-E002. They assert that, under its conditions, the PDA is determined by the distribution of $`(Y,\mathbf{X})`$. In the main statement, $`\mathbf{X}`$ is a random vector of length $`K`$ with integrable coordinates, a PDF $`f_{\mathbf{X}}`$ and mean $`\boldsymbol{\mu}=\mathrm{E}[\mathbf{X}]`$, and $`Y`$ is integrable. Let $`U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^K`$ be an open set such that $`\boldsymbol{\mu}\in U`$ and $`f_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x})\gt 0`$ for all $`\mathbf{x}\in U`$, and let $`m`$ and $`\tilde m`$ be CEFs of $`Y`$ given $`\mathbf{X}`$ that are continuous on $`U`$. If $`m`$ has partial derivative $`a`$ with respect to $`x_{[k]}`$ at $`\boldsymbol{\mu}`$, the statement asserts that $`m=\tilde m`$ on $`U`$ and that $`\tilde m`$ has the same partial derivative $`a`$ at $`\boldsymbol{\mu}`$. A second statement is the analytic step alone, for functions $`m`$ and $`\tilde m`$ that are continuous on an open set $`U`$ and equal Lebesgue-almost everywhere on $`U`$. It asserts that if $`m`$ has partial derivative $`a`$ in coordinate $`k`$ at a point of $`U`$, then $`\tilde m`$ has the same partial derivative at that point. A function $`m`$ is a CEF of $`Y`$ given $`\mathbf{X}`$ here if $`m`$ is measurable, $`Y`$ and $`m(\mathbf{X})`$ are integrable, and $`\mathrm{E}[Y\mathbf{1}\{\mathbf{X}\in A\}]=\mathrm{E}[m(\mathbf{X})\mathbf{1}\{\mathbf{X}\in A\}]`$ for every measurable set $`A`$, and the partial derivative at $`\boldsymbol{\mu}`$ is the derivative at $`t=0`$ of $`t\mapsto m(\boldsymbol{\mu}+t\mathbf{e}_k)`$.

### P001-T5

A counterexample to the rate condition of Definition 4.3.9 (Polynomial Least Squares Sieve Estimator), as printed

**Kind:** `counterexample`

**Location:** Section 4.3.5, p. 168 (PDF p. 186); Definition 4.3.9, pp. 168–169 (PDF pp. 186–187); the paragraph after it, p. 169 (PDF p. 187)

For a statement giving a counterexample, “Proved” means that the counterexample is proved. The Lean statement formalizes the counterexample of P001-C04-E001. The pairs $`(X_i,Y_i)`$ are i.i.d., with $`X_i\sim U(0,1)`$ and $`Y_i\sim N(0,1)`$ independent, so that the CEF is $`m=0`$, and $`J_n=\lfloor n^{3/4}\rfloor`$. Let $`\hat m_n`$ be a polynomial of degree at most $`J_n`$ that minimizes $`\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_i-p(X_i))^2`$ among the polynomials $`p`$ of degree at most $`J_n`$. The statement asserts that $`J_n\to\infty`$ and $`J_n/n\to0`$, and that, for every $`\delta\in(0,1]`$ and every $`\epsilon\gt 0`$, $`\liminf_{t\to\infty}\Pr\Bigl[\int_0^\delta\hat m_{t^4}(x)^2\,dx\gt \epsilon\Bigr]\ge\frac{1}{2},`$ where $`t`$ runs over the positive integers. This is the failure of consistency in $`L^2`$ on $`[0,\delta]`$, an interval of probability $`\delta`$. The objective is the sum of squared residuals, with the square that the printed display omits (P001-C04-E003). The statement is made for every choice of the minimizer $`\hat m_n`$, and it does not require this choice to be measurable; for nonmeasurable events, $`\Pr`$ denotes outer probability. It concerns the degree $`\lfloor n^{3/4}\rfloor`$ along $`n=t^4`$ only, and the lower bound on the conditional variance in the finding is not part of it.

### P001-T6

Definition 4.3.9 (Polynomial Least Squares Sieve Estimator) and the consistency claim after it, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** pp. 168–169 (PDF pp. 186–187)

Twelve Lean statements cover the corrected claim of P001-C04-E001 and the steps of its derivation. In the main statement the pairs $`(Y_i,X_i)`$ are i.i.d., $`X`$ has a PDF $`f_X`$ such that $`\{x:f_X(x)\gt 0\}=[a,b]`$ with $`a\lt b`$, the CEF $`m`$ is continuous on $`[a,b]`$, and $`\mathrm{Var}(Y\mid X)\le V\lt \infty`$. The polynomials $`p_0,p_1,\ldots`$ satisfy $`\deg p_k=k`$ and $`\mathrm{E}[p_j(X)p_k(X)]=\mathbf{1}\{j=k\}`$, and $`G_n=P^TP/n`$, where $`P`$ has the entries $`p_k(X_i)`$ for $`i\le n`$ and $`k\le J_n`$. If $`J_n\to\infty`$, $`(J_n+1)/n\to0`$ and $`\Pr[\lambda_{\min}(G_n)\ge c]\to1`$ for some $`c\gt 0`$, the statement asserts that $`\int\bigl(\hat m_n(x)-m(x)\bigr)^2\,dF(x)\xrightarrow{p}0`$ for every polynomial $`\hat m_n`$ of degree at most $`J_n`$ that minimizes the sum of squared residuals, where $`F`$ is the CDF of $`X`$. A second statement has the same content, and a supporting statement assumes only that $`X\in[a,b]`$ almost surely, with no PDF, which is broader than the correction. Two statements have the condition on $`G_n`$ with $`c=1/2`$ as their conclusion: the first assumes that $`\zeta_n^2(J_n+1)/n\to0`$, where $`\sum_{k\le J_n}p_k(X)^2\le\zeta_n^2`$ almost surely, and the second assumes that $`(J_n+1)^3/n\to0`$ and that $`X`$ is uniform on $`[a,b]`$. Two more bound $`\Pr[\|G_n-I\|_{\mathrm{op}}\gt 1/2]`$ for a fixed $`n`$ and $`K`$ basis polynomials. The bound is $`4(\zeta^2K-K)/n`$ when $`\sum_{k\lt K}p_k(X)^2\le\zeta^2`$ almost surely, and it is $`4(K^3-K)/n`$ for $`X\sim U(0,1)`$, where $`\sum_{k\lt K}p_k(x)^2\le K^2`$ on $`[0,1]`$. The remaining five give a coefficient decomposition and bounds at a fixed sample size, with $`n\gt 0`$ for the bounds. The first asserts that, when $`P^TP`$ is invertible, the least squares coefficients are the sum $`b_h+b_r+b_u`$ of the coefficients of an approximating polynomial, of its remainder $`r`$ and of the noise $`u`$. Three others concern the case $`\lambda_{\min}(G_n)\ge c`$ and assert that $`c\|b_r\|^2\le b_r^TG_nb_r\le\|r\|^2/n`$, that, under the random-design and noise hypotheses stated below for the fifth statement, the conditional mean of $`\mathbf{1}\{\lambda_{\min}(G_n)\ge c\}\|b_u\|^2`$ given the design is at most $`V\mathrm{tr}(G_n^{-1})/n\le VK/(cn)`$ almost surely on that event, and that $`\|\hat m_n-m\|_{L^2(F)}\le\epsilon(1+c^{-1/2})+\|b_u\|`$ if the approximating polynomial is within $`\epsilon`$ of $`m`$ on $`[a,b]`$. The fifth is a supporting statement for a measurable random design $`P`$ with $`n\gt 0`$: it assumes square integrable noise, conditional mean zero and zero conditional cross-products for distinct observations given $`P`$, and conditional second moments bounded by $`V\ge0`$. For $`c\gt 0`$, it sets $`Z=\mathbf{1}\{\lambda_{\min}(G_n)\ge c\}\|b_u\|^2`$, supplies a version $`q(P)=\mathrm{E}[Z\mid P]`$ that is zero off that event, asserts $`q(P)\le V\mathrm{tr}(G_n^{-1})/n\le VK/(cn)`$ almost surely on the event, and asserts $`\mathrm{E}[Z]\le VK/(cn)`$. The condition $`\lambda_{\min}(G_n)\ge c`$ is written as $`c\|v\|^2\le v^TG_nv`$ for all $`v`$, and the bound on the conditional variance as $`\mathrm{E}[(Y-m(X))^2\mathbf{1}\{X\in A\}]\le V\Pr[X\in A]`$ for every measurable set $`A`$. The main statement explicitly assumes a square integrable $`Y`$, which follows from its bounded conditional variance and continuity of the CEF on compact support. Bounded conditional variance is stronger than the finite second moment of the error assumed in the corrected passage. Gram stability is assumed here; its sufficient-rate and finite-sample bounds are separate statements.

### P001-T7

A counterexample to Theorem 5.2.8, as printed

**Kind:** `counterexample`

**Location:** Theorem 5.2.8 and Definition 5.2.7, p. 195 (PDF p. 213)

The Lean statements formalize the counterexample of P001-C05-E001. The pairs $`(X_i,Y_i)`$ are i.i.d. with $`\Pr[X=0,Y=0]=\Pr[X=1,Y=1]=3/8`$ and $`\Pr[X=0,Y=1]=\Pr[X=1,Y=0]=1/8`$, and the model is $`g(y,x;t)=t^y(1-t)^{1-y}`$ with $`t\in\Theta=[1/10,9/10]`$. Let $`K_p(t)=p\log(p/t)+(1-p)\log((1-p)/(1-t))`$ and $`\hat t_n=\min\{9/10,\max\{1/10,\overline{Y}\}\}`$. Two statements with the same content assert that, for $`p=1/4`$ and $`p=3/4`$, the function $`K_p`$ is nonnegative on $`\Theta`$ and vanishes only at $`t=p`$, so that the printed targets are $`1/4`$ at $`x=0`$ and $`3/4`$ at $`x=1`$. They assert that, for every $`n\ge1`$ and every sample of zeros and ones, $`\hat t_n`$ is the unique maximizer over $`\Theta`$ of the likelihood $`\prod_it^{y_i}(1-t)^{1-y_i}`$. They assert that $`\mathrm{E}[Y]=1/2`$ and $`\mathrm{V}[Y]=1/4`$, and that, for every $`n\ge1`$ and every $`\epsilon\gt 0`$, $`\Pr\bigl[|\hat t_n-1/2|\ge\epsilon\bigr]\le\frac{1}{4n\epsilon^2},\qquad \Pr\bigl[|\hat t_n-1/4|\gt 1/8\bigr]\ge1-\frac{16}{n},\qquad \Pr\bigl[|\hat t_n-3/4|\gt 1/8\bigr]\ge1-\frac{16}{n}.`$ Their last assertions are that $`\hat t_n\xrightarrow{p}1/2`$, that $`\hat t_n`$ converges in probability neither to $`1/4`$ nor to $`3/4`$, and that $`(K_{1/4}+K_{3/4})/2`$ has the unique minimizer $`1/2`$ on $`\Theta`$. A third statement is the assertion on the maximizer alone. A fourth takes $`\Pr[X=1]=q\in[0,1]`$ with the conditional distributions unchanged and asserts that $`\hat t_n\xrightarrow{p}1/4+q/2`$. For every $`n\ge1`$, it asserts that $`\hat t_n`$ almost surely uniquely maximizes the Bernoulli product likelihood over $`\Theta`$. For $`0\lt q\lt 1`$, it also gives the conditional means $`1/4`$ and $`3/4`$ and asserts nonconvergence in probability to either. It does not identify $`1/4+q/2`$ as the minimizer of the $`q`$-weighted averaged divergence. The derivatives of $`K_p`$ and the value $`\log(4/3)/2`$ of the gap in the averaged divergence are not part of any of these statements.

### P001-T8

Theorem 5.2.8, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** Theorem 5.2.8, p. 195 (PDF p. 213); the explanatory paragraphs on p. 196 (PDF p. 214)

Ten Lean statements cover the corrected theorem of P001-C05-E001 and the remark on p. 196 that $`\tilde{\boldsymbol\theta}=\boldsymbol\theta`$ when the model is true. In these statements $`f_{Y|\mathbf{X}}`$ and $`g(\cdot,\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t})`$ are nonnegative and integrate to one in $`y`$ with respect to a reference measure, which is Lebesgue measure for PDFs and a counting measure for PMFs, and the averaged divergence $`D(\mathbf{t})=\mathrm{E}_{\mathbf{X}}[\mathrm{KL}[f_{Y|\mathbf{X}}(\cdot|\mathbf{X})\,\|\,g(\cdot,\mathbf{X};\mathbf{t})]]`$ takes values in $`[0,\infty]`$. The main statement on consistency, with $`L_n`$ and $`L`$ as in the corrected theorem, assumes that $`|L_n(\mathbf{t})-L(\mathbf{t})|\le\Delta_n`$ for all $`\mathbf{t}\in\boldsymbol\Theta`$ with $`\Delta_n\xrightarrow{p}0`$, and that for every $`\epsilon\gt 0`$ there exists $`\delta\gt 0`$ such that $`L(\mathbf{t})\le L(\tilde{\boldsymbol\theta})-\delta`$ whenever $`\|\mathbf{t}-\tilde{\boldsymbol\theta}\|\ge\epsilon`$. The estimator either maximizes the likelihood $`\prod_ig(Y_i,\mathbf{X}_i;\mathbf{t})`$ over $`\boldsymbol\Theta`$ or satisfies $`L_n(\hat{\boldsymbol\theta}_{ML})\ge\sup_{\mathbf{t}}L_n(\mathbf{t})-\eta_n`$ with $`\eta_n\ge0`$ and $`\eta_n\xrightarrow{p}0`$. The statement asserts that $`\hat{\boldsymbol\theta}_{ML}\xrightarrow{p}\tilde{\boldsymbol\theta}`$, and that $`\tilde{\boldsymbol\theta}`$ minimizes $`D`$ over $`\boldsymbol\Theta`$ when $`f\log f`$ and $`f\log g(\cdot;\mathbf{t})`$ are integrable for every $`\mathbf{t}\in\boldsymbol\Theta`$. A second statement asserts the identity behind the last clause: under the same integrability, $`D(\mathbf{t})`$ is finite and equals $`\mathrm{E}[\log f_{Y|\mathbf{X}}(Y|\mathbf{X})]-L(\mathbf{t})`$. A third asserts that if the model is true at $`\boldsymbol\theta`$, then $`D(\boldsymbol\theta)=0`$, and a unique minimizer of $`D`$ over $`\boldsymbol\Theta`$ equals $`\boldsymbol\theta`$. One supporting statement asserts consistency for a compact $`\boldsymbol\Theta`$, a continuous $`L`$ and a unique minimizer of $`D`$, under finite entropy and cross entropy, for exact product-likelihood maximizers. It covers neither approximate maximizers nor an infinite divergence. Another supporting statement uses explicit separation and permits either exact product-likelihood maximization or vanishing-error log-likelihood maximization, without compactness or continuity of $`L`$. With finite entropy and cross entropy, it also identifies the unique minimizer of the extended averaged divergence. Three statements contain the steps of the argument for an arbitrary criterion in place of the log-likelihood. They assert that a unique maximum of a continuous function on a compact set is separated as in the second condition, that $`L(\tilde{\boldsymbol\theta})-L(\mathbf{t})\le2\Delta+\eta`$ under the first and third conditions, and that an approximate maximizer is consistent under the three conditions. The last two are supporting statements. The identity statement assumes normalized densities, the joint observation law defined by $`f`$, positivity of $`g(\cdot;\mathbf{t})`$ wherever $`f\gt 0`$ almost everywhere under the product of the covariate law and the reference measure, and integrability of $`f\log f`$ and $`f\log g(\cdot;\mathbf{t})`$ at the fixed $`\mathbf{t}`$. It asserts that the extended $`D(\mathbf{t})`$ is finite, equals $`\mathrm{E}[\log f_{Y|\mathbf{X}}(Y|\mathbf{X})]-L(\mathbf{t})`$, and agrees with the divergence computed as a real integral. The other supporting truth statement assumes measurable nonnegative model sections normalized to probability laws and a unique minimizer of the extended averaged divergence. It asserts $`D(\boldsymbol\theta)=0`$, $`\tilde{\boldsymbol\theta}=\boldsymbol\theta`$ and, on $`\boldsymbol\Theta`$, $`D(\mathbf{t})=0`$ if and only if $`\mathbf{t}=\boldsymbol\theta`$. The statistical consistency statements use i.i.d. observations. The main and separated supporting statements explicitly assume that $`\log g(Y,\mathbf{X};\mathbf{t})`$ is integrable under the observation law for every $`\mathbf{t}\in\boldsymbol\Theta`$. The statements on the consistency of the ML estimator also assume that the estimator is measurable and that $`g(\cdot;\mathbf{t})\gt 0`$ wherever $`f\gt 0`$ almost everywhere under the product of the covariate law and the reference measure. In the main consistency statement this support condition holds for all parameters on one common set of full measure; in the two supporting consistency statements it holds almost everywhere separately for each parameter.

### P001-T9

A counterexample to the statement of Section 5.2.5 that the mixture likelihood “can be maximized to find the ML estimate”, applied to Example 5.2.11, as printed

**Kind:** `counterexample`

**Location:** Section 5.2.5, pp. 198–199 (PDF pp. 216–217); Example 5.2.11, pp. 199–200 (PDF pp. 217–218); Figure 5.2.5, p. 201 (PDF p. 219)

For a statement giving a counterexample, “Proved” means that the counterexample is proved. The Lean statement formalizes the counterexample of P001-C05-E002 on the sample $`(y_1,y_2,y_3)=(1,2,3)`$. The density is the mixture $`g(y;\boldsymbol\theta)=p_Ng_N+p_{LN}g_{LN}+(1-p_N-p_{LN})g_E`$ of Example 5.2.11, with $`\boldsymbol\theta=(p_N,p_{LN},\mu_N,\sigma_N,\mu_{LN},\sigma_{LN},\lambda_E)`$. A parameter vector is admissible if $`p_N,p_{LN}\ge0`$, $`p_N+p_{LN}\le1`$ and $`\sigma_N,\sigma_{LN},\lambda_E\gt 0`$. The statement asserts that for every real $`B`$ there exists $`\sigma\gt 0`$ such that $`\boldsymbol\theta_\sigma=(1/2,1/4,1,\sigma,0,1,1)`$ is admissible and $`g(1;\boldsymbol\theta_\sigma)\,g(2;\boldsymbol\theta_\sigma)\,g(3;\boldsymbol\theta_\sigma)\gt B .`$ It asserts further that no admissible parameter vector maximizes the likelihood of this sample. The log-normal density is set to $`0`$ for $`y\le0`$ and the exponential density to $`0`$ for $`y\lt 0`$, which does not affect a sample of positive observations. The statement concerns this one sample. The lower bound $`e^{-5}/(32\sqrt{2\pi}\,\sigma)`$ of the finding is not part of it, and neither is the extension to every sample of positive observations, which belongs to the corrected passage.

### P001-T10

Section 5.2.5 and Example 5.2.11 on maximizing the mixture likelihood, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** pp. 198–200 (PDF pp. 216–218); Figure 5.2.5, p. 201 (PDF p. 219)

Two Lean statements cover the mathematical statement in the correction of P001-C05-E002, for the mixture density $`g(y;\boldsymbol\theta)`$ of Example 5.2.11. A parameter vector is admissible if $`p_N,p_{LN}\ge0`$, $`p_N+p_{LN}\le1`$ and $`\sigma_N,\sigma_{LN},\lambda_E\gt 0`$. The first statement asserts that for every $`n\ge1`$, all $`y_1,\ldots,y_n\gt 0`$ and every real $`B`$, there exists an admissible $`\boldsymbol\theta`$ such that $`\prod_{i=1}^{n}g(y_i;\boldsymbol\theta)\gt B`$. It does not repeat the conclusion that no parameter value attains the supremum. The second takes real numbers $`B,S,b`$ and $`\delta,a\gt 0`$, and restricts the admissible vectors by $`|\mu_N|,|\mu_{LN}|\le B,\qquad \delta\le\sigma_N,\sigma_{LN}\le S,\qquad a\le\lambda_E\le b .`$ If this set is nonempty, the statement asserts that it is compact and that, for all $`y_1,\ldots,y_n\gt 0`$, the likelihood is continuous on it and attains its maximum there. This statement covers the displayed parameter bounds and assumes that they define a nonempty set. The corrected passage also states attainment on arbitrary nonempty compact restrictions with positive lower bounds on the scales and rate; that broader assertion is not proved here. This theorem asserts attainment without a uniqueness conclusion. The discussion of Figure 5.2.5 and penalties is not formalized here.

### P001-T11

A counterexample to the sentence of Section 5.2.5 on subsets of the component models, as printed

**Kind:** `counterexample`

**Location:** p. 199 (PDF p. 217), second paragraph; the mixture and its parameter vector, pp. 198–199 (PDF pp. 216–217)

The Lean statements formalize the bounded example of P001-C05-N005. The outcome $`Y`$ takes values in $`\{0,1,2,3\}`$, the mixture has probabilities $`(p(1-a),\,pa,\,(1-p)(1-b),\,(1-p)b)`$, and $`\boldsymbol\theta=(p,a,b)`$ lies in $`\boldsymbol\Theta=[0,1]\times[0,1]\times[1/4,3/4]`$. Set $`\boldsymbol\theta_A=(1,1/3,1/4)`$ and $`\boldsymbol\theta_B=(1,1/3,3/4)`$. Three statements with the same content assert that $`\boldsymbol\Theta`$ is compact and contains both vectors, that both give the probabilities $`(2/3,1/3,0,0)`$, and that for every $`n`$ and every sample the likelihood attains its maximum over $`\boldsymbol\Theta`$. They assert that every sample of size $`n`$ has the same probability under $`\boldsymbol\theta_A`$ and $`\boldsymbol\theta_B`$, and that, for every function $`T_n=(\hat p_n,\hat a_n,\hat b_n)`$ of the sample, $`\Pr_{\boldsymbol\theta_A}\bigl[|\hat b_n-1/4|\lt 1/8\bigr]+\Pr_{\boldsymbol\theta_B}\bigl[|\hat b_n-3/4|\lt 1/8\bigr]\le1 .`$ Their last assertion is that no sequence of estimators converges in probability to $`\boldsymbol\theta_A`$ under $`\boldsymbol\theta_A`$ and to $`\boldsymbol\theta_B`$ under $`\boldsymbol\theta_B`$. Three further statements contain the compactness with the attained maximum, the sample probabilities with the displayed inequality, and the last assertion, each alone. A fourth says that a sequence cannot converge in probability to two distinct limits. A further statement contains the same full witness and supplies a selection of global ML estimates for every finite sample, with inconsistency at at least one of the two true full vectors. The remaining statement contains compactness, attainment and the same finite-sample probability bound, but its final clause is a generic impossibility of two distinct probability limits under one arbitrary measure, with no link between that clause and the two sampling distributions. In the finite-sample clauses the probability of an event is the sum of the likelihood over the samples of $`\{0,1,2,3\}^n`$ in the event. Asymptotic efficiency and the information matrix of the example are not formalized.

### P001-T12

The sentence of Section 5.2.5 on subsets of the component models, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** p. 199 (PDF p. 217), second paragraph

Nine Lean statements cover the correction of P001-C05-N005, for a mixture $`g=\sum_{m=1}^{M}p_mg_m`$ whose components are nonnegative and integrate to one in $`y`$ with respect to a reference measure. In the first, $`\boldsymbol\theta\ne\boldsymbol\theta'`$ have the same weights, with $`p_j=0`$, and the same components apart from the $`j`$th. The statement asserts that $`g(\cdot,\cdot;\boldsymbol\theta)=g(\cdot,\cdot;\boldsymbol\theta')`$, that samples of every size $`n`$ have the same distribution under both, and that the expected log-likelihoods agree. It asserts further that no sequence of estimators converges in probability to $`\boldsymbol\theta`$ under $`\boldsymbol\theta`$ and to $`\boldsymbol\theta'`$ under $`\boldsymbol\theta'`$. Two statements assume the conditions of the correction for consistency and assert that every measurable maximizer of the likelihood over $`\boldsymbol\Theta`$ converges in probability to the true parameter $`\boldsymbol\theta^0`$. The two differ only in how they assume that $`g`$ is positive at the observations, and a supporting statement asserts the same for maximizers of the average log-likelihood $`L_n`$. The main statement on efficiency assumes all the sufficient conditions of the correction, and asserts that $`\hat{\boldsymbol\theta}_{ML}\xrightarrow{p}\boldsymbol\theta^0`$ and $`\sqrt{n}(\hat{\boldsymbol\theta}_{ML}-\boldsymbol\theta^0)\xrightarrow{d}N(0,I^{-1})`$, where $`I=\mathrm{E}[ss^T]`$ for the score $`s`$. It asserts further that $`\mathrm{Cov}(\psi)-I^{-1}`$ is positive semidefinite for the influence function $`\psi`$ of every regular asymptotically linear estimator. Three statements contain parts of this: the normal limit when consistency is assumed, the identity $`\mathrm{E}[\psi s^T]=\mathrm{Id}`$ for a regular asymptotically linear estimator together with the bound, and the bound for any $`\psi`$ that satisfies this identity. A supporting statement asserts the normal limit for a consistent maximizer of $`L_n`$ and is narrower, since it assumes consistency and has no bound. In the Lean statements a regular asymptotically linear estimator $`T_n`$ and its influence function $`\psi`$ are measurable, $`\psi`$ has mean zero and finite second moments, and $`\sqrt{n}(T_n-\boldsymbol\theta^0)-n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^n\psi(Z_i)\xrightarrow{p}0`$ under the true distribution, where $`Z_i`$ is one observation. In addition, $`\sqrt{n}(T_n-\boldsymbol\theta_n)`$ has the same normal limit $`N(0,\mathrm{Cov}(\psi))`$ under every sequence $`\boldsymbol\theta_n=\boldsymbol\theta^0+h/\sqrt{n}`$. Differentiation under the integral is represented by the identities $`\mathrm{E}[s]=0`$ and $`\mathrm{E}[\nabla^2\log g]=-I`$, and by differentiability of the model in quadratic mean. In the statements on the normal limit and efficiency, local positivity of $`g`$ and $`C^2`$ regularity of $`\log g`$ near $`\boldsymbol\theta^0`$ hold on one measurable set $`S`$ of probability one under the common observation law; the main statement also imposes positivity on all of $`\boldsymbol\Theta`$ on this $`S`$.

### P001-T13

A counterexample to footnote 15 on bootstrap standard errors for hot deck imputation, as printed

**Kind:** `counterexample`

**Location:** footnote 15, p. 233 (PDF p. 251); Table 6.3.1, row “Hot Deck Imputation”; hot deck imputation, Section 6.2.3, pp. 224–225 (PDF pp. 242–243); the bootstrap, Section 3.4.3, p. 131 (PDF p. 149)

For a statement giving a counterexample, “Proved” means that the counterexample is proved. The Lean statements formalize the example of P001-C06-E007. The units are i.i.d.: $`P`$ is uniform on $`[1/4,3/4]`$, $`R`$ given $`P=p`$ is Bernoulli with parameter $`p`$, $`\varepsilon`$ equals $`+1`$ or $`-1`$ with probability $`1/2`$ each independently of $`(P,R)`$, and $`Y=50+\varepsilon`$. The estimator $`T_n`$ imputes to each unit with $`R=0`$ the outcome of the unit with $`R=1`$ that is nearest in $`P`$, and takes the mean of the $`n`$ completed outcomes, with $`T_n=50`$ if no unit responded. Let $`v_n`$ be $`n`$ times the variance of the estimate over the $`n^n`$ equally likely resamples of the sample. The main statement asserts that $`\mathrm{E}[T_n]=50`$ for every $`n\ge1`$, that $`n\mathrm{Var}(T_n)\to V=3\log3-\frac{1}{4},\qquad \mathrm{E}[v_n]\to V_b=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{A}{2}+B\Bigl(2\log3-\frac{3}{2}\Bigr),\qquad 0\lt V_b\lt V,`$ and that $`v_n`$ does not converge in probability to $`V`$. Here $`s=1-e^{-1}`$, $`A=2/s-s/(2-s)`$ and $`B=3/(2s)-(4-s)/(2(2-s))`$. A second statement asserts that $`\mathrm{E}[v_n^2]`$ is bounded uniformly in $`n`$, and a third that $`0\lt s\lt 1`$, $`A\lt 3`$, $`B\lt 3/2`$ and $`V_b\lt V`$. MAR and positivity are properties of the stated distribution and are not asserted separately. When two respondents are equally near, the Lean statements use a fixed choice between them.

### P001-T14

Footnote 15, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** footnote 15, p. 233 (PDF p. 251); Table 6.3.1, row “Hot Deck Imputation”

Three Lean statements cover the mathematics behind the corrected footnote of P001-C06-E007, in the example of that finding. There $`P`$ is uniform on $`[1/4,3/4]`$, $`R`$ given $`P`$ is Bernoulli with parameter $`P`$, and $`Y=50+\varepsilon`$ with $`\varepsilon=\pm1`$. For the hot deck estimator $`T_n`$, the first statement asserts that $`\mathrm{E}[T_n]=50`$ for every $`n\ge1`$ and $`n\mathrm{Var}(T_n)\to V=3\log3-1/4`$, while $`n`$ times the bootstrap variance has mean tending to a limit $`V_b\lt V`$ and does not converge in probability to $`V`$. The second fixes $`n\ge1`$, the values of $`P`$ and the responses, and lets the $`\varepsilon_j`$ be independent and equal to $`+1`$ or $`-1`$ with probability $`1/2`$ each. It asserts that $`T_n-50=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}W_j\varepsilon_j,\qquad \mathrm{E}[T_n]=50,\qquad \mathrm{Var}(T_n)=\frac{1}{n^2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}W_j^2,`$ where $`W_j`$ is the number of times that the outcome of unit $`j`$ is used, with $`W_j=0`$ if $`R_j=0`$. The third makes the same assumptions. It asserts that the mean over the $`\varepsilon_j`$ of the bootstrap variance equals $`1/(2n^2)`$ times the average of $`\sum_j(W_j^{(1)}-W_j^{(2)})^2`$ over two independent resamples, where $`W_j^{(1)}`$ and $`W_j^{(2)}`$ are the total weights of the copies of unit $`j`$ in the two resamples. Conditioning on the values of $`P`$ and $`R`$ is represented by fixing them. No conditional distribution of the whole sample is constructed. The sentences of the corrected footnote on the standard error in Table 6.3.1 are not formalized.

### P001-T15

A counterexample to the end of Section 7.2.4 and to footnote 20 on the bootstrap for matching estimators, as printed

**Kind:** `counterexample`

**Location:** end of Section 7.2.4, p. 264 (PDF p. 282), with footnote 13; footnote 20, p. 279 (PDF p. 297); Table 7.5.1, row “Matching”, p. 280 (PDF p. 298); the matching procedure, pp. 262–263 (PDF pp. 280–281)

For a statement giving a counterexample, “Proved” means that the counterexample is proved. The Lean statements formalize the counterexample of P001-C07-E011. The units are i.i.d.: $`X_i`$ is uniform on $`[0,1/2]`$, $`D_i`$ given $`X_i=x`$ is Bernoulli with parameter $`1/4+x`$, $`\varepsilon_i`$ equals $`1`$ or $`-1`$ with probability $`1/2`$ each independently of $`(X_i,D_i)`$, and $`Y_i(0)=\varepsilon_i`$, $`Y_i(1)=0`$. The propensity score is estimated by $`\hat p_D(x)=\hat a+x`$, where $`\hat a`$ is $`\overline{D}-\overline{X}`$ restricted to $`[1/8,3/8]`$, and it is estimated again in every bootstrap resample. Let $`\hat\theta_n`$ be the matching estimator of Section 7.2.4 with this score, and let $`V_n^*`$ be its variance over the $`n^n`$ equally likely resamples. The main statement asserts that $`\mathrm{E}[\hat\theta_n]=0`$ for every $`n\ge1`$, that $`n\mathrm{Var}(\hat\theta_n)\to V=3\log3-\frac{1}{4},\qquad \mathrm{E}[nV_n^*]\to V_B=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{c_1}{2}+c_2\Bigl(2\log3-\frac{3}{2}\Bigr),\qquad 0\lt V_B\lt V,`$ and that $`nV_n^*`$ does not converge in probability to $`V`$, with $`c_1`$ and $`c_2`$ as in the finding. A second statement asserts that $`\hat a\xrightarrow{p}1/4`$. A supporting statement is the main statement with the true score in place of the estimated score, which is narrower than the counterexample. Strong ignorability and the bounds on the score are properties of the stated distribution and are not asserted separately. When a sample has no control unit, the outcomes that cannot be matched are set to zero, a case that the procedure of Section 7.2.4 does not cover.

### P001-T17

A counterexample to the $`F`$-test of Section 7.2.8 (Placebo Testing) as a test of $`\mathbf{W}_i\perp\!\!\!\perp D_i\mid\mathbf{X}_i`$, as printed

**Kind:** `counterexample`

**Location:** Section 7.2.8, p. 270 (PDF p. 288)

The Lean statements formalize the first counterexample of P001-C07-E007. Let $`X_i`$ be uniform on $`\{-1,0,1\}`$, let $`Z_i`$ be uniform on $`\{-1,1\}`$ and independent of $`X_i`$, and set $`W_i=X_i^2+Z_i/2`$ and $`\Pr[D_i=1\mid X_i,Z_i]=1/4+X_i^2/2`$. The main statement and a supporting statement take i.i.d. draws from this distribution on twelve points, and assert that $`W_i\perp\!\!\!\perp D_i\mid X_i`$ and that $`(Y_i(0),Y_i(1))\perp\!\!\!\perp D_i\mid X_i`$ for $`Y_i(0)=2+Z_i`$ and $`Y_i(1)=3+Z_i`$. They assert that the matrix of second moments of $`(1,X_i,W_i)`$ has determinant $`17/54`$, and that $`(29/68,0,4/17)`$ is the unique minimizer of $`\mathrm{E}[(D_i-a-bX_i-cW_i)^2]`$. Let $`F_n`$ be the $`F`$-statistic that compares the regressions of $`D_i`$ on $`(1,X_i,W_i)`$ and on $`(1,X_i)`$, and let $`\mathcal{W}_n`$ be the robust Wald statistic for the coefficient on $`W_i`$. The two statements assert that $`\frac{F_n}{n}\to\frac{64}{531},\qquad \frac{\mathcal{W}_n}{n}\to\frac{18496}{135315}\qquad\text{almost surely.}`$ They assert that $`\Pr[F_n\gt c_n]\to1`$ when $`c_n`$ is the $`1-\alpha`$ quantile of the $`F`$ distribution with $`1`$ and $`n-3`$ degrees of freedom, for every $`\alpha\in(0,1)`$, and that $`\Pr[\mathcal{W}_n\gt c]\to1`$ for every fixed $`c`$. A third statement contains the facts about the population alone, and adds that the propensity score lies strictly between $`1/8`$ and $`7/8`$ and that $`\mathrm{Var}(W_i\mid X_i)=1/4`$. It adds further that the regression of $`D_i`$ on $`(1,X_i)`$ has the unique coefficients $`(7/12,0)`$, and that the mean squared errors of the two regressions are $`35/144`$ and $`59/272`$. The value $`5056/177`$ of the $`F`$-statistic for $`240`$ observations is asserted as arithmetic on $`240`$ times the two mean squared errors, and no sample of $`240`$ observations is constructed.

### P001-T18

The regression implementation of the placebo test in Section 7.2.8, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** Section 7.2.8, p. 270 (PDF p. 288)

The main Lean statement contains the full statement at the end of the correction of P001-C07-E007. Let $`D_i`$ take the values $`0`$ and $`1`$, each with positive probability, let $`\mathbf{X}_i`$ and $`\mathbf{W}_i`$ be random vectors with finite second moments, and suppose that the matrix of second moments of $`(1,\mathbf{X}_i,\mathbf{W}_i)`$ is invertible. Suppose that $`\mathbf{W}_i\perp\!\!\!\perp D_i\mid\mathbf{X}_i`$ and that $`\mathrm{E}[D_i\mid\mathbf{X}_i]=\gamma_0+\boldsymbol\gamma^T\mathbf{X}_i`$. The statement asserts that $`\mathrm{E}[D_i\mid\mathbf{X}_i,\mathbf{W}_i]=\gamma_0+\boldsymbol\gamma^T\mathbf{X}_i`$, and that $`\mathrm{E}\bigl[(D_i-\gamma_0-\boldsymbol\gamma^T\mathbf{X}_i)^2\bigr]\le\mathrm{E}\bigl[(D_i-a-\mathbf{b}^T\mathbf{X}_i-\mathbf{c}^T\mathbf{W}_i)^2\bigr]`$ for all $`a`$, $`\mathbf{b}`$ and $`\mathbf{c}`$, with equality only if $`a=\gamma_0`$, $`\mathbf{b}=\boldsymbol\gamma`$ and $`\mathbf{c}=\mathbf{0}`$. This says that the best linear predictor of $`D_i`$ given $`\mathbf{X}_i`$ and $`\mathbf{W}_i`$ is unique and has zero coefficients on $`\mathbf{W}_i`$. A second statement asserts the clause on equality alone, for a $`D_i`$ with values in $`\{0,1\}`$ that may be constant. The third statement has the hypotheses and all three conclusions of the main statement, including uniqueness. It additionally assumes, as an explicit hypothesis, Proposition 6.6 of Kallenberg, Foundations of Modern Probability (2nd ed., 2002), on conditional independence, stated in full generality. The constant is kept apart from $`\mathbf{X}_i`$ in the Lean statements, and conditional independence means that $`\Pr[\mathbf{W}_i\in A,D_i\in B\mid\mathbf{X}_i]=\Pr[\mathbf{W}_i\in A\mid\mathbf{X}_i]\Pr[D_i\in B\mid\mathbf{X}_i]`$ almost surely for all measurable sets $`A`$ and $`B`$. The sentences of the corrected passage on a joint test with a robust covariance estimator are not formalized.

### P001-T19

A counterexample to the statements of Section 7.3 that a failure of positivity implies incomplete overlap and that the converse of Theorem 7.3.2 “can almost be stated”, as printed

**Kind:** `counterexample`

**Location:** Section 7.3, pp. 271–272 (PDF pp. 289–290): the paragraph before Definition 7.3.1, and the sentence after Theorem 7.3.2

The Lean statement formalizes the counterexample of P001-C07-E008, in which $`\Pr[X_i=k,D_i=1]=3^{-k}`$ and $`\Pr[X_i=k,D_i=0]=2^{-(k+1)}`$ for every integer $`k\ge1`$. It asserts that $`\sum_{k\ge1}3^{-k}=\sum_{k\ge1}2^{-(k+1)}=\frac{1}{2},`$ that both probabilities are positive for every $`k`$, and that $`0\lt p_D(k)\lt 1`$ for every $`k`$, where $`p_D(k)=3^{-k}/(3^{-k}+2^{-(k+1)})`$. It asserts further that there exists no $`\epsilon\gt 0`$ such that $`\epsilon\lt p_D(k)\lt 1-\epsilon`$ for every $`k\ge1`$. Complete population overlap is expressed by the positivity of both joint probabilities at every $`k`$. Since $`\Pr[D_i=1]=\Pr[D_i=0]=1/2`$, this is the equality of the two conditional supports. The statement numbers the values of $`X_i`$ from zero, so that its index $`k`$ stands for the printed value $`k+1`$. The bound $`p_D(k)\le4/(k+6)`$ and the remark of the finding on the IPW estimator are not part of it.

### P001-T20

The relation between positivity and complete population overlap in Section 7.3, as corrected, with Theorem 7.3.2

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** Section 7.3, pp. 271–272 (PDF pp. 289–290): the paragraph before Definition 7.3.1, Definition 7.3.1, Theorem 7.3.2 and the sentence after it

Two Lean statements formalize the equivalence between complete population overlap and pointwise positivity, and the finite-support uniform-bound implication, in the correction of P001-C07-E008, for a discrete $`\mathbf{X}_i`$. The infinite-support counterexample has a separate statement under P001-T19. Let $`t(\mathbf{x})=\Pr[\mathbf{X}_i=\mathbf{x},D_i=1]`$ and $`c(\mathbf{x})=\Pr[\mathbf{X}_i=\mathbf{x},D_i=0]`$, so that $`p_D(\mathbf{x})=t(\mathbf{x})/(t(\mathbf{x})+c(\mathbf{x}))`$ on $`\mathrm{Supp}[\mathbf{X}_i]`$. The first statement asserts that $`\{\mathbf{x}:t(\mathbf{x})\gt 0\}=\{\mathbf{x}:c(\mathbf{x})\gt 0\}`$ if and only if $`0\lt p_D(\mathbf{x})\lt 1`$ for every $`\mathbf{x}`$ such that $`t(\mathbf{x})+c(\mathbf{x})\gt 0`$. This is the equivalence of complete population overlap and pointwise positivity on the support. The statement is made for any two nonnegative functions $`t`$ and $`c`$ on any set, and it does not require them to sum to one. The second asserts that if a function $`p`$ on a finite set satisfies $`0\lt p(\mathbf{x})\lt 1`$ at every point, then there exists $`\epsilon\gt 0`$ such that $`\epsilon\lt p(\mathbf{x})\lt 1-\epsilon`$ at every point. With the first, it implies the positivity assumption under complete population overlap when $`\mathrm{Supp}[\mathbf{X}_i]`$ is finite. The two sets of the first statement are the conditional supports of Definition 7.3.1 when $`\Pr[D_i=1]`$ and $`\Pr[D_i=0]`$ are positive, which is the condition $`\mathrm{Supp}[D_i]=\{0,1\}`$. Theorem 7.3.2 has no statement of its own here, and continuous covariates are not covered.

### P001-T21

A counterexample to the claim of Section 7.5 that complete coding of the applicant descriptions makes $`D_i`$ conditionally independent of the potential outcomes “by definition”, as printed

**Kind:** `counterexample`

**Location:** p. 278 (PDF p. 296), paragraph beginning “The fact that random assignment is unlikely to hold”; the setting of the application, pp. 276–278 (PDF pp. 294–296)

The Lean statements formalize the counterexample of P001-C07-E010. Every description has $`\mathbf{X}_i=0`$ and shows the gender $`D_i`$, and an electorate of type $`U_i\in\{0,1\}`$ has $`100`$ voters, of whom voter $`k`$ votes “no” exactly when $`k\le20+40U_i+5d`$ on a description showing gender $`d`$. The joint probabilities are $`\Pr[U_i=0,D_i=0]=\Pr[U_i=1,D_i=1]=3/8`$ and $`\Pr[U_i=0,D_i=1]=\Pr[U_i=1,D_i=0]=1/8`$. Two statements with the same content assert that a description records $`\mathbf{X}_i=0`$, the gender shown and that no voter knows the applicant, and that a voter casts the same vote on any two descriptions that show the same gender. They assert that the number of “no” votes is $`Y_i(d)=20+40U_i+5d`$, that the potential outcomes model holds, that $`0\le Y_i(d)\le100`$ and that $`Y_i(1)-Y_i(0)=5`$. Their last assertions are that $`\Pr[D_i=1]=1/2`$, that $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i\mid D_i=1]=55`$ and $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i\mid D_i=0]=30`$, and that $`(Y_i(0),Y_i(1))`$ and $`D_i`$ are not conditionally independent given $`\mathbf{X}_i`$. The third statement gives the counts of “no” votes alone and is narrower than the full counterexample. The premise that the coded description is all the information available to voters has no general definition in the Lean statements. It is represented within the example by the three entries of a description and by a voting rule that depends only on the type of the electorate, the voter and the description. The percentage of “no” votes is represented by their number among $`100`$ voters.

### P001-T22

Section 7.5, the justification of ignorability in the application, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** p. 278 (PDF p. 296), paragraph beginning “The fact that random assignment is unlikely to hold”

Two Lean statements give the formal content of the correction of P001-C07-E010, in which conditional independence is assumed and is not derived from the coding of the descriptions. In the first, the potential outcomes are functions of the description alone, $`Y_i(d)=m(\mathbf{X}_i,d)`$ for a fixed function $`m`$. This statement asserts that $`(Y_i(0),Y_i(1))`$ is then conditionally independent of $`D_i`$ given $`\mathbf{X}_i`$. The second is stated for a covariate vector with countably many values, with strong ignorability as an explicit hypothesis. It additionally takes Proposition 6.6 of Kallenberg, Foundations of Modern Probability (2nd edition, 2002), in its general sigma-field formulation, as an explicit hypothesis. It asserts the identity of Theorem 7.1.13, $`\mathrm{E}[\tau_i]=\sum_{\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{E}[Y_i\mid D_i=1,\mathbf{X}_i=\mathbf{x}]\Pr[\mathbf{X}_i=\mathbf{x}]-\sum_{\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{E}[Y_i\mid D_i=0,\mathbf{X}_i=\mathbf{x}]\Pr[\mathbf{X}_i=\mathbf{x}] .`$ Conditional independence is expressed through conditional probabilities given $`\mathbf{X}_i`$. For all measurable sets $`A`$ and $`B`$, the conditional probability that $`(Y_i(0),Y_i(1))`$ lies in $`A`$ and $`D_i`$ lies in $`B`$ equals the product of the two conditional probabilities, almost surely. The conditional means in the display are versions of the conditional expectation of the observed outcome given $`\mathbf{X}_i`$ on the events $`D_i=1`$ and $`D_i=0`$. In the first statement $`D_i`$ may be any real random variable and $`\mathbf{X}_i`$ may take values in any measurable space, which is broader than the setting of Section 7.5. The second statement requires the bounds $`\epsilon\lt \Pr[D_i=1\mid\mathbf{X}_i]\lt 1-\epsilon`$ at every sample point, as Definition 7.1.12 is printed, and it does not cover the case in which they hold only with probability one. Neither statement asserts that a complete coding of the descriptions implies conditional independence, and the counterexample to that implication has its own Lean statement.

### P001-T23

The explanation of monotonicity after Theorem 1.1.4, as corrected, with Theorem 1.1.4 (Basic Properties of Probability) and Theorem 1.1.7 (Addition Rule)

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** Explanation: p. 9 (PDF p. 27), first paragraph. Theorem 1.1.4: p. 8 (PDF p. 26). Theorem 1.1.7: p. 9 (PDF p. 27), proof on p. 10 (PDF p. 28)

Two Lean statements cover the corrected explanation of P001-C01-E007 and the two theorems, for a probability space $`(\Omega,S,P)`$. The first asserts that, if $`A\subseteq B`$, then every $`\omega\in A`$ lies in $`B`$, and $`P(A)\le P(B)`$. The first conclusion restates the hypothesis in the words of the corrected parenthesis, where $`B`$ occurs whenever $`A`$ does, and the second conclusion is the monotonicity property of Theorem 1.1.4. The second Lean statement asserts the remaining properties of Theorem 1.1.4 and the Addition Rule for events $`A`$ and $`B`$, namely $`P(B\backslash A)=P(B)-P(A)`$ if $`A\subseteq B`$, $`P(\emptyset)=0`$, $`0\le P(A)\le1`$, $`P(A^C)=1-P(A)`$, and $`P(A\cup B)=P(A)+P(B)-P(A\cap B) .`$ A probability space is represented by a probability measure on a measurable space, and the events are its measurable sets. The first statement does not require $`A`$ and $`B`$ to be events. For a subset of $`\Omega`$ that is not an event, $`P`$ then denotes the outer measure, so this statement is broader than the printed one. No Lean statement asserts that the parenthesis as printed is false, and the example of the finding, a fair die with $`A=\{6\}`$ and $`B=\{4,5,6\}`$, is not formalized.

### P001-T24

Theorem 1.1.13 (Law of Total Probability), with the positivity condition of its proof and the remark on partitions after it, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** Theorem 1.1.13: p. 12 (PDF p. 30). Proof and the remark after it: p. 13 (PDF p. 31). Definition 1.1.12: p. 12 (PDF p. 30). Definition 1.1.8 and Theorem 1.1.9: p. 10 (PDF p. 28)

Three Lean statements cover the theorem and the two corrections of P001-C01-E008. The main statement is Theorem 1.1.13 for a finite or countably infinite partition $`\{A_i\}`$ of $`\Omega`$ into nonempty events and an event $`B`$. It asserts that $`P(B)=\sum_iP(B\cap A_i)`$ and that, if $`P(A_i)\gt 0`$ for every $`i`$, then $`P(B)=\sum_i\frac{P(B\cap A_i)}{P(A_i)}\,P(A_i) .`$ The conditional probability $`P(B\mid A_i)`$ is written as the ratio of Definition 1.1.8, and the second formula is asserted only under the strict inequality of the corrected proof. The second statement is the corrected remark, that $`A`$ and $`A^C`$ are both nonempty if and only if $`A\ne\emptyset`$ and $`A\ne\Omega`$. It is stated for an arbitrary subset of an arbitrary set, without a probability space. A supporting statement repeats the main one for any finite or countably infinite index set, and also gives the two-cell nonemptiness criterion for every event $`A`$. It explicitly includes the identity $`P(B)=P(B\cap A)+P(B\cap A^C)`$ for all events $`A`$ and $`B`$, including $`A=\emptyset`$ and $`A=\Omega`$, and records that $`P(\emptyset)`$ is nonnegative but not strictly positive.

### P001-T25

A counterexample to the third bullet of the first list on p. 20, $`\Pr[X\lt 4]=\sum_{x=1}^{3}f(x)`$ for an integer-valued $`X`$, as printed

The Lean statement formalizes the first counterexample of P001-C01-E006, in which $`X=0`$ with probability one. The second counterexample of the finding, the Bernoulli random variable with $`p=1/3`$, is not formalized.

### P001-T26

The third bullet of the first list on p. 20, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** p. 20 (PDF p. 38), first bulleted list, third bullet

The Lean statement contains the corrected bullet of P001-C01-E006, $`\Pr[X\lt 4]=\sum_{x=-\infty}^{3}f(x)`$, for a random variable $`X`$ that takes only integer values. It makes three assertions. The probability $`\Pr[X\lt 4]`$ is the sum of $`f(x)`$ over all integers $`x\le3`$. This sum and the printed one differ by the mass at the nonpositive integers, $`\sum_{x=-\infty}^{3}f(x)-\sum_{x=1}^{3}f(x)=\sum_{x=-\infty}^{0}f(x)=\Pr[X\le0] .`$ The two sums are equal if and only if $`\Pr[X\le0]=0`$. The distribution of $`X`$ is represented by its PMF, a non-negative function on the integers with sum one, and the probability of an event is the sum of $`f`$ over the integers in it. The remark that the printed sum is correct for the die roll of Example 1.2.6, where it equals $`1/2`$, is not part of the Lean statement.

### P001-T27

A counterexample to the first sentence of Example 1.2.13, as printed

**Kind:** `counterexample`

**Location:** p. 23 (PDF p. 41), Example 1.2.13, first sentence. Definition 1.2.4: p. 18 (PDF p. 36)

For a statement giving a counterexample, “Proved” means that the counterexample is proved. The Lean statement formalizes the counterexample of P001-C01-E005. The random variable $`X`$ takes the value $`(2k+1)/2^{m+1}`$ with probability $`2^{-(2m+1)}`$, for integers $`m\ge0`$ and $`0\le k\lt 2^m`$, and its range is the set of dyadic rationals in $`(0,1)`$. The statement asserts that these probabilities sum to one and that the CDF $`F`$ of $`X`$ satisfies $`F(a)\lt F(b)`$ whenever $`0\lt a\lt b\lt 1`$. It asserts further that $`F`$ is continuous at $`1/3`$ and that there exists no $`\epsilon\gt 0`$ such that $`F(x)=F(1/3)`$ for all $`x`$ with $`|x-1/3|\lt \epsilon`$. The CDF of this discrete random variable does not jump at $`1/3`$ and is not flat near $`1/3`$, which contradicts the printed description. The CDF is represented as the sum of the probabilities of the values that are at most $`x`$, and no probability space is given explicitly. The statement does not assert that the listed values are distinct, that they are exactly the dyadic rationals in $`(0,1)`$, or what the sizes of the jumps are.

### P001-T28

Four Lean statements cover the correction of P001-C01-E005. In the first statement $`X`$ is a measurable function on a probability space. In the other three the probability space has finitely many outcomes with non-negative weights that sum to one, and $`X`$ assigns a real value to each outcome. This describes the same distributions as a random variable with finite range on a general probability space. The third statement allows a listed value to have probability zero.

### P001-T30

The sentence after Definition 1.2.14 and Definition 1.2.15, as corrected, with the corresponding statements for joint PDFs

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** p. 24 (PDF p. 42), sentence after Definition 1.2.14; p. 25 (PDF p. 43), Definition 1.2.15 and Theorem 1.2.16; p. 36 (PDF p. 54), Definitions 1.3.10 and 1.3.11; p. 38 (PDF p. 56), Definition 1.3.16; p. 39 (PDF p. 57), Theorem 1.3.17

Five Lean statements cover the correction of P001-C01-E002. Let $`f`$ and $`g`$ be PDFs of the same random variable, with CDF $`F`$. The first statement asserts that $`f=g`$ almost everywhere, that $`F'(x)=f(x)`$ for almost every $`x`$, that $`F'(x)\ge0`$ at every $`x`$ where $`F`$ is differentiable, and that $`\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(x)\,dx=1`$. The second asserts that a non-negative function $`h`$, measurable Lebesgue-almost everywhere, that equals $`f`$ almost everywhere is also a PDF, and that $`F(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{x}h(u)\,du`$ for all $`x`$. The third asserts that two joint PDFs of the same pair $`(X,Y)`$ are equal almost everywhere on $`\mathbb{R}^2`$. The fourth concerns independent $`U(0,1)`$ random variables and $`0\lt y\lt 1`$. It asserts that the mixed derivative of their joint CDF does not exist at $`(0,y)`$, the derivative in the second argument being taken first. The fifth asserts that jointly continuous $`X`$ and $`Y`$ are independent if and only if $`f(x,y)=f_X(x)f_Y(y)`$ for almost every $`(x,y)`$. In these statements a PDF of a distribution is a non-negative integrable function such that the distribution is Lebesgue measure weighted by it, which is equivalent to the integral condition of Definition 1.2.14. Independence means that the joint distribution is the product of the two marginal distributions. The statements do not cover the third bullet of Theorem 1.3.17, on the conditional PDF.

### P001-T31

A counterexample to the sentence “More specifically, $`X(\Omega)`$ must include some interval or union of intervals of the real line”, as printed

**Kind:** `counterexample`

**Location:** p. 24 (PDF p. 42), paragraph before Definition 1.2.14

For a statement giving a counterexample, “Proved” means that the counterexample is proved. The Lean statement formalizes the counterexample of P001-C01-E004. Let $`C_0=[0,1]`$, let $`C_n`$ be obtained from $`C_{n-1}`$ by removing the open middle interval of length $`4^{-n}`$ from each of its component intervals, and let $`C=\bigcap_{n\ge0}C_n`$. The sample space is $`C`$, the probability is $`P(A)=2\lambda(A)`$, where $`\lambda`$ denotes Lebesgue measure, and $`X(\omega)=\omega`$. The statement asserts that $`C`$ is closed, that $`\lambda(C)=1/2`$, and that $`P`$ is a probability measure. It asserts further that $`X`$ has the PDF $`f=2\cdot\mathbf{1}_C`$, that $`X(\Omega)=C`$, and that $`C`$ contains no interval $`[a,b]`$ with $`a\lt b`$. This makes $`X`$ a continuous random variable in the sense of Definition 1.2.14 whose range contains no interval of positive length. The component intervals of $`C_n`$ are indexed by the sequences of $`n`$ choices between a left and a right part. That $`X`$ has the PDF $`f`$ means that the distribution of $`X`$ is Lebesgue measure weighted by $`f`$. The identity $`F(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{x}f(u)\,du`$ and the equality $`\mathrm{Supp}[X]=C`$ follow from these assertions and are not stated separately.

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

For a joint PDF $`f`$ on $`\mathbb R^2`$, put $`M(y)=\int_{\mathbb R} f(x,y)\,dx\in[0,\infty]`$, using the nonnegative extended integral. The marginal statement asserts that $`M`$ is finite for Lebesgue-almost every $`y`$ and that every finite nonnegative real function $`g`$ with $`g=M`$ almost everywhere is a PDF of $`Y`$. No separate measurability premise is imposed on $`g`$: that agreement supplies its almost-everywhere measurability and integrability. The pair is represented by its joint law, and the marginal law is Lebesgue measure weighted by $`g`$. A separate conditional-density statement takes any nonnegative Borel measurable $`f`$, whether or not it is a joint PDF, and a slice with positive finite integral. The ratio of that slice to its integral is nonnegative and integrable with integral one. This is normalization, without identification of a conditional distribution.

The same statement asserts that the formula is then identically zero and has integral $`0`$ and not $`1`$. This last assertion rests on the convention of Lean that a quotient with denominator zero is zero, and it is not an assertion about the printed formula, which is undefined at $`\rho=\pm1`$.

Two Lean statements formalize the counterexample of P001-C02-E004. The sample space has three outcomes $`u`$, $`v`$ and $`n`$ with probabilities $`1/2`$, $`1/2`$ and $`0`$. The assertions about $`a`$ and $`b`$ do not restrict the sign of $`b`$, so they are stronger than the failure of the printed bullets, which require $`b\gt 0`$.

### P001-T43

Theorem 2.2.21 (Best Linear Predictor), with the first-order conditions of its proof corrected, its two corollaries and Theorem 2.2.22

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** Theorem 2.2.21: p. 76 (PDF p. 94). Proof and footnote 19: pp. 77–78 (PDF pp. 95–96). Corollaries: p. 78 (PDF p. 96). Theorem 2.2.22: p. 79 (PDF p. 97)

Two Lean statements cover the theorem with the correction of P001-C02-N006, for random variables $`X`$ and $`Y`$ with finite second moments and $`\mathrm{V}[X]\gt 0`$. I write $`Q(a,b)=\mathrm{E}[(Y-a-bX)^2]`$, $`\beta=\mathrm{Cov}[X,Y]/\mathrm{V}[X]`$ and $`\alpha=\mathrm{E}[Y]-\beta\,\mathrm{E}[X]`$. The first statement asserts that, at every $`(a,b)`$, $`\frac{\partial Q}{\partial a}(a,b)=-2\,\mathrm{E}[Y-a-bX],\qquad\frac{\partial Q}{\partial b}(a,b)=-2\,\mathrm{E}[(Y-a-bX)X] ,`$ that both derivatives vanish if and only if $`(a,b)=(\alpha,\beta)`$, and that $`Q(a,b)\ge Q(\alpha,\beta)`$ with equality if and only if $`(a,b)=(\alpha,\beta)`$. The second gives the second derivatives of footnote 19, namely $`2`$ in $`a`$, $`2\,\mathrm{E}[X^2]`$ in $`b`$ and $`2\,\mathrm{E}[X]`$ mixed, and the identity $`2\cdot2\,\mathrm{E}[X^2]-(2\,\mathrm{E}[X])^2=4\,\mathrm{V}[X]`$. It asserts that $`(\alpha,\beta)`$ minimizes $`\mathrm{E}[(\mathrm{E}[Y\mid X]-a-bX)^2]`$, and that $`\varepsilon=Y-(\alpha+\beta X)`$ satisfies $`\mathrm{E}[\varepsilon]=0`$, $`\mathrm{E}[X\varepsilon]=0`$ and $`\mathrm{Cov}[X,\varepsilon]=0`$. It asserts further that, if a linear function $`a+bx`$ is a version of the CEF, then $`\mathrm{E}[Y\mid X]=\alpha+\beta X`$ almost surely. The CEF is a measurable function $`m`$ such that $`m(X)`$ has a finite second moment and $`\mathrm{E}[Y\mathbf{1}\{X\in A\}]=\mathrm{E}[m(X)\mathbf{1}\{X\in A\}]`$ for every measurable set $`A`$. The first statement gives the partial derivative in each argument and does not assert that $`Q`$ is differentiable as a function on $`\mathbb{R}^2`$. In the second, the equality of a linear CEF with the BLP is asserted almost surely and not at every point of the range of $`X`$. Every assertion of the second statement supposes that such a function $`m`$ is given.

Example 2.2.24, the CEF formula, as corrected, with the CEF and the BLP of Example 2.2.23

Two Lean statements with the same content formalize the corrected sentence of P001-C02-E003 and the computation of Example 2.2.23. A function $`G`$ is a CEF of $`Y`$ given $`X`$ if $`G`$ is measurable, $`Y`$ and $`G(X)`$ are integrable and have the same integral over every event defined by $`X`$. ### P001-T47

Theorem 2.2.25, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** pp. 82–83 (PDF pp. 100–101)

The main Lean statement is the theorem with the sub-bullet corrected as in P001-C02-E006. Let $`X`$ and $`Y`$ be independent random variables with finite second moments, and let $`g`$ and $`h`$ agree almost surely under the respective laws of $`X`$ and $`Y`$ with Borel measurable functions, with $`g(X)`$ and $`h(Y)`$ measurable on the sample space and having finite second moments. Globally Borel measurable $`g`$ and $`h`$ are a sufficient special case. The statement asserts that $`\mathrm{E}[Y|X]=\mathrm{E}[Y]`$ and that $`\mathrm{E}[(Y-\mathrm{E}[Y])^2|X]=\mathrm{V}[Y]`$, which is the bullet $`\mathrm{V}[Y|X]=\mathrm{V}[Y]`$. It asserts that $`\mathrm{E}[(Y-\mathrm{E}[Y])^2]\le\mathrm{E}[(Y-a-bX)^2]`$ for all real $`a`$ and $`b`$. For the functions, it asserts that $`g(X)`$ and $`h(Y)`$ are independent and that $`\mathrm{E}[h(Y)|g(X)]=\mathrm{E}[h(Y)]`$, which is the corrected sub-bullet. It asserts that the constant $`\mathrm{E}[h(Y)]`$ has mean squared error no greater than that of $`a+b\,g(X)`$, for all real $`a`$ and $`b`$. Each conditional expectation is asserted as a version, determined up to a null set. In the main statement, the two bullets on the BLP are stated as these inequalities and not through the coefficients of Theorem 2.2.21. They therefore cover $`\mathrm{V}[X]=0`$ and $`\mathrm{V}[g(X)]=0`$, where the minimizer is not unique. Finite second moments make the book’s standing convention on expectations explicit. Two further statements accompany the main one. The first contains the conditional variance and the two inequalities, for functions $`g`$ and $`h`$ that are measurable on $`\mathbb{R}`$. The second covers the original conditional mean and variance, original and transformed affine-risk minimization, transformed independence and the corrected transformed CEF under the same law-almost-everywhere measurability and finite-second-moment assumptions as the main statement. It also identifies the slope as zero and the intercept as the response mean whenever the corresponding regressor variance is positive.

The second asserts that $`g(x_{[1]},\ldots,x_{[k]}+1,\ldots,x_{[K]})-g(x_{[1]},\ldots,x_{[K]})=\beta_k`$ and that $`g(0,\ldots,0)=\beta_0`$. The third asserts that the partial derivative of $`g`$ with respect to $`x_{[k]}`$ exists at every point and equals $`\beta_k`$. The statements hold for every function of this form, and they do not use that the coefficients minimize the mean squared error. In this respect they are broader than the passage. Definition 2.3.6, which selects the coefficients of the BLP, is not part of them. The derivative is taken in the real arguments $`x_{[k]}`$, where Theorem 2.3.7 writes $`\partial g(\mathbf{X})/\partial X_{[k]}`$.

### P001-T50

The variance in Example 3.1.3, the variance functional $`T_{\mathrm{V}}(F)`$ and the last line of Example 3.3.4, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** p. 94 (PDF p. 112), Example 3.1.3 (continued); p. 118 (PDF p. 136) and p. 119 (PDF p. 137), the displays for $`T_{\mathrm{V}}(F)`$; p. 119 (PDF p. 137), Example 3.3.4

Four Lean statements cover the three displays corrected in P001-C03-E003. The first concerns Example 3.1.3, and takes a finite population with responses $`x_1,\ldots,x_N`$ and a unit selected with equal probabilities, as in footnote 6 of the book. It asserts that $`\Pr[X=t]`$ is the number of units with $`x_i=t`$ divided by $`N`$, that $`\mathrm{E}[X]=\mu`$ and that $`\mathrm{V}[X]=N^{-1}\sum_{i}(x_i-\mu)^2=\sigma^2`$, where $`\mu=N^{-1}\sum_{i}x_i`$. For the population $`(3,4,3,10)`$ of the example it asserts that $`f_{FP}(3)=1/2`$, $`f_{FP}(4)=f_{FP}(10)=1/4`$, $`\mathrm{E}[X]=5`$ and $`\mathrm{V}[X]=17/2`$. For the variance functional, the second statement asserts that $`\mathrm{V}[X]=\mathrm{E}[X^2]-\mathrm{E}[X]^2`$ for every random variable with finite second moment, with the minus sign of the corrected display. The moments are integrals over the probability space, which equal the integrals of $`x^2`$ and of $`x`$ with respect to $`F`$. The third statement concerns Example 3.3.4, and takes real numbers $`X_1,\ldots,X_n`$ with $`n\ge1`$ and the distribution that puts mass $`1/n`$ at each of them, which represents $`\hat F`$. It asserts that this is a probability distribution with mean $`n^{-1}\sum_{i}X_i=\overline{X}`$ and variance $`\overline{X^2}-\overline{X}^2`$. This holds for every sample, and no i.i.d. assumption appears. The fourth statement combines the general finite-population probabilities and moments, the numerical population example, the corrected variance functional for a square-integrable random variable $`Z`$, the amount $`2(\mathrm{E}[Z])^2`$ by which the printed plus-sign expression exceeds $`\mathrm{V}[Z]`$, and the empirical-distribution mean and variance formulas.

### P001-T51

The gloss of convergence in probability after Definition 3.2.6, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

The Lean statement is the equivalence behind the gloss as corrected in P001-C03-E005. Let $`(T_{(1)},T_{(2)},T_{(3)},\ldots)`$ be a sequence of random variables and let $`c\in\mathbb{R}`$. The statement asserts that $`T_{(n)}\xrightarrow{p}c`$ if and only if, for every $`p\in[0,1)`$ and every $`\epsilon\gt 0`$, there exists $`N`$ such that $`\Pr\bigl[|T_{(n)}-c|\lt \epsilon\bigr]\ge p\quad\text{for every }n\ge N .`$ Convergence in probability is the first form of Definition 3.2.6, and $`N`$ may depend on $`p`$ and $`\epsilon`$. The random variables are measurable real functions on an arbitrary probability space. The hypotheses and the conclusion are those of the corrected gloss. The statement contains no example of a sequence for which the printed gloss, which allows $`p=1`$, fails.

### P001-T53

A counterexample to a step in the proof of Theorem 3.2.19, as printed

**Kind:** `counterexample`

**Location:** p. 107 (PDF p. 125), proof step; Theorem 3.2.19, p. 106 (PDF p. 124); Theorem 3.2.8, p. 100 (PDF p. 118)

For a statement giving a counterexample, “Proved” means that the counterexample is proved. The Lean statement formalizes the distribution in the example of P001-C03-E006, with $`\Pr[X=2^k]=7/8^{k+1}`$ for $`k=0,1,2,\ldots`$. It asserts that these masses sum to $`1`$ and that $`\sum_{k\ge0}2^k\,\frac{7}{8^{k+1}}=\frac76,\qquad \sum_{k\ge0}4^k\,\frac{7}{8^{k+1}}=\frac74 ,`$ where both series converge. It asserts that $`\sum_{k\ge0}16^k\cdot 7/8^{k+1}=\infty`$ and that $`7/4-(7/6)^2=7/18`$. These are the values of $`\mathrm{E}[X]`$, $`\mathrm{E}[X^2]`$, $`\mathrm{E}[X^4]`$ and $`\mathrm{V}[X]`$. Then $`\mathrm{V}[X]`$ is finite and positive, as Theorem 3.2.19 assumes, and $`\mathrm{V}[X^2]`$ is not finite, so that Theorem 3.2.8 does not apply to the squares. The statement is about the masses and the series only. It does not construct the random variable or i.i.d. draws from this distribution, and neither theorem is mentioned in it. The example is a counterexample to the step of the proof and not to Theorem 3.2.19.

### P001-T55

Definition 3.2.32 and footnote 23, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** pp. 113–114 (PDF pp. 131–132), Definition 3.2.32; footnote 23, p. 114 (PDF p. 132)

Five Lean statements cover the assertions attached to the definition as corrected in P001-C03-N007. In the first three statements the estimator $`\hat{\mathrm{V}}[\hat\theta]`$ is a sequence of real functions $`\hat v_n`$ on a probability space, and the limit variance is a real number $`v`$. The two conditions of the definition then read $`n\hat v_n\xrightarrow{p}v`$ and $`\sqrt{n}\sqrt{\hat v_n}\xrightarrow{p}\sqrt{v}`$. The first statement asserts that these two conditions are equivalent when $`\hat v_n\ge0`$ and $`v\ge0`$, which is the sentence after the definition. The second asserts that $`V_n\xrightarrow{p}v`$ if and only if $`V_n-v\xrightarrow{p}0`$, for a sequence of random variables $`V_n`$. The third assumes that each variance estimate is measurable and asserts the two equivalences of footnote 23, namely that $`n\hat v_n\xrightarrow{p}v`$ if and only if $`n\hat v_n-v\xrightarrow{p}0`$, and that $`\sqrt{n}\sqrt{\hat v_n}\xrightarrow{p}\sqrt{v}`$ if and only if $`\sqrt{n}\sqrt{\hat v_n}-\sqrt{v}\xrightarrow{p}0`$. The estimator $`\hat\theta`$ and the limit $`T`$ of $`\sqrt{n}(\hat\theta-\theta)`$ do not appear in these first three statements. The identity $`v=\mathrm{V}[T]`$ is therefore not part of these statements, and the hat restored by the correction has no counterpart in them. The first statement does not require $`\hat v_n`$ to be measurable, and the third has no sign condition, the square root of a negative number being read as $`0`$. The fourth and fifth statements give the same equivalences for an estimator and its limiting law. They take measurable estimators $`\hat\theta_n`$ and measurable nonnegative variance estimates $`\hat v_n`$, and assume that $`\sqrt{n}(\hat\theta_n-\theta)`$ converges in distribution to a real probability law $`\nu`$ with finite second moment. Each asserts both centered equivalences of footnote 23 and the equivalence of variance and standard-error consistency, with $`v=\mathrm{Var}_\nu(\mathrm{id})`$. These two statements include the estimator and its limiting law; the law need not be Gaussian and its variance may be zero.

### P001-T56

A counterexample to Theorem 3.3.6, as printed

**Kind:** `counterexample`

**Location:** p. 120 (PDF p. 138), Theorem 3.3.6 and footnote 31; Definition 3.2.28, p. 112 (PDF p. 130)

Four Lean statements formalize the two counterexamples of P001-C03-E002. The first three concern the constant functional $`T_0(G)=7`$. The first asserts that $`\sqrt{n}(7-7)=0`$ for all $`n`$, and that the sequence identically equal to $`0`$ does not converge in distribution to $`N(0,\phi^2)`$ for any $`\phi\gt 0`$. The second asserts that $`T_0`$ is Hadamard differentiable at every $`F`$ with respect to the Kolmogorov distance. The third asserts that the functional equal to $`7`$ on an arbitrary normed space is Hadamard differentiable with derivative $`0`$. For the second counterexample, let $`X_1,X_2,\ldots`$ be i.i.d. with $`\Pr[X_i=0]=\Pr[X_i=1]=1/2`$ and CDF $`F`$, and let $`T_1(G)=(G(0)-1/2)^2`$. The fourth statement asserts that $`T_1`$ is Hadamard differentiable at $`F`$, that $`T_1(F)=0`$, that $`\mathrm{E}\Bigl[\bigl(\sqrt{n}\,T_1(\hat F)\bigr)^2\Bigr]=\frac{3n-2}{16n^2}\quad\text{for every }n\ge1 ,`$ and that $`\sqrt{n}\,T_1(\hat F)\xrightarrow{p}0`$. The book does not define Hadamard differentiability. In the second and fourth statements it means that there exists a linear map $`L`$, bounded in the supremum norm, such that $`(T(F+t_rh_r)-T(F))/t_r\to L(h)`$ whenever $`t_r\gt 0`$, $`t_r\to0`$, the bounded functions $`h_r`$ converge uniformly to $`h`$, and every $`F+t_rh_r`$ is a CDF. The first statement contains no functional and no sample, since $`\hat\theta=\theta=7`$ for all observations. The fourth statement does not assert that the derivative of $`T_1`$ is $`0`$, or that $`\sqrt{n}\,T_1(\hat F)`$ has no limit $`N(0,\phi^2)`$ with $`\phi\gt 0`$. The second of these follows from the convergence in probability to $`0`$.
