### P001-T57

Theorem 3.3.6, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** p. 120 (PDF p. 138), Theorem 3.3.6 and footnote 31; Definition 3.2.28, p. 112 (PDF p. 130)

Three Lean statements with the same content assert the theorem as corrected in P001-C03-E002. Let $`X_1,X_2,\ldots`$ be i.i.d. with CDF $`F`$, and let $`T`$ be a real function on the space of bounded functions on $`\mathbb{R}`$ with the supremum norm, which contains $`F`$ and $`\hat F`$. Suppose that $`T`$ is Hadamard differentiable at $`F`$ with derivative $`\dot T_F`$, a continuous linear map, that $`\mathbb{B}_F`$ is an $`F`$-Brownian bridge, and that $`0\lt \mathrm{Var}[\dot T_F(\mathbb{B}_F)]\lt \infty`$. The statements assert that $`\sqrt{n}(\hat F-F)`$ converges weakly to $`\mathbb{B}_F`$ and that $`\sqrt{n}\bigl(T(\hat F)-T(F)\bigr)\xrightarrow{d}N\bigl(0,\mathrm{Var}[\dot T_F(\mathbb{B}_F)]\bigr) .`$ The bridge is a probability distribution on the space of bounded functions. Its paths are uniformly continuous with respect to $`|F(x)-F(y)|`$ and vanish where $`F`$ equals $`0`$ or $`1`$, and every finite sum $`\sum_j a_j\mathbb{B}_F(x_j)`$ is centered normal with variance $`\sum_{j,k}a_ja_k\bigl(F(\min(x_j,x_k))-F(x_j)F(x_k)\bigr)`$. Differentiability is required along such paths, for perturbations $`F+t_rh_r`$ that are CDFs, with $`t_r\gt 0`$. The statements add two hypotheses to the corrected theorem. They assume that a distribution with these properties exists on this space, and that $`T(\hat F)`$ is measurable for each $`n`$. The weak convergence of $`\sqrt{n}(\hat F-F)`$ is defined with outer expectations, since $`\hat F`$ need not be measurable as a random element of this space. A fourth statement asserts the convergence of $`\sqrt{n}(\hat F-F)`$ alone. A fifth takes a bounded measurable function $`a`$ of bounded variation and the functional $`T(G)=\int a(x)\,dG(x)`$. For each $`n\ge1`$, it asserts that $`T(\hat F)=n^{-1}\sum_{i}a(X_i)`$, that $`\sqrt{n}(T(\hat F)-T(F))=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i}(a(X_i)-\mathrm{E}[a(X)])`$, and that this quantity has variance $`\mathrm{V}[a(X)]`$. The central limit theorem for it is not stated. Two supporting statements are narrower than the corrected theorem, one for the empirical CDF and one in an arbitrary Banach space. Each additionally assumes the functional delta theorem of van der Vaart (1998, Theorem 20.8) as an explicit hypothesis. The abstract statement assumes measurable approximating functions that lie in the functional’s domain for every positive sample size, and a weak limit for their centered root-$`n`$ process; the empirical-CDF statement assumes the outer empirical-process limit and measurability of the scalar statistic. Both assume Hadamard differentiability along a tangent set that carries the limit law and a centered Gaussian law with strictly positive variance for the derivative applied to the limit. They assert the scalar normal limit conditional on these inputs. The process limit and the cited delta theorem are hypotheses of these implications, which do not identify the limiting process as a Brownian bridge.

### P001-T58

A counterexample to Definition 3.3.9 with the kernels admitted by Definition 3.3.7, as printed

**Kind:** `counterexample`

**Location:** Definition 3.3.7, p. 121 (PDF p. 139); Definition 3.3.9, p. 123 (PDF p. 141); Definition 3.3.2, p. 118 (PDF p. 136)

The Lean statement formalizes the first counterexample of P001-C03-N011. Let $`K(t)=1`$ for $`|t|\le1`$, $`K(t)=-1/2`$ for $`1\lt |t|\le2`$ and $`K(t)=0`$ for $`|t|\gt 2`$. The statement asserts that $`K`$ is even and integrable with $`\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}K(t)\,dt=1`$, so that Definition 3.3.7 admits it. It asserts that, for $`n=1`$, $`h=1`$ and every value $`a`$ of the observation $`X_1`$, $`\hat F_K(a-1)=\int_{-\infty}^{a-1}K(u-a)\,du=-\frac12 .`$ A CDF has no negative value, so $`\hat F_K`$ is not a CDF. The observation is an arbitrary real number $`a`$ and not a random variable. Since the equality holds for all $`a`$, it holds for every sample of size one, whatever the distribution of $`X_1`$. The statement does not include the other two values of the finding, $`\hat F_K(a-2)=0`$ and $`\hat F_K(a+1)=3/2`$. The second counterexample of the finding, with the functional $`T(G)=\sqrt{G(0)}`$ and a triangular density, is not formalized.

### P001-T59

Definition 3.3.9, as corrected with a nonnegative kernel

The definition as corrected in P001-C03-N011 requires that $`\hat F_K`$ be a CDF, and the Lean statement asserts this for a nonnegative kernel. Let $`K`$ be an integrable even function with $`K\ge0`$ almost everywhere and $`\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}K(x)\,dx=1`$, let $`h\gt 0`$, and let $`x_1,\ldots,x_n`$ be real numbers with $`n\ge1`$. The statement asserts that $`\hat f_K\ge0`$ almost everywhere and that $`\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat f_K(u)\,du=1`$. It asserts that $`\hat F_K`$ is nondecreasing and continuous, with limit $`0`$ at $`-\infty`$ and limit $`1`$ at $`+\infty`$, so that $`\hat F_K`$ is a CDF. The sample is a vector of fixed real numbers, which covers every realization of the observations. The statement does not mention a statistical functional $`T`$. That $`T(\hat F_K)`$ is defined is a consequence of $`\hat F_K\in\mathcal{F}`$. Evenness of $`K`$ is a hypothesis, as in Definition 3.3.7, and the conclusions do not depend on it. Integrability of $`K`$ is assumed, where the book writes only the integral.

### P001-T60

Definition 3.3.9, last line, with the integrand corrected to $`\hat f_K`$

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** p. 123 (PDF p. 141), Definition 3.3.9, last line

Two Lean statements cover the line as corrected in P001-C03-N012. In both, $`\hat F_K`$ is the integral of the kernel density estimator $`\hat f_K`$ of Definition 3.3.7, as in the corrected line, and the sample is a vector of real numbers $`X_1,\ldots,X_n`$ with $`n\ge1`$. The first statement asserts that, for an integrable function $`K`$, for $`h\gt 0`$ and for all $`x\in\mathbb{R}`$, $`\hat F_K(x)=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{-\infty}^{(x-X_i)/h}K(t)\,dt .`$ It does not require $`K`$ to be even or to have integral $`1`$, and it is broader than the definition in this respect. The second statement concerns the rectangular kernel $`K(t)=\frac12\,\mathrm{I}(-1\le t\le1)`$ of Theorem 3.3.8. It asserts that $`\hat f_K(x)=\frac{\hat F(x+h)-\hat F(x-h)}{2h}`$ at every $`x`$ such that no observation equals $`x-h`$, where $`\hat F`$ is the empirical CDF. This condition does not appear in the proof sketch on p. 123, which writes the equality without it.

### P001-T61

A counterexample to the sentence on the bootstrap standard deviation in Section 3.4.3, as printed

**Kind:** `counterexample`

**Location:** p. 131 (PDF p. 149), sentence after the four bootstrap steps; Section 3.3.1 and footnote 31, p. 120 (PDF p. 138); Definition 3.2.32, pp. 113–114 (PDF pp. 131–132)

For a statement giving a counterexample, “Proved” means that the counterexample is proved. Three Lean statements formalize the counterexample of P001-C03-N016. In each, $`X_1,X_2,\ldots`$ are i.i.d. with the distribution of the finding, which is uniform on $`[-1,1]`$ with probability $`1/2`$, and equal to $`2^{1/U^2}`$ or to $`-2^{1/U^2}`$ with probability $`1/4`$ each, where $`U`$ is uniform on $`(0,1)`$. The sample size $`n`$ is odd, and $`\mathrm{V}_*`$ is the exact variance over the $`n^n`$ equally likely samples of size $`n`$ drawn with replacement. The first statement concerns the functional $`T_0`$, where $`T_0(G)=G(0)-1/2`$ if $`1/4\le G(0)\le3/4`$ and $`T_0(G)`$ is the median of $`G`$ otherwise. It asserts that $`T_0(G)-T_0(F)=G(0)-F(0)`$ for every function $`G`$ such that $`|G(x)-F(x)|\lt 1/4`$ for all $`x`$. It asserts that $`\sqrt{n}\,T_0(\hat F)\xrightarrow{d}N(0,1/4)`$, that $`\Pr\bigl[n\mathrm{V}_*[T_0(\hat F^*)]\gt 5\bigr]\ge\frac{3}{16}\quad\text{for every odd }n\ge5 ,`$ and that $`n\mathrm{V}_*[T_0(\hat F^*)]`$ does not converge in probability to $`1/4`$. The second statement additionally assumes the bootstrap theorem of Bickel and Freedman (1981, Theorem 2.1(a,b)) as an explicit hypothesis. Under this hypothesis, it asserts that, along positive odd sample sizes, the conditional distribution of $`\sqrt{n}(T_0(\hat F^*)-T_0(\hat F))`$ given the sample converges weakly to $`N(0,1/4)`$ in probability. This statement concerns the conditional weak limit and does not assert consistency of bootstrap variances. The third statement asserts, for the sample median $`M_n`$, that $`\sqrt{n}M_n\xrightarrow{d}N(0,4)`$, that the conditional distribution of $`\sqrt{n}(M_n^*-M_n)`$ converges weakly to $`N(0,4)`$ in probability, that $`\Pr[n\mathrm{V}_*[M_n^*]\gt 5]\ge3/16`$ for all odd $`n\ge5`$, and that $`n\mathrm{V}_*[M_n^*]`$ does not converge in probability to $`4`$. Hadamard differentiability is not asserted for either functional. For $`T_0`$ it follows from the first assertion, by which $`T_0`$ is affine near $`F`$. For the median, the statement has no counterpart of the regularity condition of Section 3.3.1. The statistic $`T_0(\hat F)`$ is written as a function of the sample. It is the fraction of observations that are at most $`0`$, minus $`1/2`$, if this fraction lies in $`[1/4,3/4]`$, and the sample median otherwise.

### P001-T62

Section 3.4.3, the sentence on the bootstrap standard deviation, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** p. 131 (PDF p. 149), sentence after the four bootstrap steps; Definition 3.2.32, pp. 113–114 (PDF pp. 131–132)

Five Lean statements cover the sentence as corrected in P001-C03-N016 and its two cases. The first is the general assertion, in which the conditional distribution of $`Z_n^*=\sqrt{n}(\hat\theta^*-\hat\theta)`$ given the observations is represented by a random probability distribution $`\nu_n`$ on $`\mathbb{R}`$ with finite second moment, whose variance stands for $`n\mathrm{V}_*[\hat\theta^*]`$. Suppose that $`\nu_n`$ converges weakly to $`N(0,\phi^2)`$ in probability, with $`\phi\gt 0`$. The statement asserts that the moment condition (M) of the corrected sentence, $`\lim_{K\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\Pr\Bigl[\mathrm{E}_*\bigl[(Z_n^*)^2\mathbf{1}\{|Z_n^*|\gt K\}\bigr]\gt \epsilon\Bigr]=0\quad\text{for every }\epsilon\gt 0 ,`$ holds if and only if $`n\mathrm{V}_*[\hat\theta^*]\xrightarrow{p}\phi^2`$. It asserts that this convergence implies that $`\sqrt{n}\sqrt{\mathrm{V}_*[\hat\theta^*]}\xrightarrow{p}\phi`$. It is broader than the corrected sentence, since $`\nu_n`$ is arbitrary, and the estimator, its limit distribution and the resampling do not appear. The cutoff $`K`$ of (M) runs over the natural numbers. The second statement asserts that $`n\mathrm{V}_*[\overline{X}^*]=\overline{X^2}-\overline{X}^2`$ for every sample of size $`n\ge1`$, where $`\mathrm{V}_*`$ is the exact variance over the $`n^n`$ equally likely samples drawn with replacement. The third asserts that $`n\mathrm{V}_*[\overline{X}^*]\xrightarrow{p}\mathrm{V}[X]`$ and $`\sqrt{n}\sqrt{\mathrm{V}_*[\overline{X}^*]}\xrightarrow{p}\sqrt{\mathrm{V}[X]}`$ for i.i.d. observations with finite variance $`\mathrm{V}[X]\gt 0`$. The fourth is the case of the sample median $`M_n`$. It assumes that $`F`$ has a density $`f`$ in a neighborhood of a point $`m`$, that $`f`$ is continuous at $`m`$ with $`f(m)\gt 0`$, that $`F(m)=1/2`$ with $`F(x)\lt 1/2`$ for $`x\lt m`$ and $`F(x)\gt 1/2`$ for $`x\gt m`$, and that $`\mathrm{E}|X|^{\eta}\lt \infty`$ for some $`\eta\gt 0`$. It asserts that $`n\mathrm{V}_*[M_n^*]\xrightarrow{p}1/(4f(m)^2)`$ and that $`\sqrt{n}\sqrt{\mathrm{V}_*[M_n^*]}\xrightarrow{p}1/(2f(m))`$. For even $`n`$ the median is the larger of the two middle observations. The fifth statement is the same assertion for odd $`n`$ and a density on all of $`\mathbb{R}`$, and it is narrower. The statements on the mean and the median assert the convergence directly and do not mention (M).

### P001-T63

Section 3.5.1, the sentence before Theorem 3.5.6, as corrected, with Theorems 3.5.3–3.5.6

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** p. 138 (PDF p. 156), sentence and Theorem 3.5.6; Definitions 3.5.1 and 3.5.2, p. 136 (PDF p. 154); Theorems 3.5.3–3.5.5, p. 137 (PDF p. 155)

Two Lean statements cover the sentence as corrected in P001-C03-N019 and the four theorems that it summarizes. Let $`(X_{i1},X_{i2},\ldots,X_{im})`$, for $`i=1,2,\ldots`$, be i.i.d. random vectors whose coordinates have finite second moments, with $`m\ge1`$, and let $`W_i`$ be the $`i`$th cluster average. The first statement asserts, for all $`l\ge1`$, that $`\overline{X}=\overline{W}`$, that $`\mathrm{E}[\overline{X}]=\mathrm{E}[W]`$ and that $`\mathrm{V}[\overline{X}]=\mathrm{V}[W]/l`$. It asserts that $`\mathrm{E}[W]=m^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathrm{E}[X_{ij}]`$, and that $`\overline{X}\xrightarrow{p}\mathrm{E}[W]`$ as $`l\to\infty`$ with $`m`$ fixed. The mean of the cluster averages is $`\mathrm{E}[W]`$, as in the corrected sentence, and the coordinates of a cluster need not have a common mean. The second statement is Theorem 3.5.5. It asserts that, for all $`l\ge1`$, $`\mathrm{V}[\overline{X}]=\frac{1}{lm^2}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{j'=1}^{m}\mathrm{Cov}[X_{ij},X_{ij'}] .`$ The $`l`$ clusters are the first $`l`$ terms of an infinite i.i.d. sequence of vectors. The first statement does not list among its conclusions that $`W_1,\ldots,W_l`$ are i.i.d. It omits the condition $`\mathrm{V}[X_{ij}]\gt 0`$ of Theorem 3.5.6, and it is broader than that theorem in this respect.

### P001-T64

Example 3.5.8 and step 1 of the block bootstrap, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** Example 3.5.8, pp. 140–141 (PDF pp. 158–159); block bootstrap steps, p. 141 (PDF p. 159); Theorem 3.5.5, p. 137 (PDF p. 155)

Three Lean statements cover the two corrections of P001-C03-E004. The first is the calculation of Example 3.5.8. It takes $`l,m\ge1`$, real numbers $`\sigma^2`$ and $`\rho`$, and an $`m\times m`$ array with entries $`\sigma^2`$ on the diagonal and $`\rho\sigma^2`$ off the diagonal. It asserts that the sum of the entries, divided by $`lm^2`$, equals $`\frac{\sigma^2}{lm}\bigl(1+\rho(m-1)\bigr) .`$ The condition on $`\rho`$ applies to the pairs $`j\ne j'`$ only, as in the corrected example. The statement is an algebraic identity. It is broader than the example, since the array need not be a covariance matrix and no condition is placed on the sign of $`\sigma^2`$ or on $`\rho`$. Together with Theorem 3.5.5 it yields the formula of the example for $`\mathrm{V}[\overline{X}]`$. The cases $`\rho=0`$ and $`\rho=1`$ are included and are not stated separately. The second statement concerns step 1 of the block bootstrap, with $`l`$ clusters drawn. Let $`D_1,\ldots,D_l`$ be independent draws, each uniform on the $`l`$ clusters of the sample. The statement asserts that the resample consists of the clusters $`D_1,\ldots,D_l`$, each with its $`m`$ observations intact, and that it has $`lm`$ observations. It asserts that $`\Pr[D_1=a_1,\ldots,D_l=a_l]=l^{-l}`$ for every choice of clusters $`a_1,\ldots,a_l`$. The third statement constructs the canonical probability model for the corrected block bootstrap: for $`l\gt 0`$ it uses the product of $`l`$ uniform laws on the $`l`$ cluster labels. It asserts that this is a probability measure with independent uniform draws, probability $`l^{-l}`$ for each complete index map, $`lm`$ resampled observation slots, and all $`m`$ observations of each selected cluster kept intact. The second and third statements also permit $`m=0`$, whereas the intended procedure uses $`m\gt 0`$. The third concerns the resampling law, count and cluster integrity, separately from the covariance calculation.

### P001-T65

Theorem 4.1.4, with the stacked residual system of its proof corrected, and Theorem 4.1.5

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** Theorem 4.1.4 and the stacked system, p. 148 (PDF p. 166); Theorem 4.1.5, p. 149 (PDF p. 167); Definition 4.1.2, pp. 146–147 (PDF pp. 164–165)

The Lean statement covers Theorems 4.1.4 and 4.1.5 for a realized sample. Let $`\mathbb{X}`$ be a real $`n\times(K+1)`$ matrix whose first column consists of ones, with $`\mathbb{X}^T\mathbb{X}`$ invertible, let $`\mathbf{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^n`$, and set $`\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}=(\mathbb{X}^T\mathbb{X})^{-1}\mathbb{X}^T\mathbf{Y}`$. The statement asserts that $`\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(Y_i-(\mathbb{X}\hat{\boldsymbol\beta})_i\bigr)^2\le\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(Y_i-(\mathbb{X}\mathbf{b})_i\bigr)^2\quad\text{for every }\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1} ,`$ with equality only for $`\mathbf{b}=\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}`$. It asserts further that the residual vector $`\mathbf{e}=\mathbf{Y}-\mathbb{X}\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}`$ satisfies $`\mathbb{X}^T\mathbf{e}=\mathbf{0}`$ and $`\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_i=0`$. The $`i`$th residual is $`Y_i`$ minus the $`i`$th entry of $`\mathbb{X}\mathbf{b}`$ for all $`i`$, so that the last row of the stacked system contains $`Y_n`$, as corrected in P001-C04-E005. The data are fixed numbers and not i.i.d. random vectors. Both theorems are algebraic, and they hold for each realized sample. Invertibility is stated as $`\det(\mathbb{X}^T\mathbb{X})\ne0`$, and the sum of squares is not divided by $`n`$, which does not change the minimizer. The three bullets of Theorem 4.1.5 are not listed one by one. The first, $`\overline{e}=0`$, is the last assertion divided by $`n`$, and the other two follow from $`\mathbb{X}^T\mathbf{e}=\mathbf{0}`$.

Two Lean statements cover the two equalities of the display as corrected in P001-C04-N005. The hypotheses of the first are a random vector $`\mathbf{X}`$ and a square-integrable random variable $`\varepsilon`$ such that $`\mathrm{E}[\varepsilon\mid\mathbf{X}]=0`$ and $`\mathrm{V}[\varepsilon\mid\mathbf{X}]=\sigma^2`$ for some $`\sigma^2\ge0`$, where the conditional variance is written as $`\mathrm{E}[(\varepsilon-0)^2\mid\mathbf{X}]`$. The products $`X_{[j]}X_{[k]}`$ and $`\varepsilon^2X_{[j]}X_{[k]}`$ are supposed integrable. The statement asserts that $`\mathrm{E}[\varepsilon^2\mid\mathbf{X}]=\sigma^2`$ and that $`\mathrm{E}\bigl[\varepsilon^2X_{[j]}X_{[k]}\bigr]=\sigma^2\,\mathrm{E}\bigl[X_{[j]}X_{[k]}\bigr]\qquad\text{for all }j\text{ and }k,`$ which is $`\mathrm{E}[\varepsilon^2\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{X}]=\sigma^2\mathrm{E}[\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{X}]`$ entry by entry. The second statement is the matrix identity $`A^{-1}(\sigma^2A)A^{-1}=\sigma^2A^{-1}`$, for every real square matrix $`A`$ with nonzero determinant and every real $`\sigma^2`$. With $`A=\mathrm{E}[\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{X}]`$ in both inverse factors, as in the corrected first line, the two statements imply the second and third lines of the display. Conditional expectations are functions of $`\mathbf{X}`$ that are determined up to sets of probability zero. Both statements are broader than the display. In the first, $`\mathbf{X}`$ is a random vector of any length whose first component need not be $`1`$. In the second, $`A`$ is any nonsingular matrix, whether or not it is a matrix of second moments. Neither statement asserts the approximation in the first line, which the book takes from Section 4.2.2.

### P001-T68

The Lean statement is the identity $`\mathrm{E}[Y\mid X=x]=g(x)`$ of P001-C04-E006 in a general form. Let $`X`$ and $`\varepsilon`$ be independent random variables such that $`\varepsilon`$ is integrable and $`\mathrm{E}[\varepsilon]=0`$, let $`g`$ be a measurable function such that $`g(X)`$ is integrable, and set $`Y=g(X)+\varepsilon`$. The statement asserts that the function $`g`$ is a CEF of $`Y`$ given $`X`$. In the Lean text, a function $`m`$ is a CEF of $`Y`$ given $`X`$ if it is measurable, $`Y`$ and $`m(X)`$ are integrable, and $`\mathrm{E}\bigl[Y\,\mathrm{I}(X\in A)\bigr]=\mathrm{E}\bigl[m(X)\,\mathrm{I}(X\in A)\bigr]\qquad\text{for every measurable set }A .`$ This condition determines $`m`$ up to a set of values of $`X`$ of probability zero. The statement therefore identifies $`g`$ as a version of the CEF, and it does not assert the equality at each point of $`\mathrm{Supp}[X]`$ separately. It is broader than the example, which has $`X\sim U(-10,10)`$, $`\varepsilon\sim N(0,25)`$ and the function $`g`$ drawn in the figure. The reference to Figure 4.3.5 and the labels of Figure 4.4.4, which the same finding corrects, have no Lean statement.

### P001-T70

Definition 4.3.9 (Polynomial Least Squares Sieve Estimator), the least squares criterion, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** p. 169 (PDF p. 187), the display that defines $`\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}`$; the definition begins on p. 168 (PDF p. 186)

Two Lean statements concern the criterion of P001-C04-E003, one as printed and one as corrected. The first asserts that, for every $`n\ge1`$, all real $`y_1,\ldots,y_n`$ and every real $`B`$, there exists a real $`b_0`$ such that $`\sum_{i=1}^n(y_i-b_0)\lt B`$. This is the printed criterion, without the square, at the coefficient vectors with $`b_1=\cdots=b_J=0`$. Since the constants are among the polynomials of every degree $`J\ge0`$, the printed criterion is unbounded below and has no minimizer. The second statement concerns the corrected criterion for real data $`(x_i,y_i)_{i=1}^n`$ and a degree $`J\ge0`$. Let $`P`$ be the $`n\times(J+1)`$ matrix with entries $`P_{ik}=x_i^k`$ for $`k=0,\ldots,J`$, and suppose that $`P^TP`$ is invertible. The statement asserts that the sum of squared residuals of the regression of $`\mathbf{y}`$ on $`P`$ equals $`\sum_{i=1}^n\bigl(y_i-\sum_{k=0}^Jb_kx_i^k\bigr)^2`$ for every $`\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{J+1}`$, and that $`(P^TP)^{-1}P^T\mathbf{y}`$ is its unique minimizer. Both statements are about one sample of fixed size, and neither contains the i.i.d. sampling of the definition or its conditions $`J_n\to\infty`$ and $`J_n/n\to0`$, which are the subject of P001-C04-E001.

### P001-T71

Definition 4.3.10 (Lasso Estimator), as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** pp. 169–170 (PDF pp. 187–188)

Three Lean statements concern the criterion of the definition as corrected in P001-C04-E004, $`\mathbf{b}\mapsto\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n(Y_i-\mathbf{X}_i\mathbf{b})^2+\lambda\sum_{k=1}^K|b_k|,\qquad\mathbf{b}=(b_0,b_1,\ldots,b_K)^T\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1},`$ for a real $`n\times(K+1)`$ matrix with rows $`\mathbf{X}_i`$ and real numbers $`Y_1,\ldots,Y_n`$. The first asserts that at $`\lambda=0`$ the criterion equals $`1/n`$ times the OLS sum of squared residuals for every $`\mathbf{b}`$, and that the penalty vanishes at $`(1,0,\ldots,0)`$. The second asserts that for $`n\ge1`$ and $`\lambda\ge0`$ the criterion is a convex function on $`\mathbb{R}^{K+1}`$. The third asserts that the penalty $`\mathbf{b}\mapsto\sum_{k=1}^K|b_k|`$ is nonnegative, absolutely homogeneous and subadditive, and that it vanishes at $`(1,0,\ldots,0)`$, a vector whose $`\ell^1`$ norm is $`1`$. The penalty is then a seminorm on $`\mathbb{R}^{K+1}`$ and is not the $`\ell^1`$ norm of $`\mathbf{b}`$. The first statement is an equality of criteria and does not mention minimizers. The sentence on p. 170 that the lasso with $`\lambda=0`$ is OLS regression is covered only in that equal criteria have the same minimizers. The statements are broader than the definition in that the matrix is arbitrary and its first column need not consist of ones. The i.i.d. sampling and the remark on consistency on p. 170 are not represented.

### P001-T72

Definition 5.1.4 (Classical Linear Model), the parameter space as corrected, with Theorem 5.1.5 (CEF of the Classical Linear Model)

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** Definition 5.1.4, pp. 180–181 (PDF pp. 198–199); Theorem 5.1.5, p. 181 (PDF p. 199)

The Lean statement is the density calculation behind Theorem 5.1.5, with the condition $`\sigma\gt 0`$ of P001-C05-E009 as a hypothesis. For all $`\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1}`$ and every $`\sigma\gt 0`$, it asserts that $`\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi\bigl(y;(\mathbf{x}\boldsymbol\beta,\sigma^2)\bigr)\,dy=1,\qquad\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}y\,\phi\bigl(y;(\mathbf{x}\boldsymbol\beta,\sigma^2)\bigr)\,dy=\mathbf{x}\boldsymbol\beta,`$ where $`\phi(y;(\mu,\sigma^2))=e^{-(y-\mu)^2/(2\sigma^2)}/(\sigma\sqrt{2\pi})`$ and $`\mathbf{x}\boldsymbol\beta=\sum_{k=0}^Kx_k\beta_k`$. The statement has no random variables. It does not contain a joint distribution of $`(Y,\mathbf{X})`$, the hypothesis that the model is true, or a conclusion about the CEF. It is the computation in the proof of Theorem 5.1.5 at one fixed $`\mathbf{x}`$, and not the theorem itself. The vector $`\mathbf{x}`$ is arbitrary. Its first component need not be $`1`$, and it need not belong to $`\mathrm{Supp}[\mathbf{X}]`$. The statement does not assert that the density fails to exist at $`\sigma=0`$. In Lean a quotient with denominator zero is assigned $`0`$, so the displayed formula at $`\sigma=0`$ denotes the zero function. The correction of the parameter space enters only through the hypothesis $`\sigma\gt 0`$.

### P001-T73

A counterexample to the sentence in Section 5.1.1 that attributes independence from $`\mathbf{X}`$ to deviations from the CEF, as printed

**Kind:** `counterexample`

**Location:** p. 181 (PDF p. 199), paragraph after the display $`Y=\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\beta+\varepsilon`$; Theorem 2.2.19, p. 74 (PDF p. 92)

The Lean statement formalizes the counterexample of P001-C05-E004. The pair $`(X,Y)`$ takes the values $`(0,1)`$, $`(0,3)`$, $`(1,0)`$ and $`(1,6)`$ with probability $`1/4`$ each, and $`\varepsilon=Y-(2+X)`$. The statement asserts that $`\mathrm{E}[Y\mid X=0]=2`$ and $`\mathrm{E}[Y\mid X=1]=3`$, so that the CEF is $`2+x`$ and $`\varepsilon`$ is the deviation from the CEF. It asserts that $`\mathrm{E}[\varepsilon]=0`$ and $`\mathrm{Cov}[X,\varepsilon]=0`$, and that $`\Pr\bigl[X=0,\ |\varepsilon|=1\bigr]=\frac12,\qquad\Pr[X=0]\,\Pr\bigl[|\varepsilon|=1\bigr]=\frac14 .`$ Since the two numbers differ, $`\varepsilon`$ and $`X`$ are not independent. The distribution is a list of four points with weight $`1/4`$ each, and probabilities, expectations and conditional expectations are exact finite sums and quotients of such sums. Of the five properties of Theorem 2.2.19, the statement contains the mean zero and the zero covariance with $`X`$, and the conditional mean zero follows from the two conditional expectations. It does not list the zero covariance with every function $`g(X)`$ or the two properties of the variance. The example lies outside the classical linear model, and the statement does not contradict the independence of the errors from $`\mathbf{X}`$ in that model.

### P001-T74

Section 5.1.1, the comparison of the errors of the classical linear model with deviations from the CEF, as corrected, with Theorem 2.2.19 (Properties of Deviations from the CEF)

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** p. 181 (PDF p. 199), paragraph after the display $`Y=\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\beta+\varepsilon`$; Theorem 2.2.19, p. 74 (PDF p. 92), proof on pp. 74–75 (PDF pp. 92–93)

Three Lean statements cover the sentence as corrected in P001-C05-E004: two contain Theorem 2.2.19, and one contains the fact about the classical linear model. Let $`Y`$ be square-integrable, let $`m`$ be a CEF of $`Y`$ given $`X`$ such that $`m(X)`$ is square-integrable, and set $`\varepsilon=Y-m(X)`$. The first statement asserts that $`\mathrm{E}[\varepsilon\mid X]=0`$, that $`\mathrm{E}[\varepsilon]=0`$, and that $`\mathrm{Cov}[g(X),\varepsilon]=0`$ for every measurable $`g`$ such that $`g(X)`$ is square-integrable. The second takes a function $`v`$ that is a CEF of $`(Y-m(X))^2`$ given $`X`$, so that $`v(X)`$ is $`\mathrm{V}[Y\mid X]`$, and asserts that $`\mathrm{V}[\varepsilon]=\mathrm{E}[v(X)]`$. Its other conclusion, that $`v(X)`$ is also $`\mathrm{V}[\varepsilon\mid X]`$, repeats this hypothesis once the conditional mean of $`\varepsilon`$ is zero. The third statement supposes that $`\sigma\gt 0`$ and that $`(X,Y)`$ has the joint distribution of $`(X,m(X)+Z)`$, where $`Z\sim N(0,\sigma^2)`$ is independent of $`X`$. It asserts that $`Y-m(X)\sim N(0,\sigma^2)`$ and that $`Y-m(X)`$ is independent of $`X`$. In the third statement $`m`$ is any measurable function, and the choice $`m(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{x}\boldsymbol\beta`$ gives the model of Definition 5.1.4. This statement is then broader than the sentence. The first two statements suppose that second moments are finite, which is slightly more than the convention of the book that the expectations in a statement exist. In all three, $`X`$ takes values in an arbitrary measurable space, and conditional expectations are versions determined up to sets of probability zero.

### P001-T76

Example 5.2.4 (Maximum Likelihood Estimation of the Classical Linear Model), the maximization display, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** pp. 190–191 (PDF pp. 208–209)

The Lean statement is the equivalence in the display as corrected in part (b) of P001-C05-E010, with the coefficient vector in $`\mathbb{R}^{K+1}`$ and the scale $`s\gt 0`$ held fixed. For a real $`n\times(K+1)`$ matrix $`\mathbb{X}`$ with rows $`\mathbf{X}_i`$ and real numbers $`Y_1,\ldots,Y_n`$, the log-likelihood is $`\log\mathcal{L}\bigl((\mathbf{b},s)\mid\mathbf{Y},\mathbb{X}\bigr)=-n\log s-\frac n2\log(2\pi)-\frac{1}{2s^2}\sum_{i=1}^n(Y_i-\mathbf{X}_i\mathbf{b})^2 .`$ The statement asserts that, for every $`s\gt 0`$, a vector $`\mathbf{b}^*`$ maximizes $`\mathbf{b}\mapsto\log\mathcal{L}((\mathbf{b},s)\mid\mathbf{Y},\mathbb{X})`$ over $`\mathbb{R}^{K+1}`$ if and only if it minimizes $`\sum_{i=1}^n(Y_i-\mathbf{X}_i\mathbf{b})^2`$ over $`\mathbb{R}^{K+1}`$. The log-likelihood is entered in this closed form, which is the sum over $`i`$ of the logarithms of the normal densities of the example. The statement is broader than the example in that the matrix is arbitrary. It may lack a column of ones, and it may be rank deficient. It does not assert that a maximizer exists or is unique, and it does not contain the formula $`(\mathbb{X}^T\mathbb{X})^{-1}\mathbb{X}^T\mathbf{Y}`$, which requires $`\mathbb{X}^T\mathbb{X}`$ to be invertible. The model $`Y=\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\beta+\varepsilon`$ and the i.i.d. sampling are not represented, since the equivalence holds for each sample.

Footnote 5, as corrected

The Lean statement expresses the likelihood as a random function, as in P001-C05-E006. It asserts that there exists a map $`\mathcal{L}`$ from $`\Omega`$ to the functions $`L:\boldsymbol\Theta\to[0,\infty)`$ such that $`\mathcal{L}(\omega)(\mathbf{t})=g\bigl(Y(\omega),\mathbf{X}(\omega);\mathbf{t}\bigr)\qquad\text{for every }\omega\in\Omega\text{ and every }\mathbf{t}\in\boldsymbol\Theta .`$ The domain of each realized likelihood is $`\boldsymbol\Theta`$, of any dimension $`\tau`$, and its values are nonnegative real numbers with no upper bound. The statement is broader than the footnote in that only the nonnegativity of $`g`$ is supposed. The function $`g(\cdot,\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t})`$ need not be a PMF or a PDF, and $`Y`$ and $`\mathbf{X}`$ are arbitrary functions on $`\Omega`$, with no condition of measurability. It concerns the likelihood of one observation, as in the first display of Definition 5.2.1, and not the product over the sample. It does not contain an example of a likelihood that exceeds one.

### P001-T78

Footnote 10 (Example 5.2.4), as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** p. 191 (PDF p. 209), footnote 10

The Lean statement is the maximization behind the footnote as corrected in P001-C05-E005. Let $`n\ge1`$, let $`\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}`$ minimize $`\sum_{i=1}^n(Y_i-\mathbf{X}_i\mathbf{b})^2`$ over $`\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1}`$, and suppose that $`Q=\sum_{i=1}^n(Y_i-\mathbf{X}_i\hat{\boldsymbol\beta})^2\gt 0`$. The statement asserts that $`\sqrt{Q/n}\gt 0`$, that $`(\sqrt{Q/n})^2=Q/n`$, and that $`\log\mathcal{L}\bigl((\mathbf{b},s)\mid\mathbf{Y},\mathbb{X}\bigr)\le\log\mathcal{L}\bigl((\hat{\boldsymbol\beta},\sqrt{Q/n})\mid\mathbf{Y},\mathbb{X}\bigr)\qquad\text{for every }\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{K+1}\text{ and every }s\gt 0 .`$ The maximum over the coefficients and the scale together is then attained at $`s=\sqrt{Q/n}`$, so that $`Q/n`$ is the ML estimator of $`\sigma^2`$ and $`\sqrt{Q/n}`$ is that of $`\sigma`$. The log-likelihood is $`-n\log s-(n/2)\log(2\pi)-(2s^2)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^n(Y_i-\mathbf{X}_i\mathbf{b})^2`$, the closed form of the expression in Example 5.2.4. The hypothesis $`Q\gt 0`$ is that of the corrected footnote, and the statement does not assert that the maximizer is unique. The matrix of regressors is arbitrary and need not have a column of ones. The statement does not identify $`Q/n`$ with the plug-in sample variance of the residuals, since this requires an intercept, which makes the mean of the residuals zero.

Definition 5.2.10 (Maximum Likelihood Plug-In Estimator), as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** pp. 197–198 (PDF pp. 215–216)

The Lean statement concerns $`\hat F_{ML}`$ with the indicator of P001-C05-E007. Let $`n\ge1`$, let $`\mathbf{X}_1,\ldots,\mathbf{X}_n\in\mathbb{R}^K`$, and let $`\nu_1,\ldots,\nu_n`$ be probability distributions on $`\mathbb{R}`$. Here $`\nu_i`$ stands for the fitted conditional distribution of the outcome given $`\mathbf{X}_i`$, with PMF or PDF $`g(\cdot,\mathbf{X}_i;\hat{\boldsymbol\theta}_{ML})`$. The statement concerns the distribution on $`\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^K`$ that gives weight $`1/n`$ to each product of $`\nu_i`$ with the point mass at $`\mathbf{X}_i`$. It asserts that this is a probability distribution and that its joint CDF is $`\hat F_{ML}(y,\mathbf{x})=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\mathrm{I}\bigl(X_{[1]i}\le x_{[1]},X_{[2]i}\le x_{[2]},\ldots,X_{[K]i}\le x_{[K]}\bigr)\,\nu_i\bigl((-\infty,y]\bigr)`$ for every $`y\in\mathbb{R}`$ and every $`\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^K`$. The factor $`\nu_i((-\infty,y])`$ is $`\sum_{v\le y}g(v,\mathbf{X}_i;\hat{\boldsymbol\theta}_{ML})`$ in the PMF case and $`\int_{-\infty}^yg(v,\mathbf{X}_i;\hat{\boldsymbol\theta}_{ML})\,dv`$ in the PDF case, so that one statement covers both displays. Each threshold $`x_{[k]}`$ is a coordinate of the point of evaluation and each $`X_{[k]i}`$ carries the index of the observation, as in the corrected indicator. The statement is broader than the definition in that the $`\nu_i`$ are arbitrary probability distributions and need not come from one parametric model or from one estimate $`\hat{\boldsymbol\theta}_{ML}`$. It does not contain the estimator $`\hat\psi_{ML}=T(\hat F_{ML})`$, or the population identity that the book obtains from the Law of Iterated Expectations and from which the definition follows.

### P001-T80

Example 6.1.2 (Bounding the Expected Value), as corrected, with Theorem 6.1.3 (Sharp Bounds for the Expected Value)

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** Example 6.1.2, pp. 209–210 (PDF pp. 227–228); Theorem 6.1.3, p. 210 (PDF p. 228), proof on p. 211 (PDF p. 229)

Two Lean statements cover the example and the theorem. The first takes the six observations of the example, $`Y_i^*=1,-99,1,0,1,-99`$ and $`R_i=1,0,1,1,1,0`$. It asserts that $`\frac16\sum_{i=1}^6\bigl(Y_i^*R_i+0\cdot(1-R_i)\bigr)=\frac12,\qquad\frac16\sum_{i=1}^6\bigl(Y_i^*R_i+1\cdot(1-R_i)\bigr)=\frac56 .`$ The upper bound $`5/6`$ is obtained with the highest possible value in place of the missing outcomes, as in the correction of P001-C06-E001. The second statement is Theorem 6.1.3 together with the sharpness that the book asserts in prose, for random variables $`Y`$ and $`R`$ with $`Y\in[a,b]`$ and $`R\in\{0,1\}`$ and for $`Y^*=YR+(-99)(1-R)`$. With $`L=\mathrm{E}[Y^*R+a(1-R)]`$ and $`U=\mathrm{E}[Y^*R+b(1-R)]`$, it asserts that $`L\le\mathrm{E}[Y]\le U`$. It asserts further that for every $`c\in[L,U]`$ there exists a random variable $`Y'`$ with values in $`[a,b]`$ such that $`\mathrm{E}[Y']=c`$ and $`Y'R+(-99)(1-R)=Y^*`$. The endpoints are written as expectations of the imputed variables, and they equal $`\mathrm{E}[Y^*\mid R=1]\Pr[R=1]+a\Pr[R=0]`$ and the same expression with $`b`$ whenever $`\Pr[R=1]\gt 0`$. The bounds on $`Y`$ and the values of $`R`$ are required at every sample point, where the theorem states them through the supports. The second statement is broader than the theorem in that $`R`$ need not take both values. The variable $`Y'`$ is defined on the same probability space and has the same observed data at every sample point, which is stronger than the same joint distribution of $`(Y^*,R)`$.

Section 6.2.4, the range of the response propensity function and the description of impossible estimates, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** p. 225 (PDF p. 243), Section 6.2.4, first paragraph, last sentence on the page

Three Lean statements concern the sentence as corrected in P001-C06-E002. The main statement takes a random vector $`\mathbf{X}`$, a response indicator $`R`$ with values in $`\{0,1\}`$, and a conditional distribution of $`R`$ given $`\mathbf{X}`$. This is a family $`\kappa_{\mathbf{x}}`$ of probability distributions on $`\{0,1\}`$ that is measurable in $`\mathbf{x}`$ and satisfies $`\Pr[\mathbf{X}\in A,R\in B]=\mathrm{E}[\kappa_{\mathbf{X}}(B)\,\mathrm{I}(\mathbf{X}\in A)]`$ for every measurable set $`A`$ and every $`B\subseteq\{0,1\}`$. With $`p_R(\mathbf{x})=\kappa_{\mathbf{x}}(\{1\})`$, the statement asserts that for every $`\mathbf{x}`$ the mean of $`\kappa_{\mathbf{x}}`$ equals $`p_R(\mathbf{x})`$ and $`p_R(\mathbf{x})\in[0,1]`$, and that $`p_R`$ is a CEF of $`R`$ given $`\mathbf{X}`$. It asserts also that a real number $`q`$ lies outside $`[0,1]`$ if and only if $`q\gt 1`$ or $`q\lt 0`$, which is the corrected description of an impossible estimate. The second statement is the range alone: for any family of probability distributions on $`\{0,1\}`$, the mass at $`1`$ lies in $`[0,1]`$. The third statement constructs such a conditional distribution and a measurable propensity version $`p_R`$ from measurable $`\mathbf{X}`$ and a measurable binary $`R`$ with $`0\lt \Pr(R=1)\lt 1`$. The kernel satisfies the preceding conditional-law identity, and for every $`\mathbf{x}`$ its mass at $`1`$ and its mean equal $`p_R(\mathbf{x})\in[0,1]`$. The function $`p_R`$ is a CEF of $`R`$ given $`\mathbf{X}`$, and every other CEF version $`m`$ satisfies $`m(\mathbf{X})=p_R(\mathbf{X})`$ almost surely. This construction supplies a globally bounded version without identifying arbitrary versions pointwise at covariate values of probability zero. It includes the same description of the numbers outside $`[0,1]`$. In this existence statement $`R`$ takes both values with positive probability. In the main statement the conditional distribution is a hypothesis and is not constructed. That statement is broader than the sentence in three respects: the range holds for every $`\mathbf{x}`$ and not only on $`\mathrm{Supp}[\mathbf{X}_i]`$, $`R`$ need not take both values, and $`\mathbf{X}`$ may take values in any measurable space.

### P001-T83

Theorem 6.2.6 (Reweighting under MAR), as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** p. 226 (PDF p. 244); proof on pp. 226–227 (PDF pp. 244–245)

The Lean statement is the theorem with the hypothesis of P001-C06-E003, that $`Y_i`$ is MAR conditional on $`\mathbf{X}_i`$. Its hypotheses are an integrable random variable $`Y`$, a random variable $`R`$ with values in $`\{0,1\}`$, a random vector $`\mathbf{X}`$, and a function $`p_R`$ that is a CEF of $`R`$ given $`\mathbf{X}`$. Both parts of Definition 6.1.8 are supposed: $`Y\perp\!\!\!\perp R\mid\mathbf{X}`$, and there exists $`\epsilon\gt 0`$ such that $`p_R(\mathbf{X})\gt \epsilon`$. With $`Y^*=YR+(-99)(1-R)`$, the statement asserts that $`\mathrm{E}[Y]=\mathrm{E}\left[\frac{Y^*R}{p_R(\mathbf{X})}\right].`$ Conditional independence is written with conditional expectations given $`\mathbf{X}`$: for all measurable sets $`A`$ and $`B`$, the conditional probability of $`\{Y\in A,R\in B\}`$ is almost surely the product of those of $`\{Y\in A\}`$ and $`\{R\in B\}`$. This replaces the factorization of conditional PMFs or PDFs in footnote 6 on p. 215, and it covers discrete and continuous variables together. The conditional expectations are versions determined up to sets of probability zero, and the lower bound on $`p_R(\mathbf{X})`$ and the values of $`R`$ are required at every sample point. The statement is broader than the theorem in that $`R`$ need not take both values and $`\mathbf{X}`$ may take values in any measurable space. It concerns one unit, and i.i.d. sampling plays no part in it.

### P001-T84

A counterexample to the sentence in Section 6.2.5 on the equivalence of the IPW estimator and the plug-in estimator of Section 6.2.1, as printed

**Kind:** `counterexample`

**Location:** p. 228 (PDF p. 246), first paragraph; Definition 6.2.7, p. 227 (PDF p. 245); the plug-in estimator of Section 6.2.1, p. 219 (PDF p. 237); Section 6.2.4, pp. 225–226 (PDF pp. 243–244)

The Lean statements formalize the counterexample of P001-C06-E004, a sample of twelve units with $`\mathbf{X}_i=(1,x_i)`$. The sample enters through its three cells $`x=-1,0,1`$, with $`n_x=4`$ units in each, $`r_x=2,3,2`$ respondents, and sums $`S_x=3/4,3/2,3/4`$ of the outcomes of the respondents. The main statement, which appears twice in the Lean text, has four parts. First, the logit log-likelihood $`\ell(a,b)=\sum_x\Bigl(r_x\log h(a+bx)+(n_x-r_x)\log\bigl(1-h(a+bx)\bigr)\Bigr),\qquad h(t)=\frac{e^t}{1+e^t},`$ satisfies $`\ell(a,b)\le\ell(\log(7/5),0)`$ for all real $`a`$ and $`b`$, with equality only at $`(a,b)=(\log(7/5),0)`$. Second, every cell has $`0\lt r_x\lt n_x`$, and the fitted probability $`h(\log(7/5))`$ is $`7/12`$ in each cell. Third, the IPW estimate $`12^{-1}\sum_xS_x/(7/12)`$ equals $`3/7`$, and the plug-in estimate $`12^{-1}\sum_xn_xS_x/r_x`$ of Section 6.2.1 equals $`5/12`$. Fourth, $`3/7\ne5/12`$. The statements use the cell counts and sums only. The twelve units, the value $`-99`$ of their missing outcomes, a population and the MAR assumption do not appear.

### P001-T85

Section 6.2.5, the equivalence of the IPW estimator and the plug-in estimator of Section 6.2.1, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** p. 228 (PDF p. 246), first paragraph

Three Lean statements contain the algebra of the correction in P001-C06-E004. They are written for a finite set of covariate cells $`\mathbf{x}`$ with sizes $`n_{\mathbf{x}}\gt 0`$, numbers of respondents $`r_{\mathbf{x}}\gt 0`$, sums $`S_{\mathbf{x}}`$ of the outcomes of the respondents, and fitted probabilities $`q_{\mathbf{x}}=\hat p_R(\mathbf{x})`$. The first asserts that $`\sum_{\mathbf{x}}\frac{S_{\mathbf{x}}}{r_{\mathbf{x}}/n_{\mathbf{x}}}=\sum_{\mathbf{x}}\frac{n_{\mathbf{x}}S_{\mathbf{x}}}{r_{\mathbf{x}}},`$ which is the equality of the two estimators, before division by $`n`$, when each $`q_{\mathbf{x}}`$ is the observed fraction of respondents $`r_{\mathbf{x}}/n_{\mathbf{x}}`$. The second asserts that, for fixed $`n_{\mathbf{x}}`$, $`r_{\mathbf{x}}`$ and $`q_{\mathbf{x}}\gt 0`$, the equality $`\sum_{\mathbf{x}}S_{\mathbf{x}}/q_{\mathbf{x}}=\sum_{\mathbf{x}}n_{\mathbf{x}}S_{\mathbf{x}}/r_{\mathbf{x}}`$ holds for every choice of the sums $`S_{\mathbf{x}}`$ if and only if $`q_{\mathbf{x}}=r_{\mathbf{x}}/n_{\mathbf{x}}`$ in every cell. The third supposes that $`\sum_{\mathbf{x}}n_{\mathbf{x}}=n\gt 0`$, and it asserts that the difference of the two estimators is $`n^{-1}\sum_{\mathbf{x}}S_{\mathbf{x}}(1/q_{\mathbf{x}}-n_{\mathbf{x}}/r_{\mathbf{x}})`$ and that $`n^{-1}\sum_{\mathbf{x}}r_{\mathbf{x}}/(r_{\mathbf{x}}/n_{\mathbf{x}})=1`$. The last sum is the denominator of the stabilized IPW estimator of Definition 6.2.8 when $`q_{\mathbf{x}}=r_{\mathbf{x}}/n_{\mathbf{x}}`$. The statements start from the sums over cells, and the step to them from the sums over units in Definition 6.2.7 and in Section 6.2.1 is not formalized. They are broader than the correction in that $`n_{\mathbf{x}}`$ and $`r_{\mathbf{x}}`$ are positive real numbers, which need not be counts with $`r_{\mathbf{x}}\le n_{\mathbf{x}}`$. The second statement allows $`q_{\mathbf{x}}\gt 1`$; the third also allows $`q_{\mathbf{x}}\le0`$, with division by zero read as $`0`$.

### P001-T87

Section 6.3, the conditional expectation in the working models of the two regressions, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** p. 233 (PDF p. 251), first paragraph below Table 6.3.1

Two Lean statements contain what distinguishes the printed conditional expectation $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i^*\mid R_i,\mathbf{X}_i]`$ from the one that P001-C06-E006 restores, $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i^*\mid R_i=1,\mathbf{X}_i]`$. Both concern the stable outcomes model $`Y_i^*=Y_iR_i+(-99)(1-R_i)`$ with $`R_i\in\{0,1\}`$ and $`Y_i`$ integrable. In the first, $`m`$ is a version of the CEF of $`Y_i`$ given $`\mathbf{X}_i`$ among respondents. The statement asserts that $`m`$ is also a version of $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i^*\mid R_i=1,\mathbf{X}_i]`$, and that $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i^*\mid R_i,\mathbf{X}_i]`$ equals $`m(\mathbf{X}_i)`$ where $`R_i=1`$ and $`-99`$ where $`R_i=0`$. The second assumes that $`Y_i`$ is missing at random conditional on $`\mathbf{X}_i`$, that is, $`Y_i\mathbin{\perp\!\!\!\perp}R_i\mid\mathbf{X}_i`$ and $`\Pr[R_i=1\mid\mathbf{X}_i]\gt \epsilon`$ for some $`\epsilon\gt 0`$. It asserts that a version $`m`$ of $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i^*\mid R_i=1,\mathbf{X}_i]`$ such that $`m(\mathbf{X}_i)`$ is integrable is a version of $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i\mid\mathbf{X}_i]`$, which is Theorem 6.1.10. A CEF is represented by a measurable function $`m`$, with $`Y_i`$ and $`m(\mathbf{X}_i)`$ integrable, such that $`\int_{\{\mathbf{X}_i\in A\}}Y_i\,dP=\int_{\{\mathbf{X}_i\in A\}}m(\mathbf{X}_i)\,dP`$ for every measurable set $`A`$, and conditioning on $`R_i=1`$ restricts both the integrability requirements and the integrals to that event. The statements are slightly broader than Definition 6.1.8 in that $`R_i`$ need not take both values with positive probability. The approximations by $`\beta_0+\mathbf{X}_i\boldsymbol\beta`$ and by the polynomial specification are working assumptions of the application, and they have no Lean statement.

### P001-T88

Theorem 7.1.8 (Estimating Sharp Bounds for the ATE), with two symbols in its proof corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** Theorem 7.1.8 and the first part of its proof: p. 243 (PDF p. 261); rest of the proof: p. 244 (PDF p. 262)

The main Lean statement is Theorem 7.1.8 with the two sample means written as in P001-C07-E001. Let $`D_i\in\{0,1\}`$ with $`0\lt \Pr[D_i=1]\lt 1`$, let $`a\le b`$, let $`Y_i(0)`$ and $`Y_i(1)`$ lie in $`[a,b]`$ with probability one, and let the observed pairs $`(Y_i,D_i)`$ be i.i.d. Set $`\tau_i^L=Y_iD_i+a(1-D_i)-Y_i(1-D_i)-bD_i`$ and $`\tau_i^U=Y_iD_i+b(1-D_i)-Y_i(1-D_i)-aD_i`$. The statement asserts that, for all $`n\ge1`$, $`\mathrm{E}\bigl[\overline{\tau_i^L}\bigr]=\mathrm{E}[\tau_i^L],\qquad \mathrm{E}\bigl[\overline{\tau_i^U}\bigr]=\mathrm{E}[\tau_i^U],`$ and that $`\overline{\tau_i^L}`$ and $`\overline{\tau_i^U}`$ converge in probability to $`\mathrm{E}[\tau_i^L]`$ and $`\mathrm{E}[\tau_i^U]`$. It also makes explicit what the lower and the upper bound for $`\mathrm{E}[\tau_i]`$ are. It asserts that $`\mathrm{E}[\tau_i^L]\le\mathrm{E}[\tau_i]\le\mathrm{E}[\tau_i^U]`$, that $`\mathrm{E}[\tau_i^L]`$ and $`\mathrm{E}[\tau_i^U]`$ equal the two expressions of Theorem 7.1.7, and that each of them is the ATE of some potential outcomes in $`[a,b]`$ that give the same observed outcomes. The supporting statement also assumes bounded potential outcomes, identifies the sharp endpoints and constructs potential outcomes attaining each endpoint, in addition to proving unbiasedness and convergence of the two sample means. Its treatment-binarity hypothesis is pointwise. The sample consists of the first $`n`$ terms of an infinite i.i.d. sequence of observed pairs, and convergence in probability is that of Definition 3.2.6. As in the printed theorem, only the observed pairs are assumed i.i.d., and the population quantities are those of the first unit.

### P001-T89

Example 7.1.17 (Conditioning on Post-Treatment Variables), with the distribution of observables corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** pp. 253–254 (PDF pp. 271–272)

Two Lean statements cover the example as corrected in P001-C07-E002. The example is represented by its six points $`(Y_i(0),Y_i(1),X_i(1),X_i(0),D_i)`$, each with probability $`1/6`$, and probabilities and conditional means are exact weighted sums over these points. The first statement asserts that $`\Pr[(Y_i,X_i,D_i)=(1,1,1)]=1/3`$ and $`\Pr[X_i=0]=1/2`$, that the conditional means $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i\mid D_i=d,X_i=x]`$ are $`0`$, $`1`$, $`1/2`$ and $`1`$ for $`(d,x)=(1,0)`$, $`(1,1)`$, $`(0,0)`$ and $`(0,1)`$, and that the adjusted contrast formed from these values is $`\left(0\cdot\frac{1}{2}+1\cdot\frac{1}{2}\right)-\left(\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{2}+1\cdot\frac{1}{2}\right)=-\frac{1}{4} .`$ The second statement asserts that $`(Y_i(0),Y_i(1),X_i(1),X_i(0))\mathbin{\perp\!\!\!\perp}D_i`$, that $`\Pr[D_i=1]=1/2`$, that $`X_i=X_i(1)D_i+X_i(0)(1-D_i)`$ at every point, and that $`\mathrm{E}[\tau_i]=0`$. Its last assertion is that $`(Y_i(0),Y_i(1))`$ and $`D_i`$ are not independent conditional on $`X_i`$. Independence is the factorization of the probabilities of all cells, and conditional independence is the same factorization within each value of $`X_i`$. The two potential outcomes are one and the same variable in the Lean statements, so that $`Y_i(1)=Y_i(0)`$ holds by construction. The first statement contains the corrected cell and the quantities that enter the contrast, and it does not list the other four cells of the distribution of observables.

### P001-T90

A counterexample to the claim of Section 7.1.8 that, under ignorability, $`\mathrm{E}[\tau_i(d,d')]`$ is point identified for any $`d,d'\in\mathrm{Supp}[D_i]`$, for a continuous treatment, as printed

**Kind:** `counterexample`

**Location:** p. 255 (PDF p. 273), first paragraph; Definition 7.1.18 and the generalized conditional independence assumption: p. 254 (PDF p. 272); Definition 6.0.1: p. 207 (PDF p. 225)

The Lean statement formalizes the example of P001-C07-N004. The treatment $`D_i`$ is uniform on $`[0,1]`$, the covariate is the constant $`\mathbf{X}_i=0`$, and there are two models for the potential outcomes, $`Y_A(d)=0`$ for every $`d`$ and $`Y_B(d)=\mathbf{1}\{d=1/2\}`$. In each model the observed outcome is the potential outcome at $`d=D_i`$. The statement asserts that the distribution of $`(Y_i,D_i,\mathbf{X}_i)`$ is the same in the two models. Formally, the image of Lebesgue measure on $`[0,1]`$ under $`d\mapsto(0,d,0)`$ equals its image under $`d\mapsto(\mathbf{1}\{d=1/2\},d,0)`$. It asserts further that $`Y_B(1/2)-Y_B(0)=1`$ and that $`0\ne1`$, where $`0`$ is the value of $`Y_A(1/2)-Y_A(0)`$. Both potential outcome functions are nonrandom and therefore independent of $`D_i`$, and the two differences are the values of $`\mathrm{E}[\tau_i(0,1/2)]`$ in the two models. At this point the Lean statement is narrower than the counterexample. It contains the equality of the two distributions and the two values, and it does not state the conditional independence assumption, the expectation $`\mathrm{E}[\tau_i(0,1/2)]`$ or Definition 6.0.1 as formal objects.

### P001-T91

Section 7.1.8, the identification of $`\mathrm{E}[\tau_i(d,d')]`$ for multivalued and continuous treatments, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** p. 255 (PDF p. 273), first paragraph

Two Lean statements cover the two cases of the correction in P001-C07-N004. In the two identification statements $`\mathbf{X}_i`$ takes countably many values, $`Y_i`$ is the potential outcome $`Y_i(t)`$ at $`t=D_i`$, all outcomes are integrable, and $`\mathbf{x}`$ ranges over the values such that $`\Pr[\mathbf{X}_i=\mathbf{x}]\gt 0`$. One statement treats a treatment level $`d`$ that has positive probability. It assumes that $`(Y_i(t))_{t}\mathbin{\perp\!\!\!\perp}D_i\mid\mathbf{X}_i`$ and that $`\Pr[\mathbf{X}_i=\mathbf{x},D_i=d]\gt 0`$ for every $`\mathbf{x}`$, and it asserts that $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i(d)\mid\mathbf{X}_i=\mathbf{x}]=\mathrm{E}[Y_i\mid D_i=d,\mathbf{X}_i=\mathbf{x}],\qquad \mathrm{E}[Y_i(d)]=\sum_{\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{E}[Y_i\mid D_i=d,\mathbf{X}_i=\mathbf{x}]\Pr[\mathbf{X}_i=\mathbf{x}] .`$ The formula for $`\mathrm{E}[\tau_i(d,d')]`$ follows by applying it to $`d`$ and to $`d'`$. The other statement treats continuous doses under the following hypotheses. Write $`Y_i(t)=\mathrm{eval}(t,C_i)`$, where $`C_i`$ is a measurable curve state, evaluation is jointly measurable, the state-to-curve map is injective, and $`C_i`$ and $`D_i`$ are conditionally independent given $`\mathbf X_i`$. Fixed-dose and observed outcomes are integrable. In every positive-probability covariate cell, the potential conditional-mean curve is continuous at the two selected doses and every neighborhood of either dose has positive conditional probability. The statement asserts that this curve is an observed-outcome CEF, gives the standardized causal contrast at the two doses, and gives agreement there for every other observed CEF version continuous at both doses. Existence of such a continuous-at-the-selected-doses version is a conclusion. Independence only for the cylinder sigma algebra of the bare uncountable process does not suffice for this statement. Continuity here is on the real dose line. The corrected passage permits continuity relative to a Borel dose set $`S`$; that more general formulation requires a separate restriction or extension argument and is not proved here.

Three Lean statements formalize the calculation of P001-C07-E003 on the six units of the example. The first concerns the regression of $`Y_i`$ on $`(1,D_i,X_{[1]i},X_{[2]i})`$ with the data table, in which $`X_{[1]3}=10`$. It asserts that $`\mathbb{X}^T\mathbb{X}`$ is invertible and that the OLS coefficients of Theorem 4.1.4 are $`(\hat\beta_0,\hat\beta_1,\hat\beta_2,\hat\beta_3)=\left(\frac{13563}{2437},\frac{6178}{2437},-\frac{759}{2437},-\frac{1778}{2437}\right).`$ The second takes these four numbers and predicts unit 3 at $`X_{[1]3}=9`$, as both imputation tables print it. It asserts that the first table, which imputes only the missing potential outcomes, gives the estimate $`12103/4874`$, that $`\hat\beta_1=6178/2437`$, and that the two differ by $`-253/4874`$. The third statement combines the two steps. It asserts that the estimate from the first table as printed differs from the coefficient $`\hat\beta_1`$ computed by the OLS formula, and that the two are equal when unit 3 is predicted at $`X_{[1]3}=10`$. The second table imputes all potential outcomes, and its estimate is $`\hat\beta_1`$ whatever the covariates are, since each unit contributes $`\hat\beta_1\cdot1-\hat\beta_1\cdot0`$. This equality is not a separate clause of the statements. All quantities are exact rational numbers.

### P001-T93

Example 7.2.2 (Causal Inference with OLS Regression), with unit 3 corrected in the two imputation tables

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** p. 259 (PDF p. 277); the pooled regression and the identity $`\mathrm{E}[\tau_i\mid\mathbf{X}_i]=\beta_1`$: p. 258 (PDF p. 276)

Two Lean statements cover the example as corrected in P001-C07-E003. The first concerns the six units with $`X_{[1]3}=10`$ in every table and the coefficients $`(\hat\beta_0,\hat\beta_1,\hat\beta_2,\hat\beta_3)=(13563,\,6178,\,-759,\,-1778)/2437`$. It asserts that the estimate that imputes only the missing potential outcomes and the estimate that imputes all potential outcomes both equal $`\hat\beta_1=6178/2437`$. The coefficients enter this statement as four given numbers. That they are the OLS coefficients for the data table is the content of a separate statement, which belongs to the counterexample for the printed tables. The second statement is the general identity. Let $`n\ge1`$, let $`D_i\in\{0,1\}`$, let $`g_i`$ and $`\beta`$ be real numbers, and suppose that the residuals $`e_i=Y_i-g_i-\beta D_i`$ satisfy $`\sum_ie_i=0`$ and $`\sum_iD_ie_i=0`$. The statement asserts that $`\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n\bigl[D_i(Y_i-g_i)+(1-D_i)(g_i+\beta-Y_i)\bigr]=\beta,\qquad \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n\bigl[(g_i+\beta)-g_i\bigr]=\beta .`$ Here $`g_i`$ stands for $`\hat\beta_0+\mathbf{X}_i\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}`$ and $`\beta`$ for $`\hat\beta_1`$. The statement is broader than the example, since it assumes the two equations that Theorem 4.1.5 gives for OLS residuals instead of the OLS fit and does not require both treatment arms to be nonempty.

### P001-T94

Section 7.2.3, the maximum likelihood plug-in estimator of $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i(d)]`$, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

**Location:** p. 261 (PDF p. 279), last display on the page, and the population identity above it

Two Lean statements cover the estimator as corrected in P001-C07-E004 and the population identity on which it rests. The first is the corrected definition written out. For covariate vectors $`\mathbf{X}_1,\dots,\mathbf{X}_n`$, a function $`g`$ and a parameter value $`\boldsymbol\theta`$, the estimator is $`\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}y\,g(y,(d,\mathbf{X}_i),\boldsymbol\theta)\,dy ,`$ with the $`i`$th summand evaluated at $`\mathbf{X}_i`$. This statement asserts no statistical property of the estimator, and $`\boldsymbol\theta`$ is any parameter value, not necessarily $`\hat{\boldsymbol\theta}_{ML}`$. The second statement is the population identity for a binary treatment. Let $`D_i\in\{0,1\}`$ be strongly ignorable conditional on $`\mathbf{X}_i`$, with $`\epsilon\lt p_D(\mathbf{X}_i)\lt 1-\epsilon`$, and let $`(d,\mathbf{x})\mapsto\int y\,g(y,(d,\mathbf{x}),\boldsymbol\theta)\,dy`$ be a version of $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i\mid D_i=d,\mathbf{X}_i=\mathbf{x}]`$. The statement asserts that $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i(d)]=\mathrm{E}\left[\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}y\,g(y,(d,\mathbf{X}_i),\boldsymbol\theta)\,dy\right]\qquad\text{for } d=0 \text{ and } d=1 .`$ Its hypothesis on the model is weaker than the printed one. The conditional mean of the model must be correct, and $`g`$ need not equal the conditional density $`f_{Y_i\mid(D_i,\mathbf{X}_i)}`$. It covers the two values of a binary treatment, while the printed paragraph speaks of all average potential outcomes over the support of $`D_i`$.

### P001-T96

A counterexample to the sentence of Section 7.2.6 that, for a discrete and finite number of values of $`\mathbf{X}_i`$, the IPW estimator is logically equivalent to the plug-in estimator from Section 7.2.1, as printed

**Kind:** `counterexample`

Two Lean statements formalize the example of P001-C07-E005. There are 18 units in three covariate cells $`Z_i\in\{-1,0,1\}`$ of six units each. The numbers of treated units in the cells are $`3`$, $`1`$ and $`5`$, the sums of the treated outcomes are $`12`$, $`3`$ and $`20`$, and the sums of the control outcomes are $`9`$, $`10`$ and $`3`$. The first statement takes the propensity scores $`1/3`$, $`1/2`$ and $`2/3`$ as given. It asserts that the IPW estimator of Definition 7.2.6 equals $`59/36`$, that the plug-in estimator of Section 7.2.1 equals $`1`$, and that the two differ. The second statement connects these propensity scores with the maximum likelihood fit of the logit model $`p_{\alpha,\beta}(z)=e^{\alpha+\beta z}/(1+e^{\alpha+\beta z})`$. It asserts that the log likelihood of the sample has its unique maximum at $`(\alpha,\beta)=(0,\log2)`$, that both treatment arms are present in every cell, that the fitted propensity scores are $`1/3`$, $`1/2`$ and $`2/3`$, and that the two estimators then equal $`59/36`$ and $`1`$. The sample enters through the counts and the sums of outcomes in the cells and not as 18 rows. The log likelihood omits the binomial coefficients, which do not depend on $`(\alpha,\beta)`$. The statements do not include the further fact of the finding that this sample has positive probability under a population in which strong ignorability holds and the logit model is correctly specified.

### P001-T99

A counterexample to the sentence of Section 7.3.1 that the estimand $`\mathrm{E}[p_D(\mathbf{X}_i)\tau_i]`$ is equivalent to the ATT, $`\mathrm{E}[\tau_i\mid D_i=1]`$, as printed

**Kind:** `counterexample`

**Location:** p. 273 (PDF p. 291), Section 7.3.1, second paragraph

Three Lean statements formalize the example of P001-C07-E009. The probability space has two points, with $`D_i=0`$ at the point of probability $`3/4`$ and $`D_i=1`$ at the point of probability $`1/4`$. The covariate is the constant $`\mathbf{X}_i=0`$, and $`Y_i(0)=0`$ and $`Y_i(1)=1`$ at both points, so that $`\tau_i=1`$. The main statement asserts that $`(Y_i(0),Y_i(1))\mathbin{\perp\!\!\!\perp}D_i\mid\mathbf{X}_i`$, that $`p_D(0)=1/4`$ with $`1/8\lt 1/4\lt 1-1/8`$, that $`\mathrm{E}[\tau_i\mid D_i=1]=1`$, and that $`\mathrm{E}[p_D(\mathbf{X}_i)\tau_i]=\frac{1}{4}\ne\mathrm{E}[\tau_i\mid D_i=1] .`$ A second statement contains the arithmetic alone and adds that $`(1/4)/(1/4)=1`$, so that $`\mathrm{E}[p_D(\mathbf{X}_i)\tau_i]/\mathrm{E}[p_D(\mathbf{X}_i)]`$ equals the ATT. The third is the general fact used for the conditional independence, namely that on a finite probability space a constant is conditionally independent of any variable given any other. Probabilities and conditional means are exact weighted sums over the two points, and conditional independence is the factorization of the probabilities of all cells within each value of the covariate. The bound $`p_D(\mathbf{X}_i)\lt \epsilon\lt 1`$ that the paragraph prints for the ATT is not a clause of the statements. It holds in the example with $`\epsilon=3/8`$.

### P001-T100

Section 7.3.1, the estimand weighted by the propensity score that equals the ATT, as corrected

**Kind:** `corrected_statement`

The Lean statement is the identity of the sentence as corrected in P001-C07-E009. Let $`D_i\in\{0,1\}`$ almost surely with $`0\lt \Pr[D_i=1]\lt 1`$, let $`Y_i(0)`$ and $`Y_i(1)`$ be measurable with integrable difference $`\tau_i=Y_i(1)-Y_i(0)`$, let $`p_D`$ be a version of $`\Pr[D_i=1\mid\mathbf{X}_i]`$, and suppose that $`(Y_i(0),Y_i(1))\mathbin{\perp\!\!\!\perp}D_i\mid\mathbf{X}_i`$. The statement asserts that $`\mathrm{E}[p_D(\mathbf{X}_i)]=\Pr[D_i=1],\qquad \mathrm{E}[p_D(\mathbf{X}_i)\tau_i]=\mathrm{E}[D_i\tau_i],\qquad \mathrm{E}[\tau_i\mid D_i=1]=\frac{\mathrm{E}[p_D(\mathbf{X}_i)\tau_i]}{\mathrm{E}[p_D(\mathbf{X}_i)]} .`$ The ATT is represented as the integral of $`\tau_i`$ over the event $`D_i=1`$ divided by $`\Pr[D_i=1]`$. Conditional independence means that the conditional probabilities given $`\mathbf{X}_i`$ factor for every pair of events, one determined by the potential outcomes and one by $`D_i`$. The statement assumes no positivity bound on $`p_D`$. Integrability is required only of the effect, and both treatment values have positive probability, as in the correction. The remark that the printed expression equals the ATT only if $`\Pr[D_i=1]=1`$ or the ATT is zero has no Lean statement.

### P001-T102

Definition 4.3.7 (Partial Derivative at the Average) and the plug-in estimate that follows it, as printed

**Kind:** `result_touched_by_finding`

**Location:** p. 167 (PDF p. 185); footnote 13, p. 166 (PDF p. 184); the remark on p. 168 (PDF p. 186)

Three Lean statements concern the printed PDA claim and its sample formula. The first refutes the assertion that two CEF versions, each differentiable in a selected coordinate wherever the covariate PDF is positive, must have a common derivative at the covariate mean. It ranges over probability spaces, finite covariate dimensions and coordinates, measurable covariates with integrable coordinates, integrable responses, a covariate PDF, a joint covariate-response PDF and two CEF versions. It imposes no condition placing the mean in the support. The counterexample uses a one-dimensional covariate and response with a joint PDF and two globally $`C^1`$ versions of the same CEF whose derivatives at the covariate mean differ, as in P001-C04-E002. The second statement writes the printed plug-in formula as the derivative of the estimated CEF at the sample covariate mean. In this formula a nonexistent derivative is assigned $`0`$, and $`n=0`$ gives sample mean $`0`$; no consistency conclusion is asserted. The third refutes the same existence and agreement assertion assuming a covariate PDF, without requiring a joint covariate-response PDF. The two refutations prove counterexamples; the plug-in statement proves only the formula.

### P001-T103

Section 5.2.5 (Mixture Models), the mixture likelihood and the claims attached to it, with the mixture of Example 5.2.11, as printed

**Kind:** `result_touched_by_finding`

**Location:** pp. 198–200 (PDF pp. 216–218)

The passage makes two claims about a mixture model. The first is that the likelihood $`\prod_{i=1}^ng(Y_i,\mathbf{X}_i;\boldsymbol\theta)`$ can be maximized to find the ML estimate. The second is that this estimator is consistent and asymptotically efficient if a subset of the component models characterizes the conditional distribution of $`Y`$. The Lean statement negates the first claim for the mixture of Example 5.2.11, with parameter $`\boldsymbol\theta=(p_N,p_{LN},\mu_N,\sigma_N,\mu_{LN},\sigma_{LN},\lambda_E)`$. A parameter is admissible if $`p_N\ge0`$, $`p_{LN}\ge0`$, $`p_N+p_{LN}\le1`$, $`\sigma_N\gt 0`$, $`\sigma_{LN}\gt 0`$ and $`\lambda_E\gt 0`$. The statement asserts that some sample $`y_1,\dots,y_n`$ of positive numbers, with $`n\ge1`$, has no admissible $`\boldsymbol\theta`$ such that $`\prod_{i=1}^ng(y_i;\boldsymbol\theta')\le\prod_{i=1}^ng(y_i;\boldsymbol\theta)\qquad\text{for every admissible }\boldsymbol\theta' .`$ For the witness $`(1,2,3)`$, the admissible parameters $`(1/2,1/4,1,\sigma,0,1,1)`$ make the likelihood unbounded as $`\sigma\gt 0`$ decreases to zero. As in the printed example, the scale parameters have no positive lower bound and the parameter set is not compact, and this is the claim that P001-C05-E002 corrects. The log-normal and exponential densities are set to zero outside their supports. A counterexample in this one model, without covariates and with positive observations, suffices to refute the general existence assertion. The second claim, which is the subject of P001-C05-N005, is not part of it and is stated separately.

### P001-T104

Section 7.3, the claims that relate positivity and overlap around Definition 7.3.1 and Theorem 7.3.2, as printed

**Kind:** `result_touched_by_finding`

**Location:** p. 271 (PDF p. 289); p. 272 (PDF p. 290), with Example 7.3.3

The passage contains Theorem 7.3.2, that positivity implies complete population overlap, and two forms of the converse. The paragraph before Definition 7.3.1 says without qualification that a failure of positivity implies incomplete overlap. The sentence after the theorem says that the converse “can almost be stated” under conditions on support that it does not give. The Lean statement refutes the universal equivalence formed by joining the theorem and the unqualified converse, for a covariate $`X_i`$ with values $`k=0,1,2,\dots`$. Let $`t_k=\Pr[X_i=k,D_i=1]`$ and $`c_k=\Pr[X_i=k,D_i=0]`$, with $`\sum_kt_k\gt 0`$, $`\sum_kc_k\gt 0`$ and total mass one. The assertion it negates is that, for every such pair of mass sequences, $`\{k:t_k\gt 0\}=\{k:c_k\gt 0\}`$ if and only if there exists $`\epsilon\gt 0`$ such that $`\epsilon\lt \frac{t_k}{t_k+c_k}\lt 1-\epsilon\qquad\text{for every } k \text{ such that } t_k+c_k\gt 0 .`$ The two sets are $`\mathrm{Supp}[X_i\mid D_i=1]`$ and $`\mathrm{Supp}[X_i\mid D_i=0]`$, and the ratio is $`p_D(k)`$. The witness has $`t_k=(1/3)^{k+1}`$ and $`c_k=(1/2)^{k+2}`$, with total mass $`1/2`$ in each arm. Both supports are all natural numbers, but $`t_k/(t_k+c_k)`$ tends to zero, so no positive uniform bound exists. This countable example refutes the unqualified direction from overlap to positivity that P001-C07-E008 corrects. It leaves Theorem 7.3.2 intact and does not address a converse with additional conditions on support; the passage does not specify those conditions.

### P001-T105

Section 7.5, the paragraph that justifies ignorability in the application, as printed

**Kind:** `result_touched_by_finding`

**Location:** p. 278 (PDF p. 296)

The paragraph concerns an application, and the book does not define “all the information presented to the voters” formally. The Lean statement fixes one reading of it, the one used in P001-C07-E010. The vote share in referendum $`i`$ under $`d=0,1`$ is $`Y_i(d)=m(\mathbf{X}_i,d,U_i)`$, where $`\mathbf{X}_i`$ is the coded description, $`U_i`$ stands for the preferences of the electorate, and the two potential outcomes are measurable and lie in $`[0,100]`$. In this reading the voters respond only to the description, to $`d`$ and to their own preferences. Under this preference-based reading, the Lean statement negates the universal implication from this representation to conditional independence of $`(Y_i(0),Y_i(1))`$ and $`D_i`$ given $`\mathbf{X}_i`$. Its four-atom witness has constant $`\mathbf{X}_i`$, binary treatment with propensity $`1/2`$, and potential outcomes in $`[0,100]`$, yet the potential-outcome pair and treatment are conditionally dependent. No assumption relates $`U_i`$ to $`D_i`$, so the universal implication that the paragraph asserts “by definition” fails under this reading, as P001-C07-E010 describes. Conditional independence means that the conditional probabilities given $`\mathbf{X}_i`$ factor for every pair of events, one determined by $`(Y_i(0),Y_i(1))`$ and one by $`D_i`$. The universal assertion being negated is broader than the application in that $`D_i`$ may be any real random variable and is not required to take only the values $`0`$ and $`1`$; it also imposes no overlap assumption. The representation of the voters' information by $`m`$ and $`U_i`$ is an interpretation, and other readings of the paragraph are possible.

### P001-T106

Theorem 2.1.9 (Linearity of Expectations), Theorem 2.1.13 (Alternative Formula for Variance), Theorem 2.1.14 (Properties of Variance), Theorem 2.1.16 (Properties of Standard Deviation) and Theorem 2.1.18 (Chebyshev’s Inequality)

**Kind:** `headline_result`

**Location:** Theorem 2.1.9: p. 49 (PDF p. 67); Theorems 2.1.13 and 2.1.14: p. 52 (PDF p. 70); Theorem 2.1.16: p. 53 (PDF p. 71); Theorem 2.1.18: p. 55 (PDF p. 73)

Five Lean statements cover the five theorems. The first asserts that $`\mathrm{E}[aX+bY+c]=a\mathrm{E}[X]+b\mathrm{E}[Y]+c`$ for integrable $`X`$ and $`Y`$ and all real $`a`$, $`b`$ and $`c`$. The second asserts, for $`X`$ such that $`\mathrm{E}[X^2]\lt \infty`$, that $`\mathrm{V}[X]=\mathrm{E}[X^2]-\mathrm{E}[X]^2,\qquad \mathrm{V}[X+c]=\mathrm{V}[X],\qquad \mathrm{V}[aX]=a^2\mathrm{V}[X],`$ which are Theorems 2.1.13 and 2.1.14. The third asserts under the same hypothesis that $`\sigma[X+c]=\sigma[X]`$ and $`\sigma[aX]=|a|\sigma[X]`$. The fourth is Theorem 2.1.18: if $`\mathrm{E}[X^2]\lt \infty`$ and $`\mathrm{V}[X]\gt 0`$, then $`\Pr[|X-\mathrm{E}[X]|\ge\epsilon\sigma[X]]\le1/\epsilon^2`$ for all $`\epsilon\gt 0`$. The fifth is the same inequality in the form $`\Pr[|X-\mathrm{E}[X]|\ge\epsilon]\le\mathrm{V}[X]/\epsilon^2`$. The book does not print this form for a single random variable, and it also holds when $`\mathrm{V}[X]=0`$. A random variable is a real measurable function on a probability space, and expectations are integrals. The statements are therefore not restricted to discrete and continuous random variables, the two cases in which the book defines the expected value. The convention of footnote 2 on p. 45 that expectations exist and are finite appears as the hypothesis that $`X`$ and $`Y`$ are integrable or that $`\mathrm{E}[X^2]\lt \infty`$.

### P001-T107

Theorem 2.2.17 (Law of Iterated Expectations), Theorem 2.2.18 (Law of Total Variance) and Theorem 2.2.20 (The CEF is the Best Predictor)

**Kind:** `headline_result`

**Location:** Theorem 2.2.17: p. 72 (PDF p. 90); Theorem 2.2.18: p. 73 (PDF p. 91); Theorem 2.2.20: p. 75 (PDF p. 93), with its proof on pp. 75–76 (PDF pp. 93–94)

Two Lean statements cover the three theorems. In both, the CEF is a measurable function $`m`$, with $`Y`$ and $`m(X)`$ integrable, such that $`\int_{\{X\in A\}}Y\,dP=\int_{\{X\in A\}}m(X)\,dP`$ for every measurable set $`A`$ of values of $`X`$, so that $`m(x)`$ stands for $`\mathrm{E}[Y\mid X=x]`$. The first statement is Theorem 2.2.17: for integrable $`Y`$ and every such $`m`$, $`\mathrm{E}[Y]=\mathrm{E}[m(X)]`$. The second assumes that $`\mathrm{E}[Y^2]\lt \infty`$ and $`\mathrm{E}[m(X)^2]\lt \infty`$, and it takes a function $`v`$ that represents $`\mathrm{V}[Y\mid X]`$ in the same way, as the CEF of $`(Y-m(X))^2`$ given $`X`$. It asserts that $`\mathrm{V}[Y]=\mathrm{E}[v(X)]+\mathrm{V}[m(X)]`$, which is Theorem 2.2.18. It asserts further that, for every measurable function $`g`$ such that $`\mathrm{E}[g(X)^2]\lt \infty`$, $`\mathrm{E}[(Y-g(X))^2]=\mathrm{E}[v(X)]+\mathrm{E}[(m(X)-g(X))^2]\qquad\text{and}\qquad \mathrm{E}[(Y-m(X))^2]\le\mathrm{E}[(Y-g(X))^2] .`$ The inequality is Theorem 2.2.20, and the identity is the decomposition in its proof. The variable $`X`$ may take values in any measurable space, and no joint PMF or PDF is assumed, so that the statements cover more than the discrete and jointly continuous cases of the book’s proofs. The first statement does not require $`X`$ to be measurable, which the book’s definition of a random variable does require.

### P001-T108

Theorem 3.2.3 (The Expected Value of the Sample Mean Is the Population Mean), Theorem 3.2.4 (Sampling Variance of the Sample Mean) and Theorem 3.2.5 (Chebyshev’s Inequality for the Sample Mean)

**Kind:** `headline_result`

**Location:** Theorem 3.2.3: p. 97 (PDF p. 115); Theorems 3.2.4 and 3.2.5: p. 98 (PDF p. 116), with the proof of Theorem 3.2.5 continuing on p. 99 (PDF p. 117)

Four Lean statements concern the three theorems. In all of them $`X_1,X_2,\dots`$ is an infinite sequence of i.i.d. random variables, $`\overline{X}`$ is the mean of the first $`n`$ terms, and $`n\ge1`$ in all four statements. The first is Theorem 3.2.3 under the convention that $`\mathrm{E}[X]`$ is finite: $`\mathrm{E}[\overline{X}]=\mathrm{E}[X]`$. The second assumes that $`\mathrm{E}[X^2]\lt \infty`$ and asserts both $`\mathrm{E}[\overline{X}]=\mathrm{E}[X]`$ and $`\mathrm{V}[\overline{X}]=\frac{\mathrm{V}[X]}{n},`$ which is Theorem 3.2.4. The third is Theorem 3.2.5: if $`\mathrm{E}[X^2]\lt \infty`$, then $`\Pr\bigl[|\overline{X}-\mathrm{E}[X]|\ge\epsilon\bigr]\le\frac{\mathrm{V}[X]}{\epsilon^2n}\qquad\text{for every }\epsilon\gt 0 .`$ It omits the printed hypothesis $`\mathrm{V}[X]\gt 0`$, so that it also covers the case of zero variance. The fourth combines the three printed conclusions with separate moment conditions: a finite first moment gives $`\mathrm{E}[\overline{X}]=\mathrm{E}[X]`$; a finite second moment additionally gives the variance identity; and a finite second moment with $`\mathrm{V}[X]\gt 0`$ gives the displayed Chebyshev bound for every $`\epsilon\gt 0`$. The statements take the sample from an infinite i.i.d. sequence, where the theorems speak of $`n`$ i.i.d. random variables $`X_1,\dots,X_n`$. In them $`X`$ denotes the first term of the sequence, in accordance with the book’s convention that the unsubscripted $`X`$ has the common distribution.

### P001-T109

Theorem 4.3.3 (Polynomial Approximation of the CEF), with Theorem 4.3.2 (Weierstrass Approximation Theorem)

**Kind:** `headline_result`

**Location:** p. 158 (PDF p. 176); proof on pp. 158–159 (PDF pp. 176–177)

Two Lean statements are given, one for each theorem. The first is Theorem 4.3.2: for real $`a\le b`$, a function $`f`$ that is continuous on $`[a,b]`$ and $`\epsilon\gt 0`$, there exists a real polynomial $`p`$ such that $`|f(x)-p(x)|\lt \epsilon`$ for all $`x\in[a,b]`$. The second is Theorem 4.3.3. Let $`Y`$ have a finite second moment, let $`X`$ lie in $`[a,b]`$ almost surely, and let $`m`$ be a version of $`\mathrm{E}[Y\mid X=x]`$ that is continuous on $`[a,b]`$. The support must be the full interval in the book’s PMF/PDF sense: either the law of $`X`$ has a PDF whose positive set is exactly $`[a,b]`$, or $`X`$ has countable range and its positive-mass points are exactly $`[a,b]`$. The latter case permits only a singleton interval. The statement asserts that there exists a family $`p_J`$ of polynomials of degree at most $`J`$ minimizing $`\mathrm{E}[(Y-q(X))^2]`$ over all polynomials $`q`$ of degree at most $`J`$. These minimizers represent the BLP of $`Y`$ given $`X,X^2,\ldots,X^J`$. For every such minimizing family and every $`\epsilon\gt 0`$, there exists $`J`$ such that $`\mathrm{E}[(m(X)-p_{J'}(X))^2]\lt \epsilon`$ for all $`J'\ge J`$. The CEF is represented by the identity $`\mathrm{E}[Y\,\mathrm{I}(X\in A)]=\mathrm{E}[m(X)\,\mathrm{I}(X\in A)]`$ for all measurable sets $`A`$, which determines $`m`$ up to null sets. The support condition uses the positive set of a PDF or the positive-mass points, rather than topological support.

### P001-T110

Example 5.2.3 (Maximum Likelihood Estimation for a Biased Coin Flip): the ML estimate of $`p`$ is the sample mean

**Kind:** `headline_result`

**Location:** pp. 187–189 (PDF pp. 205–207); footnote 9, p. 189

Two Lean statements are given, and the first is the general claim of the example. For every $`n\ge1`$ and every sample $`(y_1,\ldots,y_n)\in\{0,1\}^n`$, set $`N_1=\sum_{i=1}^ny_i`$, $`N_0=n-N_1`$ and $`\overline{y}=N_1/n`$. The statement asserts that $`\overline{y}\in[0,1]`$ and that $`(1-t)^{N_0}\,t^{N_1}\le(1-\overline{y})^{N_0}\,\overline{y}^{N_1}\qquad\text{for all }t\in[0,1],`$ with equality if and only if $`t=\overline{y}`$. This says that the likelihood on $`\boldsymbol\Theta=[0,1]`$ has its unique maximum at the sample mean. The observations are represented as truth values, and $`N_1`$ counts those that are true. The convention $`0^0=1`$ covers the samples of all zeros and of all ones, which the book treats as separate cases. The claim concerns each sample separately, and no i.i.d. assumption enters. The second statement is the numerical case $`\mathbf{Y}=(1,0,1)`$ on p. 189: $`(1-t)t^2\le(1-2/3)(2/3)^2`$ for all $`t\in[0,1]`$, with equality if and only if $`t=2/3`$. Neither statement is narrower or broader than the printed example.
