### P001-T111

Theorem 6.1.6 (Expected Value under MCAR), Theorem 6.1.9 (Expected Value under MAR) and Theorem 6.1.10 (CEF under MAR), with the estimates of Examples 6.1.7 and 6.2.1

**Kind:** `headline_result`

**Location:** Definition 6.1.5 and Theorem 6.1.6, p. 213 (PDF p. 231); Example 6.1.7, p. 214 (PDF p. 232); Definition 6.1.8, p. 215 (PDF p. 233); Theorem 6.1.9, p. 216 (PDF p. 234); Theorem 6.1.10, p. 217 (PDF p. 235); Section 6.2.1 and Example 6.2.1, pp. 219–220 (PDF pp. 237–238)

Four Lean statements are given, and the first is Theorem 6.1.6. If $`Y_i`$ is integrable, $`R_i`$ takes values in $`\{0,1\}`$ almost surely, $`Y_i\perp\!\!\!\perp R_i`$ and both $`\Pr[R_i=0]`$ and $`\Pr[R_i=1]`$ are positive, it asserts that $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i]=\mathrm{E}[Y_i^*\,\mathrm{I}(R_i=1)]/\Pr[R_i=1]`$, which is $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i^*\mid R_i=1]`$ written as a ratio. The second and third also require $`R_i\in\{0,1\}`$ almost surely with both response-arm probabilities positive, and assume MAR: $`Y_i`$ is integrable, $`Y_i\perp\!\!\!\perp R_i\mid\mathbf{X}_i`$, and a version $`p_R`$ of the response propensity function satisfies $`p_R(\mathbf{X}_i)\gt \epsilon`$ almost surely for some $`\epsilon\gt 0`$. Let $`m`$ be a version of $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i^*\mid R_i=1,\mathbf{X}_i=\mathbf{x}]`$, defined as a CEF with respect to the measure restricted to the event $`R_i=1`$, with $`m(\mathbf{X}_i)`$ integrable. The third statement is Theorem 6.1.10 for finite-dimensional real covariate vectors: $`m(\mathbf{X}_i)`$ has mean $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i]`$, and $`m`$ is a version of $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i\mid\mathbf{X}_i=\mathbf{x}]`$. The second assumes that $`\mathbf{X}_i`$ takes countably many values and adds the identity of Theorem 6.1.9, $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i]=\sum_{\mathbf{x}}m(\mathbf{x})\Pr[\mathbf{X}_i=\mathbf{x}]`$, with a summable series. The fourth is the arithmetic of Examples 6.1.7 and 6.2.1: for the six units, the estimate under MCAR is $`3/4`$ and the estimate under MAR with the binary covariate is $`7/9`$. Conditional independence is represented by the almost sure factorization of $`\Pr[Y_i\in A,\,R_i\in B\mid\mathbf{X}_i]`$ for all measurable sets $`A`$ and $`B`$, and CEFs by integral identities that determine them up to null sets. The first three statements match the printed binary distributional support: $`R_i`$ is binary almost surely and each response arm has positive probability. Constant response indicators are excluded.

In case (ii), $`q(\mathbf{X}_i)=p_R(\mathbf{X}_i)`$ almost surely, and nothing is assumed about $`m`$ beyond measurability and integrability. Covariates range over finite-dimensional real vector spaces, and CEF correctness is expressed through versions up to null sets.

### P001-T114

Theorem 7.1.7 (Sharp Bounds for the ATE) and the width of the bounds, with the estimates of Example 7.1.6

**Kind:** `headline_result`

**Location:** Theorem 7.1.7, p. 241 (PDF p. 259), with its proof on pp. 241–242 (PDF pp. 259–260); the width, pp. 242–243 (PDF pp. 260–261); the sentence on sharpness, p. 244 (PDF p. 262); Example 7.1.6, p. 240 (PDF p. 258)

Three Lean statements are given. In the first, $`Y_i(0)`$ and $`Y_i(1)`$ take values in $`[a,b]`$ almost surely, $`D_i`$ takes values in $`\{0,1\}`$ almost surely, and both treatment arms have positive probability. The end points are the printed conditional-arm formulas, equivalently written as expectations of observable quantities, $`L=\mathrm{E}\bigl[Y_iD_i+a(1-D_i)-Y_i(1-D_i)-bD_i\bigr],\qquad U=\mathrm{E}\bigl[Y_iD_i+b(1-D_i)-Y_i(1-D_i)-aD_i\bigr].`$ These equal the printed end points, since $`\mathrm{E}[Y_iD_i]=\mathrm{E}[Y_i\mid D_i=1]\Pr[D_i=1]`$ when $`\Pr[D_i=1]\gt 0`$, and likewise for $`D_i=0`$. The statement asserts that $`\mathrm{E}[\tau_i]\in[L,U]`$ and that $`U-L=b-a`$. It also asserts that the bounds are sharp: for every $`c\in[L,U]`$ there exist measurable potential outcomes on the same probability space, with values in $`[a,b]`$ almost surely and the same $`D_i`$, whose observable pair has the same joint distribution as $`(Y_i,D_i)`$ and whose average treatment effect is $`c`$. The second statement is the algebra behind the width: for all real $`y,d,a,b`$, the two expressions inside the expectations differ by $`b-a`$. The third is the arithmetic of Example 7.1.6: with $`a=0`$ and $`b=1`$, the averages of the two expressions over the six units are $`-2/6`$ and $`4/6`$. The treatment-arm and outcome restrictions are interpreted almost surely, allowing null-set changes and matching the printed support conditions. The sharpness clause requires equality of observable laws.

### P001-T115

Theorem 7.1.10 (ATE under Random Assignment) and the selection-bias decomposition

**Kind:** `headline_result`

**Location:** Definition 7.1.9, p. 244 (PDF p. 262); Theorem 7.1.10, p. 245 (PDF p. 263); the decomposition, p. 247 (PDF p. 265)

The Lean statement has two parts. Let $`Y_i(0)`$ and $`Y_i(1)`$ be integrable, and let $`D_i`$ take values in $`\{0,1\}`$ with $`\Pr[D_i=1]\gt 0`$ and $`\Pr[D_i=0]\gt 0`$. For a random variable $`Z`$, the conditional mean $`\mathrm{E}[Z\mid D_i=d]`$ is written as the ratio $`\mathrm{E}[Z\,\mathrm{I}(D_i=d)]/\Pr[D_i=d]`$. The first part is Theorem 7.1.10: if $`(Y_i(0),Y_i(1))\perp\!\!\!\perp D_i`$, then $`\mathrm{E}[\tau_i]=\mathrm{E}[Y_i\mid D_i=1]-\mathrm{E}[Y_i\mid D_i=0]`$. The second part is the decomposition on p. 247, $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i\mid D_i=1]-\mathrm{E}[Y_i\mid D_i=0]=\mathrm{E}[\tau_i\mid D_i=1]+\mathrm{E}[Y_i(0)\mid D_i=1]-\mathrm{E}[Y_i(0)\mid D_i=0],`$ which is asserted without the independence hypothesis, as in the book. The two conditions on $`\Pr[D_i=d]`$ together are the positivity condition $`0\lt \Pr[D_i=1]\lt 1`$ of Definition 7.1.9. The potential outcomes are assumed integrable, in accordance with the convention of the book that expectations are finite. The statement does not include the difference-in-means estimator of p. 245. In no other respect is it narrower or broader than the printed statements.

### P001-T116

Theorem 7.1.13 (ATE under Strong Ignorability), Theorem 7.1.14 (Conditional ATE under Strong Ignorability) and Theorem 7.1.16 (Strong Ignorability and the Propensity Score)

**Kind:** `headline_result`

**Location:** Definition 7.1.12, p. 247 (PDF p. 265); Theorem 7.1.13, p. 248 (PDF p. 266); Theorem 7.1.14, p. 249 (PDF p. 267); Definition 7.1.15, p. 250 (PDF p. 268); Theorem 7.1.16, p. 251 (PDF p. 269), with its proof on pp. 251–252

The main Lean statement combines identification and propensity-score conclusions, with separate hypotheses for each; a second statement gives the discrete ATE identity. For identification, the potential outcomes $`Y_i(0)`$ and $`Y_i(1)`$ are integrable, $`D_i`$ takes values in $`\{0,1\}`$, $`(Y_i(0),Y_i(1))\perp\!\!\!\perp D_i\mid\mathbf{X}_i`$, and a version $`p_D`$ of the treatment propensity function satisfies $`\epsilon\lt p_D(\mathbf{X}_i)\lt 1-\epsilon`$ for some $`\epsilon\gt 0`$. For $`d\in\{0,1\}`$, let $`m_d`$ be a version of $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i\mid D_i=d,\mathbf{X}_i=\mathbf{x}]`$, defined as a CEF with respect to the probability measure restricted to the event $`D_i=d`$, with $`m_d(\mathbf{X}_i)`$ integrable. The identification statement allows covariates in any measurable space. It asserts that $`m_1-m_0`$ is a version of $`\mathrm{E}[\tau_i\mid\mathbf{X}_i=\mathbf{x}]`$, which is Theorem 7.1.14, and that $`\mathrm{E}[\tau_i]=\mathrm{E}[m_1(\mathbf{X}_i)-m_0(\mathbf{X}_i)]`$. The propensity-score statement assumes the same measurability, binary treatment, conditional independence and propensity bounds, with no outcome-moment or outcome-regression hypotheses. It asserts that $`D_i\perp\!\!\!\perp\mathbf{X}_i\mid p_D(\mathbf{X}_i)`$, that $`(Y_i(0),Y_i(1))\perp\!\!\!\perp D_i\mid p_D(\mathbf{X}_i)`$, and that $`\mathrm{E}[D_i\mid p_D(\mathbf{X}_i)]=p_D(\mathbf{X}_i)`$ almost surely. With the bounds on $`p_D(\mathbf{X}_i)`$, the last identity implies positivity conditional on the propensity score, and these are the three conclusions of Theorem 7.1.16. The further statement is Theorem 7.1.13 as printed, for $`\mathbf{X}_i`$ with countably many values: $`\mathrm{E}[\tau_i]=\sum_{\mathbf{x}}m_1(\mathbf{x})\Pr[\mathbf{X}_i=\mathbf{x}]-\sum_{\mathbf{x}}m_0(\mathbf{x})\Pr[\mathbf{X}_i=\mathbf{x}].`$ The sums run over all values, including those of probability zero, which contribute nothing. Conditional expectations and conditional independence are represented by integral identities that hold up to null sets, where the book uses PMFs and PDFs. Apart from this representation, the identification and discrete statements cover the printed ATE claims under the finite-expectation convention, and the propensity-score statement covers Theorem 7.1.16 without outcome-moment hypotheses.

### P001-T117

Theorem 7.2.5 (Reweighting under Strong Ignorability) and Theorem 7.2.8 (Double Robustness Theorem for Causal Inference)

**Kind:** `headline_result`

**Location:** Theorem 7.2.5, p. 265 (PDF p. 283), with its proof on pp. 265–266; Theorem 7.2.8, pp. 267–268 (PDF pp. 285–286), with its proof on pp. 268–269

One Lean statement covers both theorems. Strong ignorability is assumed in the following form: $`Y_i(0)`$ and $`Y_i(1)`$ are integrable, $`(Y_i(0),Y_i(1))\perp\!\!\!\perp D_i\mid\mathbf{X}_i`$, and a version $`p_D`$ of the treatment propensity function satisfies $`\epsilon\lt p_D(\mathbf{X}_i)\lt 1-\epsilon`$ almost surely for some $`\epsilon\gt 0`$. Treatment is binary almost surely, both treatment arms have positive probability, and covariates are finite-dimensional real vectors. For the double-robustness identity, the functions $`m_0`$, $`m_1`$ and $`q`$ stand for $`\tilde{\mathrm{E}}[Y_i\mid D_i=0,\mathbf{X}_i=\mathbf{x}]`$, $`\tilde{\mathrm{E}}[Y_i\mid D_i=1,\mathbf{X}_i=\mathbf{x}]`$ and $`\tilde p_D`$. They are measurable, and $`m_0(\mathbf{X}_i)`$ and $`m_1(\mathbf{X}_i)`$ are integrable. Either (i) $`m_0`$ and $`m_1`$ are versions of the CEFs of $`Y_i`$ given $`\mathbf{X}_i`$ in the two treatment arms and $`\epsilon'\lt q(\mathbf{X}_i)\lt 1-\epsilon'`$ almost surely for some $`\epsilon'\gt 0`$, or (ii) $`q(\mathbf{X}_i)=p_D(\mathbf{X}_i)`$ almost surely. The first conjunct asserts the identity of Theorem 7.2.5 under strong ignorability, independently of these nuisance functions and correctness alternatives, $`\mathrm{E}[\tau_i]=\mathrm{E}\biggl[\frac{Y_iD_i}{p_D(\mathbf{X}_i)}-\frac{Y_i(1-D_i)}{1-p_D(\mathbf{X}_i)}\biggr],`$ and the second conjunct asserts the identity of Theorem 7.2.8 under (i) or (ii), with $`m_0`$, $`m_1`$ and $`q`$ in place of the approximations. For Theorem 7.2.8, CEF correctness is expressed through versions, and propensity equality and bounds hold almost surely.

### P001-T118

Theorem 3.3.6 (Asymptotic Normality of Plug-In Estimators), with footnote 31 and the paragraph after it, as printed

**Kind:** `result_touched_by_finding`

**Location:** p. 120 (PDF p. 138)

Two Lean statements negate the two universal claims extracted from the passage as printed, without the corrections of P001-C03-E001 and P001-C03-E002. The first negates the following reading of Theorem 3.3.6. For i.i.d. random variables $`X_1,X_2,\ldots`$ with CDF $`F`$ and a functional $`T`$ that is Hadamard differentiable at $`F`$ with respect to the Kolmogorov distance, the claim is that there exists $`\phi\gt 0`$ such that $`\sqrt{n}\,(T(\hat F)-T(F))\xrightarrow{d}N(0,\phi^2)`$. The constant functional $`T(G)=7`$ has zero derivative and identically zero centered root-$`n`$ statistics, including for i.i.d. standard Gaussian observations, contradicting a strictly positive-variance limit. The second negates the sentence after the theorem: for every distribution with finite variance, the functional $`T_{\mathrm{E}}(G)=\int x\,dG(x)`$ is Hadamard differentiable at the CDF of that distribution. The book gives no formal definition of Hadamard differentiability, and the Lean statements fix one. A functional $`T`$ is Hadamard differentiable at $`F`$ if there exists a linear functional $`A`$, bounded with respect to the supremum norm, such that $`(T(F+t_nh_n)-T(F))/t_n\to A(h)`$ whenever $`t_n\gt 0`$, $`t_n\to0`$, $`h_n\to h`$ uniformly and every $`F+t_nh_n`$ is a CDF. In the second refuted claim, the perturbed CDFs are restricted to distributions with finite second moment, which is the reading most favorable to the printed sentence. In the first refuted claim, $`T`$ is defined on all real functions, and $`T(\hat F)`$ is not required to be measurable. In this formal respect, that claim is broader than the theorem, but the constant counterexample has measurable statistics. For the second claim, the base law is $`(\delta_0+\delta_2)/2`$; finite-second-moment perturbations have directions tending uniformly to zero while their mean difference quotients equal one. The conclusions concern the stated Hadamard definitions and leave a delta method with an appropriate nondegeneracy condition unaffected.

### P001-T119

Definition 4.3.9 (Polynomial Least Squares Sieve Estimator) and the claim of consistency attached to it, as printed

**Kind:** `result_touched_by_finding`

**Location:** pp. 168–169 (PDF pp. 186–187)

The Lean statement negates the universal consistency assertion on p. 169. The only conditions on the degree are $`J_n\to\infty`$ and $`J_n/n\to0`$, and the stronger condition of P001-C04-E001 is not added. In that assertion, the observations $`(Y_i,X_i)`$ are i.i.d., $`Y`$ is integrable, $`X`$ has a PDF that is positive exactly on an interval $`[a,b]`$ with $`a\lt b`$, and the CEF has a version $`m`$ that is real analytic on $`\mathbb{R}`$. For each $`n`$ and each sample, $`\hat m_n`$ is a polynomial of degree at most $`J_n`$ that minimizes $`\sum_{i=1}^n(Y_i-p(X_i))^2`$ over all polynomials $`p`$ of degree at most $`J_n`$. The square, which the printed display omits, is restored as in P001-C04-E003. The refuted assertion would imply that $`\int\bigl(\hat m_n(x)-m(x)\bigr)^2\,dF_X(x)\xrightarrow{p}0,`$ where $`F_X`$ is the CDF of $`X`$. The book does not define “smooth”, “appropriate support” or “consistent across regions of the data with nonzero probability mass”, and the refuted assertion fixes one reading of each. This assertion is narrower than the printed claim, since it excludes discrete $`X`$ and CEFs that are less smooth. It states consistency as convergence of the integrated squared error, which is not the same assertion as consistency at a point or over a region. The minimizer $`\hat m_n`$ is assumed to exist for every sample, and it is not required to be measurable. Independent uniform covariates on $`[0,1]`$ and standard Gaussian outcomes, with $`m=0`$ and $`J_n=\lfloor n^{3/4}\rfloor`$, contradict the integrated squared-error conclusion. This conclusion concerns the integrated squared-error reading, rather than every possible pointwise reading of the regional-consistency prose.

### P001-T120

Theorem 5.2.8 (The ML Estimator Is Consistent for the Minimum KL Divergence Predictor), as printed

**Kind:** `result_touched_by_finding`

**Location:** p. 195 (PDF p. 213)

The Lean statement negates the universal reading of the theorem with the KL divergence taken at one fixed value $`\mathbf{x}`$ of the explanatory variables. The divergence averaged over the distribution of $`\mathbf{X}`$, which is the correction of P001-C05-E001, does not appear. In the refuted assertion, the observations $`(Y_i,\mathbf{X}_i)`$ are i.i.d. The true conditional density $`f_{Y|\mathbf{X}}(y\mid\mathbf{x})`$ and the model $`g(y,\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t})`$, $`\mathbf{t}\in\boldsymbol\Theta`$, are measurable nonnegative functions that integrate to one in $`y`$ for every $`\mathbf{x}`$. The integral is taken with respect to a reference measure on the outcomes, which is Lebesgue measure for PDFs and a counting measure for PMFs. Fix $`\mathbf{x}`$ with positive covariate singleton mass $`\nu_X(\{\mathbf{x}\})\gt 0`$ and $`\tilde{\boldsymbol\theta}\in\boldsymbol\Theta`$, and suppose that $`\tilde{\boldsymbol\theta}`$ is the unique minimizer over $`\boldsymbol\Theta`$ of $`\mathrm{KL}\bigl[f_{Y|\mathbf{X}}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{x})\,\|\,g(\cdot,\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t})\bigr]`$. The refuted assertion would imply that $`\hat{\boldsymbol\theta}_{ML}\xrightarrow{p}\tilde{\boldsymbol\theta}`$ for every estimator with values in $`\boldsymbol\Theta`$ that maximizes $`\prod_{i=1}^ng(Y_i,\mathbf{X}_i;\mathbf{t})`$ over $`\boldsymbol\Theta`$ for every $`n`$ and every sample. No compactness or continuity assumption is imposed. The refuted assertion also adds conditions absent from the printed theorem. It assumes that, at the fixed $`\mathbf{x}`$, every $`g(\cdot,\mathbf{x};\mathbf{t})`$ is positive almost everywhere on the set where the true density is positive, and that every KL integrand is integrable. These conditions control the logarithm on the true-positive set and make each divergence finite. A counterexample has $`X`$ fair on $`\{0,1\}`$, with $`Y\mid X=0`$ Bernoulli$`(1/4)`$ and $`Y\mid X=1`$ Bernoulli$`(3/4)`$. A fitted Bernoulli$`(t)`$ model that ignores $`X`$, with $`t\in[1/10,9/10]`$, has fixed-$`x`$ KL minimizer $`1/4`$ at $`x=0`$, while its clipped sample-proportion MLE tends to $`1/2`$. The conclusion concerns this fixed-$`x`$ assertion, leaving the covariate-averaged KL and common-minimizer formulations unaffected.

### P001-T121

Section 5.2.5, the sentence on consistency and asymptotic efficiency of the ML estimator of a mixture (“If any subset of the models …”), as printed

**Kind:** `result_touched_by_finding`

**Location:** p. 199 (PDF p. 217), second paragraph

Two Lean statements negate universal assertions based on the sentence as printed, without the identification conditions of P001-C05-N005. The first is a special case, and it covers consistency only. The outcome takes the values $`0,1,2,3`$ with probabilities $`(p(1-a),\,pa,\,(1-p)(1-b),\,(1-p)b)`$, a mixture of two components with parameter $`\boldsymbol\theta=(p,a,b)\in[0,1]\times[0,1]\times[1/4,3/4]`$. If the observations are i.i.d. from the mixture at $`(1,1/3,b_0)`$, where the first component alone characterizes the distribution, the first refuted assertion would require every finite-sample likelihood-maximizing statistic to converge in probability to $`(1,1/3,b_0)`$ in all three coordinates. At $`p=1`$ the distribution does not depend on $`b`$, so a single maximizing statistic cannot consistently recover every true $`b_0`$. The second refuted assertion concerns a general mixture of $`M`$ conditional densities whose weights at the true parameter $`\boldsymbol\theta`$ vanish outside a nonempty proper subset of the components. That assertion would imply that every ML estimator satisfies $`\hat{\boldsymbol\theta}_{ML}\xrightarrow{p}\boldsymbol\theta`$ and is asymptotically efficient. Here efficiency means that $`\sqrt{n}\,(\hat{\boldsymbol\theta}_{ML}-\boldsymbol\theta)`$ has a normal limit whose covariance matrix is no larger, in the order of quadratic forms, than that of the influence function of any regular asymptotically linear estimator. The second assertion differs from the printed sentence in four ways. Its weights and components are arbitrary functions of one common parameter vector, where the vector of the book consists of the weights $`p_1,\ldots,p_{M-1}`$ and a separate $`\boldsymbol\theta_m`$ for each component. It requires the subset to be proper, where the book says “any subset”, and its estimators need not be functions of the sample. Its definition of efficiency, which the book does not give, compares the ML estimator only with regular estimators, and it does not require that the ML estimator be regular or that a regular estimator exist. The second negation follows from a failure of consistency in a model with separate weight and component parameters and a maximizing statistic on the observed samples. It does not separately establish failure of every possible efficiency formulation.

### P001-T124

Section 7.2.8 (Placebo Testing), the $`F`$-test passage, as printed

**Kind:** `result_touched_by_finding`

**Location:** p. 270 (PDF p. 288)

The book does not state the reference distribution or the size of the $`F`$-test. The Lean statement negates the assertion that the conventional $`F`$ test has asymptotic size at most its nominal level. The linearity condition of P001-C07-E007 is not added. In that assertion, the observations $`(\mathbf{X}_i,\mathbf{W}_i,D_i)`$ are i.i.d., with $`\mathbf{X}_i\in\mathbb{R}^K`$, $`\mathbf{W}_i\in\mathbb{R}^L`$, $`L\ge1`$ and $`D_i\in\{0,1\}`$, and the null hypothesis $`\mathbf{W}_i\perp\!\!\!\perp D_i\mid\mathbf{X}_i`$ is assumed. Let $`F_n`$ be the $`F`$-statistic that compares the OLS regression of $`D_i`$ on $`(1,\mathbf{X}_i)`$ with the OLS regression of $`D_i`$ on $`(1,\mathbf{X}_i,\mathbf{W}_i)`$, computed from the two residual sums of squares. Let $`c_n`$ be the $`(1-\alpha)`$ quantile of the $`F`$ distribution with $`L`$ and $`n-K-L-1`$ degrees of freedom. The refuted assertion would imply that $`\limsup_{n\to\infty}\Pr[F_n\gt c_n]\le\alpha`$ for every $`\alpha\in(0,1)`$. That assertion is narrower than the passage in one respect. It assumes finite second moments of the coordinates of $`\mathbf{X}_i`$ and $`\mathbf{W}_i`$ and an invertible matrix of second moments of $`(1,\mathbf{X}_i,\mathbf{W}_i)`$, which the passage does not state. It assumes no linear model for $`\mathrm{E}[D_i\mid\mathbf{X}_i]`$ and no homoskedasticity. In a counterexample, $`X`$ is uniform on $`\{-1,0,1\}`$, $`W=X^2+Z/2`$ with a conditionally independent fair sign $`Z`$, and $`D`$ is conditionally Bernoulli with probability $`3/4`$ at $`|X|=1`$ and $`1/4`$ at $`X=0`$. Conditional independence, the moment conditions and the rank condition hold, but rejection probability at the conventional $`F`$-reference median tends to one.

### P001-T125

Theorem 3.2.8 (Weak Law of Large Numbers)

**Kind:** `headline_result`

**Location:** p. 100 (PDF p. 118); Definition 3.2.6, p. 99 (PDF p. 117)

The Lean statement asserts that $`\overline{X}_{(n)}\xrightarrow{p}\mathrm{E}[X]`$ for an infinite sequence $`X_1,X_2,\ldots`$ of i.i.d. random variables with a finite second moment. Convergence in probability is that of Definition 3.2.6: for every $`\epsilon\gt 0`$, $`\Pr[|\overline{X}_{(n)}-\mathrm{E}[X]|\ge\epsilon]\to0`$ as $`n\to\infty`$. The variables of the whole sequence are mutually independent, and each has the distribution of $`X_1`$. The printed theorem assumes $`\mathrm{V}[X]\gt 0`$, footnote 10 notes that the case $`\mathrm{V}[X]=0`$ is trivial, and the Lean statement covers both cases. A finite second moment is equivalent to a finite mean and a finite variance. In no other respect is the Lean statement narrower or broader than the printed theorem.

### P001-T126

Theorem 3.2.24 (Central Limit Theorem)

**Kind:** `headline_result`

**Location:** p. 109 (PDF p. 127); Definition 3.2.23, p. 109; Definition 3.2.22, p. 108 (PDF p. 126)

Three Lean statements are given, for an infinite sequence of i.i.d. random variables with a finite second moment, $`\mathrm{E}[X]=\mu`$ and $`\mathrm{V}[X]=\sigma^2\gt 0`$. The first asserts that $`\sqrt{n}\,(\overline{X}-\mu)\xrightarrow{d}N(0,\sigma^2)`$. The second asserts that $`Z\xrightarrow{d}N(0,1)`$, where $`Z=(\overline{X}-\mathrm{E}[\overline{X}])/\sigma[\overline{X}]`$ is the standardized sample mean in the first form of Definition 3.2.23. The third is the finite algebra that connects the two forms: for every $`n\ge1`$, $`\sigma[\overline{X}]=\sigma/\sqrt{n}`$, $`Z=\sqrt{n}\,(\overline{X}-\mu)/\sigma`$, $`\mathrm{E}[Z]=0`$ and $`\mathrm{V}[Z]=\sigma[Z]=1`$. Convergence in distribution is that of Definition 3.2.22: the CDFs converge at every point at which the CDF of the limit is continuous. The three statements are neither narrower nor broader than the printed theorem.

### P001-T127

Theorem 3.3.8 (Consistency of the Kernel Density Estimator, Rectangular Kernel)

**Kind:** `headline_result`

**Location:** p. 122 (PDF p. 140); proof sketch on p. 123 (PDF p. 141); the continuity condition is stated on p. 121 (PDF p. 139)

Two Lean statements are given. In both, $`X_1,X_2,\ldots`$ are i.i.d. with PDF $`f`$ and CDF $`F`$, and $`\hat f_K(x)`$ is the kernel density estimator of Definition 3.3.7 with $`K(u)=\mathrm{I}(-1\le u\le1)/2`$. The first is the theorem. If $`f`$ is continuous on $`\mathbb{R}`$ and the bandwidths $`h=h_n\gt 0`$ form a deterministic sequence with $`h_n\to0`$ and $`nh_n\to\infty`$, it asserts that $`\hat f_K(x)\xrightarrow{p}f(x)`$ for every $`x\in\mathbb{R}`$. The continuity of $`f`$ is the condition stated on p. 121, which the box does not repeat (see P001-C03-A001). The second statement gives the exact moments behind the proof sketch on p. 123. For every $`n\ge1`$, $`h\gt 0`$ and $`x\in\mathbb{R}`$, with $`p_h=F(x+h)-F(x-h)`$, it asserts that $`\mathrm{E}[\hat f_K(x)]=\frac{p_h}{2h},\qquad\mathrm{V}[\hat f_K(x)]=\frac{p_h(1-p_h)}{4nh^2}.`$ No continuity of $`f`$ is assumed in the second statement. The first statement is neither narrower nor broader than the theorem read with the condition on p. 121.

### P001-T128

Theorem 3.4.2 (Normal Approximation–Based Confidence Interval)

**Kind:** `headline_result`

**Location:** p. 125 (PDF p. 143); proof on pp. 125–126 (PDF pp. 143–144); Definition 3.4.1, p. 124 (PDF p. 142)

Two Lean statements are given, and the first is the theorem. Let $`\hat\theta`$ and $`\hat{\mathrm{V}}[\hat\theta]\ge0`$ be sequences of random variables, and suppose that $`\sqrt{n}\,(\hat\theta-\theta)\xrightarrow{d}N(0,\phi^2)`$ with $`\phi\gt 0`$ and that $`n\hat{\mathrm{V}}[\hat\theta]\xrightarrow{p}\phi^2`$. The second condition is the consistency of Definition 3.2.32, with the hats restored as in P001-C03-N007. For $`\alpha\in(0,1)`$ and $`z_{1-\alpha/2}`$ such that $`\Phi(z_{1-\alpha/2})=1-\alpha/2`$, the statement asserts that $`\Pr[\theta\in CI_{1-\alpha}(\theta)]`$ converges as $`n\to\infty`$ and that its limit is at least $`1-\alpha`$, where $`CI_{1-\alpha}(\theta)`$ is the open interval of the theorem. This is asymptotic validity as defined on p. 125. The proof in the book gives a limit equal to $`1-\alpha`$, and the Lean statement does not include that equality. The second statement is the finite algebra of the proof. For $`n\ge1`$, $`\phi\gt 0`$, real $`\hat\theta`$ and $`\theta`$, a variance estimate $`v\gt 0`$ and any real $`z`$, it asserts that $`\frac{\hat\theta-\theta}{\sqrt{v}}=\frac{\sqrt{n}\,(\hat\theta-\theta)}{\phi}\cdot\frac{\phi}{\sqrt{nv}},`$ and that $`\theta`$ lies in the open interval $`(\hat\theta-z\sqrt{v},\,\hat\theta+z\sqrt{v})`$ if and only if $`-z\lt (\hat\theta-\theta)/\sqrt{v}\lt z`$.

### P001-T129

Theorem 5.2.6 (Consistency of the Maximum Likelihood Estimator) and the lemma in its proof sketch

**Kind:** `headline_result`

**Location:** Theorem 5.2.6, p. 193 (PDF p. 211); proof sketch, pp. 193–194 (PDF pp. 211–212)

Theorem 5.2.6 leaves its regularity conditions informal, and the six Lean statements given here name them. In four consistency statements, the parameter space $`\boldsymbol\Theta`$ is compact, and the expected log-likelihood $`L(\mathbf{t})=\mathrm{E}[\log g(Y,\mathbf{X};\mathbf{t})]`$ is finite, continuous on $`\boldsymbol\Theta`$ and uniquely maximized at $`\boldsymbol\theta`$. The convergence $`L_n(\mathbf{t})\xrightarrow{p}L(\mathbf{t})`$ of the proof sketch is assumed through an error envelope $`\Delta_n`$ with $`|L_n(\mathbf{t})-L(\mathbf{t})|\le\Delta_n`$ for every $`\mathbf{t}\in\boldsymbol\Theta`$ and every sample point, and $`\Delta_n\xrightarrow{p}0`$. These four statements assert that $`\hat{\boldsymbol\theta}_{ML}\xrightarrow{p}\boldsymbol\theta`$ under these conditions, for i.i.d. observations whose conditional density is $`g(y,\mathbf{x};\boldsymbol\theta)`$. One is for estimators that maximize $`L_n`$, and three are for estimators that maximize the product $`\prod_{i=1}^ng(Y_i,\mathbf{X}_i;\mathbf{t})`$. The conditional model is measurable, nonnegative and normalized, and the observed log likelihood is integrable for every parameter. They apply to every maximizer and do not assume a unique maximum in the sample. The average-log result and two product results assume $`g(Y,\mathbf{X};\mathbf{t})\gt 0`$ almost surely for each $`\mathbf{t}`$ separately; the other product result assumes this simultaneously for all $`\mathbf{t}\in\boldsymbol\Theta`$. One product result makes the estimator a measurable function of the first $`n`$ observed pairs, requires membership in $`\boldsymbol\Theta`$ and product maximization for every finite input sample, and requires measurable error envelopes. The other three consistency statements use measurable maximizing selections on the sampling space. Two statements give the lemma of the sketch: $`L(\mathbf{t})\le L(\boldsymbol\theta)`$ for all $`\mathbf{t}\in\boldsymbol\Theta`$, with equality if and only if $`\Pr[g(Y,\mathbf{X};\mathbf{t})=g(Y,\mathbf{X};\boldsymbol\theta)]=1`$. The two lemma statements have the same hypotheses: a measurable, nonnegative, normalized conditional model at the true parameter, candidate densities positive reference-almost-everywhere wherever the true density is positive, and integrable observed log likelihoods. They allow zeros outside the true-positive set and require neither compactness nor uniform convergence. Equality of density values almost surely does not imply parameter equality without identification.

### P001-T130

Theorem 6.1.4 (Estimating Sharp Bounds for the Expected Value)

**Kind:** `headline_result`

**Location:** pp. 211–212 (PDF pp. 229–230); proof on p. 212

The Lean statement includes latent outcomes $`Y_i`$ and response indicators $`R_i`$, with $`a\le b`$, $`Y_i\in[a,b]`$ and $`R_i\in\{0,1\}`$ almost surely, and positive probabilities for both response values. It defines $`Y_i^*=Y_iR_i+(-99)(1-R_i)`$ and assumes that the observable pairs $`(Y_i^*,R_i)`$ are i.i.d. Set $`Y_i^L=Y_i^*R_i+a(1-R_i)`$ and $`Y_i^U=Y_i^*R_i+b(1-R_i)`$, as in the proof on p. 212. The statement asserts that the sample means of these variables are unbiased for $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i^L]`$ and $`\mathrm{E}[Y_i^U]`$ for every $`n\ge1`$, and that they converge in probability to these expectations. It also identifies $`\mathrm{E}[Y_0^L]`$ and $`\mathrm{E}[Y_0^U]`$ with the endpoints of Theorem 6.1.3, $`\mathrm{E}[Y_0^*\mid R_0=1]\Pr[R_0=1]+a\Pr[R_0=0]`$ and the same expression with $`b`$ in place of $`a`$. Thus the sample means are unbiased and converge in probability to those endpoint formulas. The support restrictions allow null-set changes.
